Definition 0.4.1
.

A group
X
is said to be of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type
provided each composition factor of
X
is either a
p
-group, a
p^{\prime}
-group or is isomorphic to
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
.

We will require the following result due to Feit.

Theorem 0.4.2

(Feit)
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime,
\mathbb{K}
is a field of characteristic
p
,
L
is a finite group with a cyclic Sylow
p
-subgroup
P
and
V
is a faithful indecomposable
\mathbb{K}L
-module with
d=\dim V\leq p
. Assume that
L
is not of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type. Then
p
is odd,
|P|=p
,
V|_{P}
is indecomposable,
C_{L}(P)=P\times Z(L)
and
d\geq\frac{2}{3}(p-1)
.

Proof.

This is
[
23
, Theorem 1]
. ∎

Theorem
0.4.2
illuminates the importance of representations of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. Some of the results in this section hold for an arbitrary field
\mathbb{K}
but our applications will only be for
\mathbb{K}=\mathrm{GF}(p)
. We follow the standard construction of certain irreducible modules for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
. Let
\mathbb{K}[x,y]
be the polynomial ring in two commuting variables
x
and
y
and coefficients in
\mathbb{K}
. Then, for
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
and
a,b\geq 0
natural numbers, the extension linearly of

x^{a}y^{b}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(\alpha x+\beta y)^{a}(\gamma x+\delta y)^{b}

makes
\mathbb{K}[x,y]
into a
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-module. The subspaces of
\mathbb{K}[x,y]
consisting of homogenous polynomials of fixed degree at most
p-1
provide us with an explicit construction of the
basic
irreducible
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-modules by restriction.

Notation 0.4.3
.

For
0\leq e\leq p-1
,
\mathbf{V}_{e}
represents the
(e+1)
-dimensional
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-submodule of
\mathbb{K}[x,y]
consisting of degree
e
homogeneous polynomials. We use the same notation for
\mathbf{V}_{e}
when we consider
\mathbf{V}_{e}
as a module for certain subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
, for example, when considered as a
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module. We often call
\mathbf{V}_{1}
the natural
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module.

For
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
, every irreducible
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module is basic and can be realized over
\mathrm{GF}(p)
(see
[
1
, page 15]
for example). In particular, in this case there are
p
irreducible modules and they have dimensions
1,2,\dots,p
. The faithful modules are the ones of even-dimension and the odd-dimensional modules are representations of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
.

The next eight results provide the facts that we shall require about these representations. The first result is used silently in the text.

Lemma 0.4.4
.

Suppose that
L\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
,
T\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(L)
and
V
is an irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module. Then
V
is indecomposable as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)T
-module. In particular, for all
0\leq k\leq\dim V-1
,
\dim[V,T;k]/[V,T;k+1]=1
and, if
\dim[V,T]=1
, then
V\cong\mathbf{V}_{1}
has dimension
2
.

Proof.

This is calculated using the description of the modules above.∎

Obviously, if
p
is odd and
V
is a faithful
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module, then the centre of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
negates
V
and so a complement to a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
acts fixed-point-freely on any faithful module. The same is not true for the irreducible
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
-modules.

Lemma 0.4.5
.

Suppose that
L\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
,
T\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(L)
and
H
is a complement to
T
in
N_{L}(T)
. Assume that
V
is an irreducible
d
-dimensional
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-module. If
C_{V}(H)\neq 0
, then
d
is odd and either

(i)

d\leq p-2
,
\dim C_{V}(H)=1
and
[V,T;(d-1)/2]/[V,T;(d+1)/2]
is centralized by
H
; or

(ii)

d=p
,
\dim C_{V}(H)=3
and
V/[V,T]
,
[V,T;(p-1)/2]/[V,T;(p+1)/2]
and
C_{V}(T)
are centralized by
H
.

In particular, if
C_{V}(T)
is centralized by
H
, then either
V
is the trivial module or
\dim V=p
and
\dim C_{V}(H)=3
.

Proof.

Let
e=d-1
and remember that
V=\mathbf{V}_{e}
. Take
\tau=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
to be a generator of
T
and
\delta=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
where
\lambda\in\mathrm{GF}(p)
has order
p-1
to be a generator of
H
.
Then we calculate

[V,T;k]=\langle x^{j}y^{e-j}\mid 0\leq j\leq e-k\rangle.

We also calculate that
\delta
acts as the scalar
\lambda^{e-k}\lambda^{-k}=\lambda^{e-2k}
on the quotient

[V,T;k]/[V,T;k+1]=\langle x^{e-k}y^{k}+[V,T;k+1]\rangle.

Hence
\tau
centralizes
[V,T;k]/[V,T;k+1]
if and only if either
k=e/2
or
e=p-1
and
k=0
or
p-1
. In particular,
e=d-1
is even and this gives the result.
∎

Assume that
\mathbb{K}
has characteristic
p\geq 0
(allowing
p=0
for a moment)  and that
\mathbf{V}_{d}
is the
(d+1)
-dimensional
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-module of homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
.
Define

\displaystyle\Omega:\mathbb{K}[x,y]\otimes\mathbb{K}[x,y]

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}[x,y]\otimes\mathbb{K}[x,y]

\displaystyle(f\otimes g)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\otimes\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\otimes\frac{\partial g}{\partial x}.

Then
\Omega
is
\mathbb{K}
-linear. Let
A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
B_{\theta}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-\theta\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
with
\theta\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
be elements of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
. Then
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})=\langle A,B_{\theta}\mid\theta\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\rangle
. We calculate

\displaystyle(x^{a}y^{b}\otimes x^{c}y^{d})A\Omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((x+y)^{a}y^{b}\otimes(x+y)^{c}y^{d})\Omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(x+y)^{a-1}y^{b}\otimes\left(c(x+y)^{c-1}y^{d}+d(x+y)^{c}y^{d-1}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(a(x+y)^{a-1}y^{b}+b(x+y)^{a}y^{b-1}\right)\otimes c(x+y)^{c-1}y^{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ad(x+y)^{a-1}y^{b}\otimes(x+y)^{c}y^{d-1}

\displaystyle-bc(x+y)^{a}y^{b-1}\otimes(x+y)^{c-1}y^{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(adx^{a-1}y^{b}\otimes x^{c}y^{d-1}-bcx^{a}y^{b-1}\otimes x^{c-1}y^{d}\right)A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x^{a}y^{b}\otimes x^{c}y^{d})\Omega A.

Hence
\Omega A=A\Omega.

Similarly, we calculate that

B_{\theta}\Omega=\theta\Omega B_{\theta}
and so
C\Omega=(\det C)\Omega C
for all
C\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
. In particular,
\Omega
is a
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-module homomorphism. The multiplication map
\mu:\mathbb{K}[x,y]\otimes\mathbb{K}[x,y]\rightarrow\mathbb{K}[x,y]
defined by
(f\otimes g)\mu\mapsto fg
is also a
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-module homomorphism. It follows that, for
r\geq 0
, the
r
-transvectant

\Theta_{r}=\Omega^{r}\mu:\mathbb{K}[x,y]\otimes\mathbb{K}[x,y]\rightarrow\mathbb{K}[x,y]

is also a
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-module homomorphism.

Suppose that
d
and
e
are natural numbers and observe that by restriction

\Theta_{r}:\mathbf{V}_{d}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{e}\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{d+e-2r}.

Assume now that
\mathbb{K}
has characteristic
p>0
. Then, for
\ell\leq p-1
,
\mathbf{V}_{\ell}
is an irreducible
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-module. Therefore, if
d+e-2r\leq p-1
, the restriction of
\Theta_{r}
is either the zero map or is a surjection. In particular, if
r\leq e\leq d
and
d+e\leq p-1
, then, as
(x^{d}\otimes y^{e})\Theta_{r}\neq 0
,
\Theta_{r}
restricts to a surjection. By counting dimensions, we conclude

\mathbf{V}_{d}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{e}\cong\mathbf{V}_{d+e}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{d+e-2}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbf{V}_{d-e}

which in characteristic
0
is known as the
Clebsch-Gordan decomposition
. Let
\iota
be the
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-module endomorphism of
\mathbb{K}[x,y]
which maps
f\otimes g
to
g\otimes f
. Then
\Theta_{r}\iota=(-1)^{r}\Theta_{r}
and, for a fixed natural number
d
,
\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{d})
is the submodule of
\mathbf{V}_{d}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{d}
negated by
\iota
and
S^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{d})
is the submodule centralized by
\iota
.
We have recreated the following

Proposition 0.4.6
.

Suppose that
d,e\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq e
and
d+e\leq p-1
. Then, as
\mathbb{K}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{K})
-modules,

\mathbf{V}_{d}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{e}\cong\mathbf{V}_{d+e}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{d+e-2}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbf{V}_{d-e}.

Furthermore, if
2d\leq p-1
,

S^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{d})\cong\mathbf{V}_{2d}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{2d-4}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbf{V}_{a}

and

\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{d})=\mathbf{V}_{2d-2}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{2d-6}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbf{V}_{b}

where
a=2d\pmod{4}
and
b=2d-2\pmod{4}
with
a,b\leq 2
.∎

Notice that, if we take
d+1=(p-1)/2
, then
\mathbf{V}_{d}
is involved in
\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{d})
if and only if
d\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.

Lemma 0.4.7
.

We have

\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3}=\mathbf{V}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{2}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{p-1}\oplus P(p-3)\oplus\dots\oplus P(4)

where, for
1\leq j\leq p-1
,
P(j)
is the projective cover of
\mathbf{V}_{j}
. In particular,
(\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3})/\mathrm{Rad}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
is the direct sum of all the irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
-modules.

Proof.

It suffices to work over an algebraically closed field.
From
[
17
, Lemma 3.1 (ii)]
, we have

\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3}=\mathbf{V}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{2}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{p-1}\oplus T(p+1)\oplus\dots\oplus T(2p-6)

where, for an integer
j
,
T(j)
is the
tilting module
associated to
j
. We know
\mathbf{V}_{p-1}
has dimension
p
and is a projective module (see
[
1
]
). Using
[
17
, Lemma 3.1 (iii)]

\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-1}=\mathbf{V}_{p-1}\oplus T(p+1)\oplus\dots\oplus T(2p-4).

Since
\mathbf{V}_{p-1}
is projective, so is
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-1}
and every direct summand of
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-1}
by
[
7
, Lemma 1.5.2]
. Hence the tilting modules
T(p+1),\dots,T(2p-6)
are projective
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-modules. Now, the discussion before
[
17
, Lemma 3.1]
reveals that for
p+1\leq j\leq 2p-2
,
T(j)
has a quotient
\mathbf{V}_{2(p-1)-j}
and dimension
2p
. We conclude that
T(j)
is the projective cover
P(2(p-1)-j)
of
\mathbf{V}_{2(p-1)-j}
. With this notation, we have

\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3}=\mathbf{V}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{2}\oplus\mathbf{V}_{p-1}\oplus P(p-3)\oplus\dots\oplus P(4).

In particular, we note that every irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
-module appears exactly once as a quotient of
(\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3})/\mathrm{Rad}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
. This proves the claim.
∎

We recall from
[
1
, pages 15 and 48]
that
P(0)
has dimension
p
and is a uniserial
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
-module with composition factors
\mathbf{V}_{0}
,
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
and
\mathbf{V}_{0}
. In particular, there is a unique indecomposable
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
-module with socle of dimension
1
and quotient
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
.

Lemma 0.4.8
.

Suppose that
L\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
and
W
is the unique indecomposable
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-module with socle of dimension
1
and quotient
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. Then
\Lambda^{2}(W)
has a submodule
U\leq\mathrm{Rad}(\Lambda^{2}(W))
with
U\cong\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
and
\Lambda^{2}(W)/U\cong\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
.

Proof.

Let
R
be the socle of
W
. Then
W\otimes W
has a submodule

U^{*}=\langle r\otimes w,w\otimes r\mid r\in R,w\in W\rangle.

Plainly
W/U^{*}\cong\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. Set
U=\langle w\otimes r-r\otimes w\mid r\in R,w\in W\rangle
. Then
U\cong W/R=\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
and
\Lambda^{2}(W)/U\cong\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
. Hence we only need to show that
U\leq\mathrm{Rad}(\Lambda^{2}(W))
. Since
\Lambda^{2}(W)
is a direct summand of
W\otimes W
, we have
\mathrm{Rad}(\Lambda^{2}(W))=\mathrm{Rad}(W\otimes W)\cap\Lambda^{2}(W)
. In particular, if
U\not\leq\mathrm{Rad}(\Lambda^{2}(W))
, then
U
is a direct summand of
W\otimes W
. So suppose that this is the case. Then
W\otimes W\cong\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\oplus(\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
. Let
T\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(L)
. Then using Lemma
0.4.7
, as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)T
-module
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\oplus(\mathbf{V}_{p-3}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
is a sum of indecomposable modules
\mathbf{V}_{0}
of dimension 1,
\mathbf{V}_{2}
of dimension
3
,
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
of dimension
p-2
and a number of free modules of dimension
p
. On the other hand, as
W
is indecomposable with socle
\mathbf{V}_{0}
and quotient
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
, using Lemma
0.4.4
and
[
7
, Corollary 3.6.10]
we have that
W
restricted to
T
is indecomposable of dimension
p-1
. It follows that, as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)T
-module,
W\otimes W
is a direct sum of a trivial module and a free module (for example use
[
17
, Lemma 3.1 (ii)]
to write down
\mathbf{V}_{p-2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{p-2}
and then restrict to
T
). Since the two structures are incompatible, we conclude that
U\leq\mathrm{Rad}(\Lambda^{2}(W))
.
∎

We can now establish the technical point that we require.

Lemma 0.4.9
.

Suppose that
L\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
,
T\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(L)
,
H\leq N_{L}(T)
is a complement to
T
and
W
is the unique indecomposable
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-module with socle of dimension
1
and quotient
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. Assume that
\theta:W\times W\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
is a surjective alternating
L
-invariant bilinear map. Let
u\in W\setminus[W,T]
and
w\in[W,T;p-3]\setminus C_{W}(T)
be such that
\langle w\rangle
and
\langle u\rangle
are
H
-invariant. Then
(u,w)\theta\neq 0
.

Proof.

Since
\theta:W\times W\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
is a surjective
L
-invariant bilinear map, there is a unique surjective
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-module homomorphism
\widetilde{\theta}
from
\Lambda^{2}(W)
to
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. Using Lemma
0.4.8
, we have that
\widetilde{\theta}
determines a surjective homomorphisms
\theta^{*}
from
\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
to
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. By Lemma
0.4.7
,
\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
either has no quotient isomorphic to
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
, which is against our assumption that
\theta
is surjective, or
\ker\theta^{*}
is the unique maximal submodule of
\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
which has quotient
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. Now the
(p-3)/2
-transvectant

\Theta_{(p-3)/2}
restricted to
\Lambda^{2}(\mathbf{V}_{p-3})
also has image in
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. Hence
\ker\theta^{*}=\ker\Theta_{(p-3)/2}
.

We may take
u=x^{p-3}
and
w=y^{p-3}
in
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
. We calculate
(u\otimes w)\Theta_{(p-3)/2}=\left({(p-3)}\atop{(p-3)/2}\right)^{2}(xy)^{(p-3)/2}\neq 0
.
Therefore
(u,w)\not\in\ker\Theta_{(p-3)/2}=\ker\theta^{*}
. This shows that
(u,w)\theta\neq 0
.
∎

In the next lemma we are interested in modules for
L=\mathrm{Sym}(p)
defined in characteristic
p
. The notation
S^{\lambda}
denotes the
Specht module
for
L
corresponding to the partition
\lambda
of
p
. The module
D^{\lambda}
is the unique irreducible quotient of
S^{\lambda}
(see
[
33
]
). Thus
S^{p-1,1}
is a characteristic
p
representation of
L
of dimension
p-1
and can be identified with the submodule of the natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-permutation module
\langle v_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq p\rangle
which is the kernel of the augmentation map
\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}v_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}
. In this case,
D^{p-1,1}=S^{p-1,1}/\langle\sum_{i=1}^{p}v_{i}\rangle
has dimension
p-2
. The result we shall need is as follows.

Lemma 0.4.10
.

Suppose that
p\geq 5
is a prime,
L\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
and
V=S^{p-1,1}
considered as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-module. Then
\Lambda^{2}(V)=S^{p-2,1^{2}}
has irreducible composition factors
D^{p-1,1}
and
D^{p-2,1^{2}}
both with multiplicity
1
and, furthermore,
\Lambda^{2}(V)
has no quotient of dimension
1
or isomorphic to
D^{p-1,1}
.

Proof.

For prime
p\geq 11
, the isomorphism type and composition factors of
\Lambda^{2}V
are explicitly given in
[
38
, Section 2]
. The fact that
D^{p-1,1}
is not a quotient of
\Lambda^{2}(V)
follows from
[
33
, Corollary 12.2]
. For
p=5
and
p=7
, we have checked the assertion by computer   (see Subsection
R.2
).
∎

Lemma 0.4.11
.

Assume that
p\geq 5
is a prime,
X=\mathrm{Sym}(p)
,
Y=X^{\prime}=\mathrm{Alt}(p)
and
V=D^{p-1,1}
is the
p-2
-dimensional module. Let
W=V|_{Y}
. Then
\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}(Y,W)=1
.

Proof.

Let
\tau=(1,2,3)
and
\sigma=(3,\dots,p)
. Then
H=\langle\tau,\sigma\rangle
acts transitively on
\Omega=\{1,\dots,p\}
and, as
p
is a prime, it is primitive. Since
H
contains a
3
-cycle,
Y=H
by Jordan’s Theorem
[
32
, II.4.5]
. If
U
is a
\mathrm{GF}(p)Y
-module with
W
of codimension
2
and
[U,Y]=W
, then
\dim C_{U}(\tau)=2+\dim C_{W}(\tau)=2+p-4=p-2
and
\dim C_{U}(\sigma)=2+\dim C_{W}(\sigma)=2+1=3
. Hence
\dim C_{U}(H)\geq 1
and this proves
\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}(Y,W)\leq 1
. Since the natural
p
-point permutation module has a quotient
T
with
[T,Y]=W
and
C_{T}(Y)=0
, we have
\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}(Y,W)=1
.
∎

0.5.
A primer on fusion systems

We assume some basic familiarity with fusion systems and recommend the references
[
5
,
16
]
as introductory texts. We follow the notation from these sources. We start by flying over the standard definitions and at the same time introduce some of the standard terminology from
[
5
,
16
]
.

Definition 0.5.1
.

For a finite group
G
and subgroups
H,K\leq G
, define

\mathrm{Hom}_{G}(H,K)=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{Hom}(H,K)\mid\varphi=c_{g}\text{ for some }g\in G\text{ such that }H^{g}\leq K\}

and set
\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(H)=\mathrm{Hom}_{G}(H,H)\cong N_{G}(H)/C_{G}(H)
.

More generally, if
H,K\leq G
, we define
\mathrm{Aut}_{K}(H)=\{c_{k}\mid k\in K\cap N_{G}(H)\}
to be the group of automorphisms of
H
induced by conjugation by elements of
K
which normalize
H
. Visibly
\mathrm{Aut}_{K}(H)\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(H)\leq\mathrm{Aut}(H)
. Similarly, if
K\leq L
and
A\leq\mathrm{Aut}(L)
, we write
\mathrm{Aut}_{A}(K)
to represent the group of automorphisms of
K
generated by the restriction of automorphisms in
N_{A}(K)=\{\gamma\in A\mid K\gamma=K\}
. In this case
\mathrm{Out}_{A}(K)=\mathrm{Aut}_{A}(K)\mathrm{Inn}(K)/\mathrm{Inn}(K)
.   For groups
P
and
Q
,
\mathrm{Inj}(P,Q)
is the set of injective group homomorphisms from
P
to
Q
.

Definition 0.5.2
.

A
fusion system
on a
p
-group
S
is a category
\mathcal{F}
, with objects the set of all subgroups of
S
, and   morphisms
\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)
between objects
P
and
Q
which satisfy the following two properties:

(i)

\mathrm{Hom}_{S}(P,Q)\subseteq\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(P,Q)
; and

(ii)

each
\varphi\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)
is the composite of an
\mathcal{F}
-isomorphism followed by an inclusion.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a fusion system and
P,Q\leq S
, then we write
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)=\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)=\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,P)
.

Definition 0.5.3
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a fusion system on a finite
p
-group
S
and
P\leq S
. Then

(i)

the
\mathcal{F}
-
conjugacy class
of
P
, is
P^{\mathcal{F}}=\{P\alpha\mid\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,S)\}
;

(ii)

P
is
strongly
\mathcal{F}
-closed
if and only if
Q^{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq P
for all
Q\leq P
;

(iii)

for
R\in P^{\mathcal{F}}
,
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(R,P)
,
\alpha^{*}
is the isomorphism between
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
defined by
\gamma\mapsto\alpha^{-1}\gamma\alpha
;

(iv)

P
is
fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized
if and only if
|N_{S}(P)|\geq|N_{S}(R)|
for all
R\in P^{\mathcal{F}}
;

(v)

P
is
fully
\mathcal{F}
-centralized
if and only if
|C_{S}(P)|\geq|C_{S}(R)|
for all
R\in P^{\mathcal{F}}
;

(vi)

P
is
S
-centric
if and only if
C_{S}(P)=Z(P)
, and
P
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric
if and only if
R
is
S
-centric for all
R\in P^{\mathcal{F}}
;

(vii)

if
R\in P^{\mathcal{F}}
and
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(R,P)
,

N_{\alpha}=\{g\in N_{S}(R)\mid\alpha^{-1}c_{g}\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P)\}

is the
\alpha
-
extension control subgroup
of
S
;

(viii)

P
is
\mathcal{F}
-
receptive
if and only if for all
R\in P^{\mathcal{F}}
and
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(R,P)
, there exists
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{\alpha},S)
such that
\widetilde{\alpha}|_{R}=\alpha;

(ix)

P
is
fully
\mathcal{F}
-automized
if and only if
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P)\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P))
;

(x)

P
is
\mathcal{F}
-
saturated
provided there exists
R\in P^{\mathcal{F}}
such that
R
is simultaneously

(a)

fully
\mathcal{F}
-automized; and

(b)

\mathcal{F}
-receptive.

(xi)

\mathcal{F}
is
saturated
if every subgroup of
S
is
\mathcal{F}
-saturated.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a fusion system and
X
is a set of morphisms in
\mathcal{F}
,
\langle X\rangle
is the intersection of all the fusion systems on
S
which contain
X
. We say that
\langle X\rangle
is the
fusion system generated
by
X
. Obviously
\langle X\rangle
is contained in
\mathcal{F}
.

In our arguments, an important role is played by
normalizer fusion systems.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
,
T\leq S
and
K\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
.  Then
N_{\mathcal{F}}^{K}(T)
is the fusion system on
N_{S}(T)
with, for
P,Q\leq N_{S}(T)
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P,Q)
consisting of morphisms
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)
such that there is
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(PT,QT)
with
T\widetilde{\alpha}=T
,
\widetilde{\alpha}|_{T}\in K
and
\alpha=\widetilde{\alpha}|_{P}
. Importantly, if
T
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized, then
N_{\mathcal{F}}^{K}(T)
is saturated
[
5
, Theorem I.5.5]
.   The two extreme cases
K=\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, and
K=1
are of main interest. In the former case we have the
\mathcal{F}
-normalizer
of
T
and we write
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)=N_{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathrm{Aut}(T)}(T)
whereas in the latter we have the
\mathcal{F}
-centralizer
of
T
and we define
C_{\mathcal{F}}(T)=N_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}(T)
.

A subgroup
T\leq S
is
normal
in
\mathcal{F}
if and only if
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
.
The subgroup
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
is the product of all subgroups
T\leq S
such that
T
is normal in
\mathcal{F}
.  It follows that
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(O_{p}(\mathcal{F}))
.
A saturated fusion system
\mathcal{F}
is
constrained
provided
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric.

Theorem 0.5.4

(The Model Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a constrained fusion system on
S
. Then there exists a finite group
G
with
O_{p^{\prime}}(G)=1
,
S\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(G)
,
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
and
C_{G}(O_{p}(G))\leq O_{p}(G)
.

Proof.

See
[
5
, Theorem III.5.10]
.
∎

We now gather some elementary consequences of the definitions above.
We have the following well-known fact and, as we shall use it several times, we provide the proof.

Lemma 0.5.5
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a fusion system on
S
and
P\leq S
.

(i)

If
\alpha\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))
, then
N_{\alpha}={N}_{S}(P)
.

(ii)

If
P
is
\mathcal{F}
-receptive, then

N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))=\{\alpha|_{P}\mid\alpha\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(P))}(P)\}.

Proof.

Part (i) is just the definition of
N_{\alpha}
.

Suppose that
\alpha\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))
. Then
N_{\alpha}=N_{S}(P)
by (i). Since
P
is
\mathcal{F}
-receptive,
\alpha=\widetilde{\alpha}|_{P}
where
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(P),S)
. As
P\widetilde{\alpha}=P\alpha=P
, we know
{N}_{S}(P)\widetilde{\alpha}=N_{S}(P\widetilde{\alpha})=N_{S}(P)
. Hence
\widetilde{\alpha}\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(P))}(P)
and so

N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))\subseteq\{\alpha|_{P}\mid\alpha\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(P))}(P)\}.

Conversely, assume
g\in N_{S}(P)
. Then
c_{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P)
and, for
\beta\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(P))}(P)
, we have
(\beta|_{P})^{-1}c_{g}\beta|_{P}=c_{g\beta}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P)
. Hence
\beta|_{P}\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))
. This proves (ii).
∎

Lemma 0.5.6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a fusion system on
S
,
T\leq S
and
\mathcal{K}=N_{\mathcal{K}}(T)
is a subfusion system of
\mathcal{F}
on
N_{S}(T)
. Assume that
R\leq T
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{K}}(T)
-invariant. Then
\mathcal{K}\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
. In particular, if
R\leq T
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-invariant, then
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
X,Y\leq N_{S}(T)
and
\theta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)
. Then, as
R
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{K}}(T)
-invariant, it is also
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(T)
-invariant and thus
R
is normalized by
N_{S}(T)
. In particular,
X,Y\leq N_{S}(T)\leq N_{S}(R)
and so
X
and
Y
are objects in
{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
. Since
\theta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)
and
\mathcal{K}=N_{\mathcal{K}}(T)
, the morphism
\theta
extends to
\hat{\theta}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{K}}(XT,YT)
so that
T\hat{\theta}=T
. Thus
\hat{\theta}|_{T}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{K}}(T)
and
R\hat{\theta}=R
because
R
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{K}}(T)
-invariant. As
\hat{\theta}
extends
\theta
,
\hat{\theta}|_{XR}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(XR,YR)
also extends
\theta
and this means that
\theta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(R)}(X,Y)
. Hence
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)\subseteq\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(R)}(X,Y)
and this proves the main statement of the lemma.

Taking
\mathcal{K}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
and noting
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(T)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
, yields the remaining statement.
∎

If
Q
is normal in
\mathcal{F}
, then the
factor system

\mathcal{F}/Q
has objects
\{T/Q\mid Q\leq S\}
and, for
Q\leq T,R\leq S
, morphisms
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}/Q}(T/Q,R/Q)=\{\bar{\phi}\mid\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(T,R)\}
where
(tQ)\bar{\phi}=t\phi Q
for
\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(T,R)
.

Lemma 0.5.7
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is saturated and
Q
is normal in
\mathcal{F}
, then
\mathcal{F}/Q
is saturated

Proof.

This is
[
5
, Lemma II.5.5]
.
∎

Definition 0.5.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a fusion system. A subgroup
P
of
S
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential
if
P\neq S
,
P
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric, fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
contains a strongly
p
-embedded subgroup. We write
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
to denote the set of
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups of
\mathcal{F}
.

Note that if
E\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
then
O_{p}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))=1
, that is,
O_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))=\mathrm{Inn}(E)
. This fact will be used several times.

The main tool for classifying   saturated  fusion systems is provided by the following lemma.

Theorem 0.5.9

(Alperin-Goldschmidt)
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on the
p
-group
S
, then

\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S),\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\mid E\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\rangle.

Proof.

See
[
5
, Theorem I.3.5]
.
∎

Lemma 0.5.10
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
and
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup. Then
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})\leq E
and
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant.

Proof.

Since
N_{O_{p}(\mathcal{F})}(E)
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant and
O_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))=\mathrm{Inn}(E)
, we have
N_{O_{p}(\mathcal{F})}(E)\leq E
by Lemma
0.2.6
. Hence
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})\leq E
and so is normal in
E
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant.
∎

We will also meet the fusion subsystems
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
and
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})
. We define the
focal
and
hyperfocal
subgroups as follows

\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=\langle[g,\alpha]\mid g\in Q\leq S\text{ and }\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)\rangle

\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})=\langle[g,\alpha]\mid g\in Q\leq S\text{ and }\alpha\in O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))\rangle

where
[g,\alpha]=g^{-1}(g)\alpha
. The subfusion system
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
is a saturated fusion system on
\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})
defined as follows:

O^{p}(\mathcal{F})=\langle\mathrm{Inn}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})),O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))\mid Q\leq\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})\rangle.

Lemma 0.5.11
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a saturated fusion system on
S
. The following hold:

(i)

\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=\langle[g,\alpha]\mid g\in Q\leq S,Q\text{ is }\mathcal{F}\text{-essential or }Q=S,\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)\rangle
.

(ii)

O^{p}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}
if and only if
\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=S
if and only if
\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})=S
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
0.5.9
and
[
5
, Corollary I.7.5]
.
∎

The subfusion system
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})
is more complicated to define, so we just settle for saying that it is the unique saturated subfusion system on
S
minimal subject to containing
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))
for all
Q\leq S
(see
[
5
, Definition I.7.3]
).

Definition 0.5.12
.

A saturated fusion system
\mathcal{F}
is called
reduced
if and only if
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
and
\mathcal{F}=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})=O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})
.

Normal subfusion systems are defined in
[
5
, Definition I.6.1]
and a
simple
saturated fusion system is a fusion system which has no proper normal subfusion system. Simple fusion systems are reduced as
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
,
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathcal{F}_{O_{p}(\mathcal{F})}(O_{p}(\mathcal{F}))
are normal subsystems of
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 0.5.13
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
. Assume that each
P\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
is minimal among all
\mathcal{F}
-centric subgroups.
For each
P\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
define

\mathrm{Aut}^{(P)}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)=\langle\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\mid P\alpha=P,\alpha|_{P}\in O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P))\rangle.

Then
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}
if and only if
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)=\langle\mathrm{Inn}(S),\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}^{(P)}(S)\mid P\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\rangle
.

Proof.

This is
[
42
, Lemma 1.4]
.
∎

In the next lemma, the containment
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
means that
E
is an object in
\mathcal{G}
and that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(E)
. We will use this notation from here on.

Lemma 0.5.14
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are saturated fusion systems with
{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
. Assume that
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
. Then
E
is
{\mathcal{G}}
-essential.

Proof.

We have
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(E)
and
E^{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq E^{\mathcal{F}}
. Therefore, as
E
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized and
\mathcal{F}
-centric it is also fully
{\mathcal{G}}
-normalized and
{\mathcal{G}}
-centric. Since
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)=\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{G}}(E)
, we know
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{G}}(E)
has a strongly
p
-embedded subgroup. Thus
E
is
\mathcal{G}
-essential.
∎

Recall that, following
[
5
, Proposition I.3.3]
, if
P<S
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized,
H_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
is defined to be the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
which is generated by those morphisms of
P
which extend to
\mathcal{F}
-isomorphisms between strictly larger subgroups of
S
. Then the statement in
[
5
, Proposition I.3.3]
, includes the fact that, if
P
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then
H_{\mathcal{F}}(P)/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
is strongly
p
-embedded in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
.

The next result is widely used to show that certain subgroups are not
\mathcal{F}
-essential.

Lemma 0.5.15
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
and
Q\leq S
. Assume that
Q_{s}<Q_{s-1}<\dots<Q_{0}=Q
are
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-invariant with
Q_{s}\leq\Phi(Q)
. If
A\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(Q)
and
[Q_{i},A]\leq Q_{i+1}
for
0\leq i\leq s-1
, then
A\leq O_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))
. In particular, if
A\not\leq\mathrm{Inn}(Q)
, then
Q
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential.

Proof.

Set
B=\langle A^{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)}\rangle
. Assume that
\beta\in B
has order coprime to
p
. Then
[Q,\beta;s]\leq Q_{s}
and so
[
24
, Theorem 5.3.6]
implies that
[Q,\beta]\leq Q_{s}\leq\Phi(Q)
. Thus
[
24
, Theorem 5.1.4]
implies
\beta=1
and so
B
is a
p
-group. Therefore
A\leq B\leq O_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))
and this proves the first claim. Since
O_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))=\mathrm{Inn}(E)
for
E\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
, if
A\not\leq\mathrm{Inn}(Q)
, then
Q
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential.
∎

If
Q
has a series of subgroups as in Lemma
0.5.15
and
P\leq S
centralizes   all quotients
Q_{i}/Q_{i+1}
, then we say that
P

stabilizes the series

Q_{s}<Q_{s-1}<\dots<Q_{0}=Q
and we conclude that, if
P\not\leq Q
, then
Q
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential.

The next lemma relies on Proposition
0.2.12
which provides a list of all non-abelian simple groups with a strongly
p
-embedded subgroup containing an elementary abelian subgroup of order
p^{2}
.

Lemma 0.5.16
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
and
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential. Then
|E/\Phi(E)|\geq|N_{S}(E)/E|^{2}
.

Proof.

This is
[
50
, Proposition 4.6 (4)]
. ∎

We now employ work of Henke
[
30
]
.

Proposition 0.5.17
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on the
p
-group
S
and
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup. Assume that
U
and
W
are
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant subgroups of
E
with
V=U/W
centralized by
\mathrm{Inn}(E)
and non-trivial as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))
-module. If
|[V,\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)]|\leq p
or
|V:C_{V}(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E))|\leq p
, then

O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))/C_{O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))}(V)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p),

and
V/C_{V}(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
is the natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module.

Proof.

The hypothesis that
V
is a non-trivial
\mathrm{GF}(p)O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))
-module implies that
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(E)
does not centralize
V
. Hence

|[V,\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)]|=p\leq|\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)/C_{\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)}(V)|

or

|V:C_{V}(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E))|=p\leq|\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)/C_{\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)}(V)|.

This, by definition, means that either the dual of
V
or
V
is a failure of factorisation module for the group
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))/C_{O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))}(V)
. Using
[
30
, Theorem 5.6]
, we obtain
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))/C_{O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))}(V)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{a})
and
V/C_{V}(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
is the natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{a})
-module. Since we know one of
|[V,\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)]|=p
or
|V:C_{V}(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E))|=p
, we deduce that
p=p^{a}
and this completes the proof.
∎

We continue this section by recalling important properties of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls (see Definition
0.1.1
).

Lemma 0.5.18
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime,
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
and
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
. Then

(i)

S
has maximal class;

(ii)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(p)
and
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
;

(iii)

P/\Phi(P)
is a natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module for
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P))
;

(iv)

[{N}_{S}(P)\colon P]=p
; and

(v)

every subgroup of
S
that is
\mathcal{F}
-conjugate to
P
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl.

Proof.

This is a combination of
[
28
, Lemma 1.5, Corollary 1.11 and Lemma 1.13]
.
∎

Lemma 0.5.19
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
of maximal class with
|S|\geq p^{4}
. If

E\leq S
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup of
S
, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl;

(ii)

E
is not contained in
\gamma_{1}(S)
or in
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
; and

(iii)

there exists an element
x\in S\setminus C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
of order
p
such that
either
E=\langle x\rangle Z(S)
or
E=\langle x\rangle Z_{2}(S)
.

Proof.

This is a restatement of
[
28
, Lemma 2.4]
. ∎

The next lemma helps when we apply Lemma
0.5.13
.

Lemma 0.5.20
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
with
|S|\geq p^{4}
. If
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
, then
no proper subgroup of
P
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric.

Proof.

There is nothing to do if
P\in\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
is abelian. Assume
P\in\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
is extraspecial. Since no subgroup of order
p
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric we can assume
Q\leq P
has order
p^{2}
. Then parts (ii) and (iii) of Lemma
0.5.18
imply that
Q
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
conjugate to
Z_{2}(S)\leq P
. Since
|S|\geq p^{4}
, and
|S:C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))|=p
, we conclude
Z_{2}(S)
is not
\mathcal{F}
-centric. Hence
Q
is not
\mathcal{F}
-centric.
∎

For a subgroup
A
of a group
B
, we define
N^{0}(A)=A
and then, for
i>0
,
N^{i}(A)=N_{B}(N^{i-1}(A))
. The ordered collection of these subgroups is called the
normalizer tower
of
A
in
B
and its length is the minimal
\ell
such that
N^{\ell+1}(A)=N^{\ell}(A)
.

Theorem 0.5.21
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime,
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
and
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl with
|S:P|=p^{m}
.
Then

(i)

the members of the normalizer tower of
P

{N}^{0}(P)<{N}^{1}(P)<{N}^{2}(P)<\dots<{N}^{m-1}(P)<{N}^{m}(P)=S

are the only subgroups of
S
which contain
P
; also
|{N}^{i}(P)\colon{N}^{i-1}(P)|=p
for every
1\leq i\leq m
;

(ii)

P
is not properly contained in any
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup of
S
; and

(iii)

every morphism in
{N}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))
is the restriction of an automorphism of
S
that normalizes each
{N}^{i}(P)
.

Proof.

This is an application of
[
28
, Theorem 3.6]
.
∎

Lemma 0.5.22
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
.
Let
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
and let
m
be such that
|S:P|=p^{m}
. For every
1\leq i\leq m-1
let
\mathcal{F}_{i}
be the smallest fusion subsystem of
\mathcal{F}
defined on
{N}^{i}(P)
such that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(P)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}({N}^{i}(P))=\mathrm{Inn}({N}^{i}(P)){N}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}({N}^{i}(P))}(P)
. Then
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a saturated fusion subsystem of
\mathcal{F}
and
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F}_{i})
.

Proof.

This is
[
28
, Lemma 3.7]
. ∎

Lemma 0.5.23
.

Assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
,
\mathcal{C}
is a set of
\mathcal{F}
-class representatives of
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups, and
P\in\mathcal{C}
. If
P
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, then
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S),\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\mid E\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{P\}\rangle
is saturated.

Proof.

This is
[
50
, Lemma 6.5]
.
∎

The next lemma is part of
[
28
, Theorem 3.15]
.

Lemma 0.5.24
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
with
|S|\geq p^{4}
and
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
.
Assume that the group
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian or extraspecial. Then

(i)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is a Hall
p^{\prime}
-subgroup of
\mathrm{Out}(S)
, is cyclic of order
p-1
and acts faithfully on
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
.

(ii)

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)=N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(P)\mathrm{Inn}(S)
.

(iii)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
.

Proof.

Because
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
,
S
has maximal class. Since
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian or extraspecial, Corollary
0.3.12
yields the Hall
p^{\prime}
-subgroups of
\mathrm{Out}(S)
are cyclic of order at most
p-1
and act faithfully on
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
. By Theorem
0.5.21
, every automorphism in
N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))
is the restriction of an automorphism of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Hence
|N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))/\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P)|\leq p-1
. Since
P
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, Lemma
0.5.18
(ii) implies that
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and we deduce that
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is cyclic of order
p-1
.
∎

Theorem 0.5.25
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime  and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
of order
p^{n}
with
n\geq 4
. Assume that
P\in\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
.

(i)

If
T
is strongly
\mathcal{F}
-closed in
S
, then either
T=S
or
T=\gamma_{2}(S)P
has index
p
in
S
and
\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})=P^{S}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian or extraspecial, then either

(a)

\mathcal{F}
is simple; or

(b)

O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
is simple,
\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=\gamma_{2}(S)P
,
S
is not exceptional and
n=j(p-1)+1
for some
j\geq 2
.

(iii)

If
p\geq 5
,
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian or extraspecial and
\mathcal{F}
is simple, then
\mathcal{F}
is exotic.

Proof.

Let
T\leq S
be strongly
\mathcal{F}
-closed in
S
. Since
T
is normal in
S
and
|Z(S)|=p
, we have
Z(S)\leq T
. Recall that
P\in\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
. Hence
Z(S)\leq P
,
P\not\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
and
P\not\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
by Lemma
0.5.19
. As
P
is an abelian
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, there exists
\beta\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
such that
P=Z(S)Z(S)\beta
. Since
T
is strongly
\mathcal{F}
-closed, this implies
P\leq T
. Using
T
is normal in
S
yields,
T\in\{S,\gamma_{2}(S)P\}
. Assume that
P_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\setminus P^{S}
.
Since
S
has maximal class,
S
acts transitively by conjugation on the subgroups of order
p^{2}
containing
Z(S)
which are not contained in
\gamma_{1}(S)
but which are contained in
\gamma_{2}(S)P
. Hence
P_{2}\not\leq\gamma_{2}(S)P
whereas we know
P_{2}\leq T
. Hence
T=S
in this case. This proves (i).

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
and that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian or extraspecial. This implies
|S|\geq p^{5}
.
Assume that
\mathcal{G}
is a weakly normal subsystem of
\mathcal{F}
defined on a non-trivial subgroup
T
of
S
. Then
T
is strongly
\mathcal{F}
-closed in
S
and so
T\in\{\gamma_{2}(S)P,S\}
by (i). Since
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian or extraspecial,
Lemma
0.5.24
implies that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is cyclic of order
p-1
. By
[
16
, Lemma 5.33]
,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(P)
is a normal subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. Since
|\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P)|=p
, we deduce that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(P)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
and this is true for all pearls
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
.
As
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
, for
R\leq S
with
|R|>p^{2}
, we have
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
is generated by restrictions of automorphisms of
S
by Theorem
0.5.9
. Hence no such subgroup can be
\mathcal{G}
-essential.
In particular,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
By Theorem
0.5.21
(iii),
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
has order divisible by
p-1
. On the other hand,
T
is not an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup by Theorem
0.5.21
(ii) and thus every element of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
is the restriction of some element of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
. We deduce that
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
is cyclic of order
p-1
.

If
T=S
, we have
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)
and Theorem
0.5.9
implies that
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{G}
. Therefore
\mathcal{F}
is simple in this case and this is listed as (ii)(a).

Suppose that
T=\gamma_{2}(S)P
.
Then, by (i),
\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})=P^{S}
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\neq P^{T}
.
Since
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
is normal in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
has order
p-1
, we conclude that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)\geq O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T))
. Using (i), we now know
\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})=\gamma_{2}(S)P
and so
\mathcal{G}=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{G})\neq P^{T}
,
T
is the only strongly
\mathcal{G}
-closed subgroup of
\mathcal{G}
by (i). Since
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
has order
p-1
, we now have
\mathcal{G}
is simple as before.

Continue to assume that
T=\gamma_{2}(S)P
. It remains to prove that
S
is not exceptional and that
n\equiv 1\pmod{p-1}
. Let
\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
have order
p-1
be such that
P\phi=P
and
\phi|_{P}
acts as the diagonal matrix
\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
. Then, choosing
x\in P\setminus\gamma_{1}(S)
and using the notation introduced in Lemma
0.3.10
, we have

x\phi\equiv x^{a}\pmod{\gamma_{2}(S)}
, and
s_{n-1}\phi=s_{n-1}{a^{-1}}
where
a\in\mathrm{GF}(p)
has order
p-1
.
Since
\phi\in O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T))=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
,
\phi
centralizes
S/T
and so

s_{1}\phi\equiv s_{1}\pmod{\gamma_{2}(S)}
(
b=1
in Lemma
0.3.10
). If
S
is exceptional, then
\phi
leaves
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
,
T
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
invariant, contrary to
a\neq 1
. Hence
S
is not exceptional. Now Lemma
0.3.10
applies to give

s_{n-1}^{a^{-1}}=s_{n-1}\phi=s_{n-1}^{a^{n-2}}.

Thus
a^{n-1}=1
so that
n\equiv 1\pmod{p-1}
, that is,
n=j(p-1)+1
for some
j\geq 1
. To complete the proof of (ii), note that if
j=1
then
n=p
and
S
has a maximal subgroup that is abelian by
[
28
, Theorem A]
, a contradiction. Hence
j\geq 2
.

Finally, assume that
\mathcal{F}
is simple and
p\geq 5
. If
\mathcal{F}
is not exotic, then
\mathcal{F}
is realised by a finite simple group by
[
16
, Theorem 5.71]
. But then Lemma
0.3.8
yields
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial, a contradiction. This demonstrates (iii).
∎

Semeraro’s theorem of can be used to add pearls to saturated fusion systems without destroying saturation.

Theorem 0.5.26
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be a saturated fusion system on a finite
p
-group
S
. Let
V\leq S
be a fully
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-normalized subgroup, set
H=\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(V)
and let

\widetilde{\Delta}\leq\mathrm{Out}(V)
be such that
H
is a strongly
p
-embedded subgroup of
\widetilde{\Delta}
. For
\Delta
the full preimage of
\tilde{\Delta}
in
\mathrm{Aut}(V)
, write

\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{0}),\Delta\rangle.

Assume further that

(i)

V
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-centric and minimal under inclusion amongst all
\mathcal{F}
-centric subgroups; and

(ii)

no proper subgroup of
V
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-essential.

Then
\mathcal{F}
is saturated.

Proof.

This is a statement of
[
55
, Theorem C]
for the special case
m=1
.
∎

In Section
0.13
we exploit results about fusion systems on
p
-groups with an abelian subgroup of index
p

[
42
,
19
,
45
]
. The tool for doing this is the following proposition.

Proposition 0.5.27
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime,
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
,
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)=\mathrm{Inn}(S){N}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(P)
. Assume that
Z(S)<V\leq Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
-invariant, put
S_{1}=VP
,

H=\mathrm{Inn}(S_{1})\langle\phi|_{S_{1}}\mid\phi\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(P)\rangle\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S_{1})

and

B=\{\phi|_{V}\mid\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\}\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(V).

Then the fusion system

\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P),B,H\rangle

on
S_{1}
is saturated and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S_{1})=H
.

Proof.

We have
P\leq S_{1}
and so, if
|S_{1}:P|=p^{k}
,
S_{1}=N^{k}(P)
by Theorem
0.5.21
(i).
Let
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P),H\rangle
. Then
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is the smallest fusion subsystem of
\mathcal{F}
defined on
S_{1}
such that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(P)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(S_{1})=\mathrm{Inn}(S_{1})N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S_{1})}(P)
. By Lemma
0.5.22
,
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is saturated.

Since
V
is normal in
S_{1}
,
V
is fully
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-normalized and, as
V>Z(S)
,
V
is abelian and
[S_{1}\colon V]=p
,
V
is an
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-centric subgroup of
S_{1}
. We have
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(V)=\mathrm{Aut}_{H}(V)
is a subgroup of
B\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(V)
. If
\mathrm{Aut}_{H}(V)=B
, then
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{k}
and
\mathcal{G}
is saturated. So we may assume that
\mathrm{Aut}_{H}(V)<B
. In particular, as
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)=\mathrm{Inn}(S){N}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(P)
,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))>\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and so
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is strongly
p
-embedded in
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Therefore
\mathrm{Out}_{H}(V)
is strongly
p
-embedded in
\mathrm{Out}_{B}(V)
. Furthermore, as
V
is abelian,
V
is minimal by inclusion amongst all
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-centric subgroups and no proper subgroup of
V
is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-essential. Now application of Theorem
0.5.26
delivers
\mathcal{G}
is saturated. That
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S_{1})=H
is apparent from its construction.
∎

0.6.
Fusion systems on groups of maximal class: generalities

This section begins the study of saturated fusion systems on maximal class
p
-groups.

Lemma 0.6.1
.

Suppose that
S
is a non-abelian
2
-group of maximal class and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
.   Then
S
is dihedral, semidihedral or generalized quaternion of order at least
8
and
\mathcal{F}
is known and realizable. In particular, if
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup of
S
, then
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is   cyclic .

Proof.

If
|S|=8
, then
S
is either dihedral or quaternion and it is an easy exercise to write down all the saturated fusion systems on
S
.   That the maximal class
2
-groups of order at least
2^{4}
are dihedral, semidihedral or generalized quaternion is well-known and can be found in
[
37
, Corollary 3.3.4 (iii)]
for example. In this case, the totality of the saturated fusion systems on such groups can be found in
[
5
, Example I.3.8]
.   ∎

Lemma 0.6.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a non-abelian maximal class
3
-group. Then
\mathcal{F}
is known,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
and, if
\gamma_{1}(S)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
, then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian.

Proof.

This is Theorem
Q.5
.
∎

Because of Lemmas
0.6.1
and
0.6.2
, for the remainder of this   work  we focus on the case
p\geq 5
.

Hypothesis 0.6.3
.

The prime
p
is at least
5
,
S
is a maximal class
p
-group of order at least
p^{4}
and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
.

Throughout this section and Sections
0.7
,
0.8
,
0.9
,
0.10
and
0.11
, we assume that Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds sway and adopt its notation.
We start with some lemmas which loosely locate the potential
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups within
S
. First we recall

Lemma 0.6.4
.

If
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup which is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, then either
E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
or
E\leq{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))
.
Furthermore, there are no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls in
\gamma_{1}(S)
or in
{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))
.

Proof.

This is just a restatement of Lemma
0.5.19
.
∎

The next result is included for completeness.

Corollary 0.6.5
.

If
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian, then the candidates for
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups are
\gamma_{1}(S)
and
\mathcal{F}
-pearls. In particular, if
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
, then
\mathcal{F}
has an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl.

Proof.

(See also
[
42
, Lemma 2.3 (a)]
). Since
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian,
S
is not exceptional. Thus, if
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, then
E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
by Lemma
0.6.4
. As
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric, this yields
E=\gamma_{1}(S)
as claimed. In particular, if
\mathcal{F}
has no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls, then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is the only
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup in
\mathcal{F}
. Since
\gamma_{1}(S)
is a characteristic subgroup of
S
, the Alperin-Goldschmidt Theorem yields
\gamma_{1}(S)=O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
. Hence, if
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
,  then
\mathcal{F}
has an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl.
∎

Lemma 0.6.6
.

If there exists a morphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}({Z}_{2}(S))
such that
{Z}(S)\varphi\neq{Z}(S)
, then
{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Since
{Z}_{2}(S)
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized and so
\mathcal{F}
-receptive, there exists an automorphism
\overline{\varphi}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}({C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S)))
such that
\overline{\varphi}|_{{Z}_{2}(S)}=\varphi
. In particular
\overline{\varphi}
does not normalize
{Z}(S)
and so it cannot be the restriction of an automorphism of
S
. Since
{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))
is characteristic in
S
, by the Alperin-Goldschmidt fusion theorem we deduce that
{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential.
∎

Proposition 0.6.7
.

Suppose
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
E
is normal in
S
. Then
E
is a maximal subgroup of
S
. Moreover, either
E\in\{\gamma_{1}(S),{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))\}
or
|S|=p^{4}
and
E
is a non-abelian
\mathcal{F}
-pearl.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
E
is not a maximal subgroup of
S
. Since
E\mathrel{\unlhd}S
and
S
has maximal nilpotency class,
E
is a member of the lower central series of
S
. Hence
E=\gamma_{k}(S)
for some
k\geq 2
. In particular,
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)\cong S/E
is a
p
-group of maximal class. Since
p
is odd,
S/E
contains an elementary abelian subgroup of order
p^{2}
by
[
24
, 5.4.10 (ii)]
. Lemma
0.2.10
yields that
F^{*}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)/O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
is a non-abelian simple group. Set
W=F^{*}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)/O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
and
X=W\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))/O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))
. Using Proposition
0.2.12
we know the candidates for
X
. Remember also that
p\geq 5
. Suppose first that
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)
is abelian. Then
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)\cong S/S^{\prime}
is elementary abelian of order
p^{2}
. The possibilities for
X
are
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{2})
,
\mathrm{Alt}(2p)
for arbitrary odd
p
, and
{}^{2}\mathrm{F}_{4}(2)^{\prime}
or
\mathrm{Fi}_{22}
with
p=5
. By a Frattini Argument and using
[
26
, Theorem 7.8.1]
,
{N}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(E))
acts irreducibly on
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)
. As every morphism in
{N}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(E))
is the restriction of an
\mathcal{F}
-automorphism of
S
(because
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-receptive), there exists an automorphism
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
such that
\gamma_{1}(S)\tau\neq\gamma_{1}(S)
, which is absurd as this subgroup is characteristic in
S
. If
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)
is non-abelian, then
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)
is extraspecial and the possibilities for
X
are
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(p)
for all
p\geq 5
,
\mathrm{McL}
or
{}^{2}\mathrm{B}_{2}(32){:}5
with
p=5
, and
\mathrm{J}_{4}
with
p=11
. This time
[
26
, Theorem 7.6.2]
shows that either
{N}_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(E))
acts irreducibly on
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)/Z(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E))
or
X\cong{}^{2}\mathrm{B}_{2}(32){:}5
. In the former case, we obtain a contradiction exactly as in the abelian case. Suppose that
X\cong{}^{2}\mathrm{B}_{2}(32){:}5
. Then
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)\cong 5^{1+2}_{-}\cong S/\gamma_{3}(S)
has exponent 25. Since
|S|\geq 5^{4}
, applying Lemma
0.3.2
(vi) to
S/\gamma_{4}(S)
yields
S/\gamma_{3}(S)
has exponent
5
, which is a contradiction. Therefore, if
E
is normal in
S
, then
E
is a maximal subgroup of
S
.

If
E\not\in\{\gamma_{1}(S),{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))\}
, then Lemma
0.5.19
implies
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl and so
|S|=p^{4}
. This proves the lemma. ∎

Lemma 0.6.8
.

Suppose that
|S|\geq p^{5}
and
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup which is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl. Then
|E|\geq p^{4}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
|E|\leq p^{3}
. Then, as
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
E
is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl,
|E|=p^{3}
and
E
is abelian. Furthermore, Lemma
0.6.4
indicates that either
E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
or
E\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
. Since abelian groups of order
p^{3}
and exponent at least
p^{2}
cannot be
\mathcal{F}
-essential, we have
E
is elementary abelian. Of course
Z(S)\leq E
. By
[
29
, Corollary 1.23]
, every automorphism in
N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(E))
is the restriction of an
\mathcal{F}
-automorphism of
S
and
|\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(E)|=p
. Since
E
is not normal in
S
by Proposition
0.6.7
,
E\neq Z_{3}(S)
. If
E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
, then
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(E)
and, if
E\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
, then
Z_{2}(S)\leq E
and
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(E)
. In particular, as
E\neq Z_{3}(S)
and
|N_{S}(E)|=p^{4}
,
N_{S}(E)=Z_{3}(S)E
in both cases. Since
|S|\geq p^{5}
,
Z_{3}(S)
is abelian and so
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Z(N_{S}(E))=}\,E\cap Z_{3}(S)
is a maximal subgroup of
E
. Proposition
0.5.17
(applied with
U=E
and
W=1
)  implies that
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and, if we take
\tau\in Z(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
to be an involution, then
[E,\tau]
is a natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))
-module. Let
\hat{\tau}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
be such that
\hat{\tau}|_{E}=\tau
and
\hat{\tau}
has
p^{\prime}
-order. Then

\hat{\tau}\text{ centralizes }Z_{3}(S)E/E\cong Z_{3}(S)/(Z_{3}(S)\cap E).

Suppose that
Z_{2}(S)=E\cap Z_{3}(S)
. Then
Z_{2}(S)=C_{E}(Z_{3}(S))
. If
[E,Z_{3}(S)]=Z(S)
, then
\hat{\tau}
inverts
Z(S)
and centralizes
Z_{2}(S)/Z(S)
. Hence
\hat{\tau}
centralizes the group
Z_{3}(S)/Z(S)
and this contradicts Lemma
0.3.14
. Hence
[E,Z_{3}(S)]\leq Z_{2}(S)
but is not contained in
Z(S)
. Therefore
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
Z(S)
and inverts
Z_{2}(S)/Z(S)
. Since
[Z_{3}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq Z(S)
, we have
E\not\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
and,   in particular,
S
is exceptional and
n
is even by Lemma
0.3.3
(v).  We apply Lemma
0.3.10
and use the notation from there with
\varphi=\hat{\tau}
. Thus, as
\hat{\tau}
inverts
E\gamma_{1}(S)/\gamma_{1}(S)\cong E/(E\cap\gamma_{1}(S))=E/Z_{2}(S)
,
a=-1
, we have
\hat{\tau}
acts as

(-1)^{n-3}b=-b=-1

on
Z_{2}(S)/Z(S)
and as

(-1)^{n-3}b^{2}=-b^{2}=1

on
Z(S)
. This is impossible and thus
Z_{2}(S)\not\leq E
. Now
N_{S}(E)=Z_{2}(S)E
and
[E,Z_{2}(S)]=Z(S)
. It follows that
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
Z_{2}(S)/Z(S)
and
(Z_{3}(S)\cap E)/Z(S)
. Hence
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
Z_{3}(S)/Z(S)
, which is impossible by Lemma
0.3.14
. We have proved, that, if
|E|=p^{3}
and
E
is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, then
E
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential.
∎

Lemma 0.6.9
.

If
O_{p^{\prime}}(Z(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))))
is non-trivial, then either

(i)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian; or

(ii)

S
is exceptional and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial.

Proof.

Assume that
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
projects to a non-trivial element of the group
O_{p^{\prime}}(Z(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))))
. Then
\tau
is the restriction of
\hat{\tau}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
of
p^{\prime}
-order and
[S,\hat{\tau}]\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
.
Lemma
0.3.11
now gives the result.∎

0.7.
Essential subgroups in exceptional maximal class groups

In this section, we start the investigation of
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups when
S
is exceptional of order
p^{n}
. We assume that

Hypothesis 0.7.1
.

Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds with
S
exceptional.

Because
S
is exceptional, we know from Lemma
0.3.3
(v) that
n
is even and

p^{6}\leq|S|=p^{n}\leq p^{p+1}.

Our aim is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 0.7.2
.

Suppose Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds. Then

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{{C}_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\}\cup\{E\mid{Z}(S)<E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)\}.

Furthermore,

(i)

if
\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\neq\emptyset
, then
|S|=p^{p-1}
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial;

(ii)

if
{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
, then
|S|=p^{6}
,
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and either

(a)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial; or

(b)

p=5
,
S=\mathrm{SmallGroup(5^{6},661)}
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\}
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is cyclic of order
4
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)
and
\mathcal{F}
is unique.

and

(iii)

if
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial, then

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)\}.

We prove Proposition
0.7.2
via a series of lemmas.

Lemma 0.7.3
.

Assume Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds. Then
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
(that is, every
\mathcal{F}
-pearl is abelian).

Proof.

Aiming for a contradiction, suppose
E
is an extraspecial
\mathcal{F}
-pearl. Then we have
{Z}(S)={Z}(E)
and the involution
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
which maps into
{Z}(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
inverts
E/{Z}(S)
and centralizes
{Z}(S)
. In addition, by Theorem
0.5.21
(iii), there is an automorphism
\hat{\tau}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
of
p^{\prime}
-order  such that
\hat{\tau}|_{E}=\tau
. Let
M
be the maximal subgroup of
S
containing
E
. Then
M\notin\{\gamma_{1}(S),C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\}
by Lemma
0.5.19
. Thus
MC_{S}(Z_{2}(S))=\gamma_{1}(S)C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))=S
and so, as
\gamma_{1}(S)
and
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
are characteristic in
S
,

\gamma_{1}(S)/\gamma_{2}(S)\cong S/C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\cong M/\gamma_{2}(S)=E\gamma_{2}(S)/\gamma_{2}(S)\cong E/{Z}_{2}(S)

as
\hat{\tau}
-groups. Hence
\gamma_{1}(S)/\gamma_{2}(S)
and
M/\gamma_{2}(S)
are inverted by
\hat{\tau}
. Therefore
\hat{\tau}
inverts
S/\gamma_{2}(S)
and so is an involution. Since
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
Z(S)
, this contradicts Lemma
0.3.13
. Thus every
\mathcal{F}
-pearl is abelian.
∎

Lemma 0.7.4
.

Assume Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds with
\gamma_{1}(S)/{Z}(S)
abelian. Then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial and no proper subgroup of
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential.

Proof.

Since
S
is exceptional,
Z(S)=Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
.
By assumption we have
{Z}(S)={Z}(\gamma_{1}(S))=[\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]
. Hence
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial.

Aiming for a contradiction, suppose there exists a subgroup
E<\gamma_{1}(S)
that is
\mathcal{F}
-essential.
Note that

[E,E]\leq[E,\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq[\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]={Z}(S)\leq E.

In particular,
E
is normal in
\gamma_{1}(S)
. If
E
is not elementary abelian, then
E
has normal series
E>\Phi(E)=Z(S)
which is stabilized by
\gamma_{1}(S)
. This contradicts Lemma
0.5.15
. Hence
E
is elementary abelian. Since
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial of order
p^{n-1}
, this implies
|E|\leq p^{n/2}
. In particular the quotient
\gamma_{1}(S)/E
is elementary abelian of order
[\gamma_{1}(S)\colon E]\geq p^{(n-2)/2}
. On the other hand,
Lemma
0.5.16
now yields that
|E|\geq p^{n-2}
. Thus
n=4
and Lemma
0.3.3
(v) contradicts the assumption that
S
is exceptional. Therefore no proper subgroup of
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential.
∎

Lemma 0.7.5
.

Assume Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds with
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Then
|S|=p^{6}
,
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)\}.

In addition, either
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial or
p=5
,
S=\mathrm{SmallGroup(5^{6},661)}
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\}
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is cyclic of order
4
,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)
and
\mathcal{F}
is unique.

Proof.

Set
R=C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
. Then, as
R\neq\gamma_{1}(S)
and
S/Z(S)
is not exceptional by Lemma
0.3.3
(vi), we glean
[\gamma_{2}(S),R]=\gamma_{3}(S)
. It follows that
R^{\prime}=\Phi(R)=\gamma_{3}(S)
and
R/\Phi(R)
has order
p^{2}
. Therefore
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
embeds into
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(p)
and

O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(R))=\langle\mathrm{Out}_{S}(R)^{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)}\rangle\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)

acts irreducibly on
R/\gamma_{3}(S)
. In particular,
\mathrm{Inn}(R)\leq O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R))
. Suppose that
|S|\geq p^{7}
.
Then

\gamma_{4}(S)=[\gamma_{3}(S),S]=[\gamma_{3}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)R]\geq[\gamma_{3}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)][\gamma_{3}(S),R]

Since
[\gamma_{3}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq\gamma_{5}(S)
and
[\gamma_{3}(S),R]
is normal in
S
, we deduce
[\gamma_{3}(S),R]=\gamma_{4}(S)
and similarly
[\gamma_{4}(S),R]=\gamma_{5}(S)
. Since
[R,R]=\gamma_{3}(S)
, we have
\gamma_{4}(S)=[R,R,R]
and
\gamma_{5}(S)=[R,R,R,R]
. Now we must have
O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R))
centralizes
\gamma_{3}(S)/\gamma_{5}(S)
. Since
\mathrm{Inn}(R)\leq O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R))
, this means that
[\gamma_{3}(S),R]\leq\gamma_{5}(S)
, a contradiction.
Hence
|S|\leq p^{6}
. Since
S
is exceptional, Lemma
0.3.3
(v) implies that
|S|=p^{6}
.

We next show that there are no
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups properly contained in
R
.
Aiming for a contradiction, suppose that
E<R
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup
E
. Then, as
E
is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, Lemma
0.6.8
implies
|E|=p^{4}
and so
E
is a maximal subgroup of
R
.
Recall that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
acts irreducibly on
R/\gamma_{3}(S)
. In particular, it acts transitively on the maximal subgroups of
R
containing
\gamma_{3}(S)=\Phi(R)
. Thus
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-conjugate to
\gamma_{2}(S)
. Since
E
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized, we deduce that
E=\gamma_{2}(S)
is normal in
S
, contradicting Proposition
0.6.7
. This proves the claim.

It remains to show that either
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial or
p=5
and, in the latter case, determine the structure of
\mathcal{F}
.
Let
\varphi\in N_{O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(R))}(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(R))
be the automorphism of order
p-1
corresponding to the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{-1}&0\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}
for a fixed
\lambda\in\mathrm{GF}(p)
of order
p-1
. Then by saturation there is an automorphism
\hat{\varphi}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
such that
\hat{\varphi}|_{R}=\varphi
. In particular
\hat{\varphi}
acts on
\gamma_{i}(S)
for every
i\geq 1
. Thus
for every
x,y\in S
such that
R=\langle x\rangle\gamma_{2}(S)
and
\gamma_{2}(S)=\langle y\rangle\gamma_{3}(S)
we have

(x\gamma_{2}(S))\varphi=x^{\lambda^{-1}}\gamma_{2}(S)\quad\text{ and }\quad(y\gamma_{3}(S))\varphi=y^{\lambda}\gamma_{3}(S).

Since
[R,\gamma_{1}(S)]=\gamma_{2}(S)
we also deduce that
\hat{\varphi}
raises the elements of
\gamma_{1}(S)/\gamma_{2}(S)
to the power
\lambda^{2}
.
By Lemma
0.3.10
we deduce that
\hat{\varphi}
raises the elements of
{Z}_{2}(S)/{Z}(S)
to the power
\lambda^{-1}
.
Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
be such that
\gamma_{1}(S)=\langle s_{1}\rangle\gamma_{2}(S)
and
\gamma_{2}(S)=\langle s_{2}\rangle\gamma_{3}(S)
. Then
s_{1}\hat{\varphi}=s_{1}^{\lambda^{2}}u
and
s_{2}\hat{\varphi}=s_{2}^{\lambda}v
for some
u\in\gamma_{3}(S)
and
v\in{Z}_{2}(S)
. Thus

[s_{1},s_{2}]\hat{\varphi}=[s_{1}^{\lambda^{2}}u,s_{2}^{\lambda}v]=[s_{1},s_{2}]^{\lambda^{3}}\mod{Z}(S).

Suppose
[s_{1},s_{2}]\notin{Z}(S)
. Then, since
[s_{1},s_{2}]\in[\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{2}(S)]\leq\gamma_{4}(S)={Z}_{2}(S)
, we get

[s_{1},s_{2}]\hat{\varphi}=[s_{1},s_{2}]^{\lambda^{-1}}\mod{Z}(S)

from Lemma
0.3.10
.
Therefore
\lambda^{3}\equiv\lambda^{-1}\pmod{p}
and so
\lambda^{4}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
. Hence either
[s_{1},s_{2}]\in{Z}(S)
or
p=5
.
In the former case, as
[\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{3}(S)]\leq{Z}(S)
, we deduce that

[\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]=[\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{2}(S)]=[\langle s_{1}\rangle\gamma_{2}(S),\langle s_{2}\rangle\gamma_{3}(S)]\leq{Z}(S).

Thus
\gamma_{1}(S)/{Z}(S)
is abelian   and Lemma
0.7.4
implies
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial.  This proves that either
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial or
p=5
.

Suppose that
p=5
and assume that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not extraspecial. Then
|S|=5^{6}
. All the groups of order
5^{6}
are known. We use
Magma
and the package from
[
50
]
to check that there are 39 maximal class
5
-groups,
16
of which are exceptional. Four of these groups have
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
non-soluble and two of them have
\gamma_{1}(S)
not extraspecial. This leaves two groups to consider. For one of the cases
\mathrm{Aut}(S)
is a
5
-group and so this cannot support a fusion system with
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
non-soluble. In
\mathrm{SmallGroup}(5^{6},661)
, we have
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
has cyclic Sylow
2
-subgroups of order
4
. In particular,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is uniquely determined up to isomorphism and by restriction we have a subgroup
Y
of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
of order
2^{2}.5^{4}
. Calculating in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
we find two conjugacy classes of subgroups
X
containing
\mathrm{Inn}(R)
and with
X/\mathrm{Inn}(R)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)
. Exactly one of these classes contains an
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
-conjugate of
Y
. We check that the corresponding fusion system is saturated using
[
50
]
(though it obviously is). This is the fusion system described in (ii). It remains to prove that there are no other candidates for
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups on
S=\mathrm{SmallGroup}(5^{6},661)
when
R
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential.   Computer code to do this using
[
50
]
is described in Subsection
R.3
.
However, we can also present an argument which does not require a computer. Suppose that
E\leq S
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl. Then there exist
\phi\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E))
of order
4
which is the restriction
\hat{\phi}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Recall that the Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(S)
has order
4
. It follows that
\hat{\phi}|_{R}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}({R})
. But then
\hat{\phi}
normalizes
{R}
,
\gamma_{1}(S)
and
E\gamma_{2}(S)
and these are distinct maximal subgroups of
S
. In particular,
\phi
acts on
S/\gamma_{2}(S)
as a scalar. However, we know that
\hat{\phi}|_{R}\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}({R})}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}({R}))
and this element does not act as a scalar on
S/\gamma_{2}(S)
, a contradiction. Hence
\mathcal{F}
has no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls. Now suppose that
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
with
E\not\leq R
. Notice that
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))=Z(S)
and, as
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not extraspecial,
\gamma_{1}(S)^{\prime}=Z_{2}(S)
and
\gamma_{3}(S)/Z(S)=Z(\gamma_{1}(S)/Z_{2}(S))
. Moreover,
\gamma_{2}(S)=C_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(Z_{2}(S))
. Thus
S
stabilizes the characteristic series
\gamma_{1}(S)>\gamma_{2}(S)>\gamma_{3}(S)>\gamma_{4}(S)
of
\gamma_{1}(S)
. Lemma
0.5.15
implies that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential. Hence
E<\gamma_{1}(S)
. By Lemma
0.6.8
we deduce that
|E|=5^{4}
. Thus
E
is a maximal subgroup of
\gamma_{1}(S)
. In particular
Z_{2}(S)=\gamma(S)^{\prime}=[\gamma_{1}(S),E]\leq E
. Since
E\nleq R
we deduce that
Z_{2}(S)\nleq{Z}(E)
and so
E
is not abelian. Thus
1\neq[E,E]<Z_{2}(S)
, that implies
|[E,E]|=p
. In particular
Z(S)[E,E]\leq{Z}(E)
. If
Z(S)\neq[E,E]
then
Z(S)[E,E]=Z_{2}(S)
and so
Z_{2}(S)\leq{Z}(E)
, a contradiction. Thus
Z(S)=[E,E]
. The group
\gamma_{3}(S)
stabilizes the sequence
1<Z<E
and by Lemma
0.5.15
we deduce that
\gamma_{3}(S)\leq E
. Also
E
is not extraspecial (it has order
5^{4}
), hence
|Z(E)|=5^{2}
. The group
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E)
acts non-trivially on
E/Z(E)
which is elementary abelian of order
25
. Hence by Proposition
0.5.17
we deduce that
O^{5^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)
. Let
\tau\in Z(O^{5^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
be an involution. We have already proved that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential, hence
E
is maximal
\mathcal{F}
-essential in
S
and so there is
\hat{\tau}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
such that
\hat{\tau}|_{E}=\tau
. Note that
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
\gamma_{2}(S)/\gamma_{3}(S)\cong\gamma_{1}(S)/E
and
Z(E)/Z(S)
. Since
E
is non abelian and
\gamma_{3}(S)
is abelian, we deduce that
Z(E)\leq\gamma_{3}(S)
and so
\gamma_{3}(S)=Z_{2}(S)Z(E)
. Hence
\gamma_{3}(S)/Z_{2}(S)
is congruent to
Z(E)/Z(S)
as a
\hat{\tau}
-group and it is therefore centralized by
\hat{\tau}
. We showed that
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
\gamma_{2}(S)/Z_{2}(S)
. Now,
\hat{\tau}
acts non-trivially on
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
and
\hat{\tau}
is an automorphism of the group
S/Z(S)
, that is not exceptional. Therefore we get a contradiction from Lemma
0.3.14
.

This proves that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{R\}
and completes the description of
\mathcal{F}
in the case when
S=\mathrm{SmallGroup}(5^{6},661)
and
R
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential.
∎

Lemma 0.7.6
.

Assume Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds. Suppose that
E\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
with
E\not\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
.  Then either
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl or
|S|=p^{6}
and
E=C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
.

Proof.

Suppose that the lemma is false. Set
R=C_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))
, and let
E
be an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup of
S
chosen of maximal order with
E
not contained in
\gamma_{1}(S)
. Then
E
is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl.   Lemma
0.6.4
and Lemma
0.6.8
together imply that
E\leq R
and
|E|\geq p^{4}
. By Lemma
0.7.5
, we have
R\not\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
and so
E<R
. Because
E<R
and
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, we have
{Z}_{2}(S)<E
. By Lemma
0.3.3
(vi),
S/Z(S)
is not exceptional. Since
E/{Z}(S)
is not contained in
\gamma_{1}(S/{Z}(S))=\gamma_{1}(S)/{Z}(S)
, Lemma
0.3.6
implies that
E/Z(S)
has maximal class. Since
|E|\geq p^{4}
,
E/Z(S)
is not abelian.
In particular,
Z(E/Z(S))=Z_{2}(S)/Z(S)
and this implies
Z(E)=Z_{2}(S)
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant.
Since
R\not\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
, Lemma
0.6.6
implies that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
leaves
Z(S)
invariant. Now
C_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(Z(S))
has
p^{\prime}
-index in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
and, since
E/Z(S)
has maximal class, Theorem
0.3.9
implies that
|E/Z(S)|=p^{3}
. Therefore
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))
acts on
E/Z(E)
as
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. Let
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
project to
Z(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
be an involution. As
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))
centralizes
Z_{2}(S)
,
\tau
centralizes
Z_{2}(S)
. Then, the maximal choice of
E
implies that there exists
\hat{\tau}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
so that
\hat{\tau}|_{E}=\tau
. In particular,
\hat{\tau}
has even order and inverts
E/Z(E)
. Since
Z(E)=Z_{2}(S)
and
E\gamma_{1}(S)=S
,
\hat{\tau}
inverts
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
. Assume that
(y\gamma_{2}(S))\hat{\tau}=y^{b}\gamma_{2}(S)
for some
b\in\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}.
Then, in Lemma
0.3.10
, we have
n
is even and
a=-1
and, as
\tau
centralizes
Z_{2}(S)
, we obtain the unfathomable equations

\displaystyle a^{n-3}b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b=1

\displaystyle a^{n-3}b^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b^{2}=1.

This contradiction completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Proposition
0.7.2
.

Assume Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds.
Let
E
be an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup. By Lemma
0.6.4
either
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl,
E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
or
E\leq{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))
. If
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, then
E
is abelian by Lemma
0.7.3
. If
E
is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl and it is not contained in
\gamma_{1}(S)
then
E={C}_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
by Lemma
0.7.6
. This proves that

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))\}\cup\{E\mid{Z}(S)<E\leq\gamma_{1}(S)\}

which is the displayed statement of the proposition.

If
\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\neq\emptyset
, then
[
28
, Theorem 3.14]
implies
|S|=p^{p-1}
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial. Hence (i) holds.

If
{C}_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then Lemma
0.7.5
gives (ii).

If
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial, then
\gamma_{1}(S)/{Z}(S)
is abelian and Lemma
0.7.4
gives (iii).
∎

0.8.
The structure of
\gamma_{1}(S)
when
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
S
is not exceptional

In this section we continue to assume Hypothesis
0.6.3
. In addition, we assume that
S
is not exceptional and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential. So we work with

Hypothesis 0.8.1
.

Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds with
S
not exceptional and
\gamma_{1}(S)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Our objective in this section is to explore the structure of
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
when Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds.

Lemma 0.8.2
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds. If
O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
is not centralized by
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, then
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is elementary abelian of order either
p^{p-1}
or
p^{p}
.

Proof.

Assume that
O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
is not centralized by
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Set
R_{0}=O_{p,p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
and

R=[R_{0},\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))]\mathrm{Inn}(\gamma_{1}(S)).

Then
R_{0}/\mathrm{Inn}(\gamma_{1}(S))=O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
,
R>\mathrm{Inn}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and, by
[
24
, Theorem 5.3.10]
, there exists an
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-chief factor
V
in
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
which is not centralized by
R
. The definition of
R
and coprime action implies that
R/C_{R}(V)
is not centralized by
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))C_{R}(V)/C_{R}(V)
. Applying Proposition
0.2.5
delivers
|V|\geq p^{p-1}
. Since
V
is elementary abelian, either
n=p+1
and
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
or
V=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iv).   In the latter case,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is elementary abelian and we are done.
Assume
|S|=p^{p+1}
and
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has order
p^{p}
.  If
V=\gamma_{2}(S)
, then, as
V
is irreducible,
V\leq Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
and
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is abelian. If
V=\gamma_{1}(S)/Z(S)
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial, then
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\neq\gamma_{1}(S)
and
S
is exceptional, a contradiction.
We conclude that
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is elementary abelian of order
p^{p-1}
or
p^{p}
.
∎

Remark 0.8.3
.

When
p=5
and
|S|=5^{6}
, the baby Monster sporadic simple group provides an example which demonstrates that when
S
is exceptional
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
may have a non-central normal subgroup of
5^{\prime}
-order which is not centralized by
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. See
[
47
, Table 5.1]
for example.

Recall that groups of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type are defined in Definition
0.4.1
.

Lemma 0.8.4
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds. If
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type, then either

(i)

\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
; or

(ii)

|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)):Z(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p
.

In particular,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is elementary abelian.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type. We have
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\leq p^{p}
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iv). Set
Z=Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
. If
Z\not<\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, then, as
S
has maximal class,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq Z
and (i) holds. So suppose that
Z<\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Then
Z
is elementary abelian because
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has exponent
p
.

Since
S
is not exceptional,
Z\geq Z_{2}(S)
and so
Z
is not centralized by
S
. In particular,
Z
admits a non-trivial action of
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and
C_{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))}(Z)
is a
p^{\prime}
-group.
As
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))/C_{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))}(Z)

is not of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type.
Therefore Theorem
0.4.2
implies
|Z|\geq p^{\frac{2}{3}(p-1)}
.

Define
X=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))/Z
. Then, assuming that (ii) does not hold,
|X|\geq p^{2}
. Since
\gamma_{1}(S)
is the
2
-step centralizer,
V=X/[X,\gamma_{1}(S)]Z
has order at least
p^{2}
. In particular,
V
is a
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
-module and it is not centralized by
S
.
Furthermore,
C_{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(V)
is a
p^{\prime}
-group and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)/C_{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(V)
is not of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type.
Applying Theorem
