0.4.2
yields
|V|\geq p^{\frac{2}{3}(p-1)}
.
Therefore, as
p\geq 5
, we obtain the contradiction

p^{p}\geq|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\geq|Z||V|\geq p^{2\frac{2}{3}(p-1)}=p^{\frac{4}{3}(p-1)}>p^{p}.

Hence
|X|\leq p
and (ii) holds. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 0.8.5
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds. If
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is non-abelian, then
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p^{p-1}
,

Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))=\Phi(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))=[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]=Z(\gamma_{1}(S)).

In particular,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has nilpotency class
2
. Furthermore,

L=\langle\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))^{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))}\rangle\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)

and, if
H\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
has
p^{\prime}
-order and projects to a complement to
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
in
N_{L}(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(E))
, then
|C_{\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))}(H)|=p^{2}
.

Proof.

Set
V=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))/[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]
and

V_{1}=[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]/[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)].

Then, as
\gamma_{1}(S)
is the
2
-step centralizer,
|V|\geq p^{2}
and so,
V
and
V_{1}
are not centralized by
S
unless
V_{1}=[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]=Z(S)
has order
p
.

By Lemmas
0.8.2
, and
0.8.4
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type and
O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
is centralized by
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Since
O_{p}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))=1
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not
p
-soluble and so, setting

L=\langle\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))^{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))}\rangle
, we have
L

has a quotient isomorphic to
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
and
L
is quasisimple. Hence
L\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
. Using Lemma
0.6.9
, we obtain
Z(L)=1
and so
L\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
. Let
\overline{H}
be a complement to
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
in
N_{L}(\mathrm{Out}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
and
H
be a preimage of
\overline{H}
of
p^{\prime}
-order. Then
H
is cyclic of order
(p-1)/2
.

In
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, assume that
W>U>T\geq 1
are
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-invariant subgroups with
\overline{W}=W/U
and
\overline{U}=U/T
both
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-chief factors. Then
\overline{W}
and
\overline{U}
can be regarded as
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-modules.
If
|\overline{W}|=|\overline{U}|=p
, then, as
\gamma_{1}(S)
is the
2
-step centralizer,
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\mathrm{Inn}(\gamma_{1}(S))
centralizes
U/T
, contrary to
S
having maximal class. Hence at least one of the chief factors has order greater then
p
.
Write
|\overline{W}|=p^{w}
and
|\overline{U}|=p^{u}
with
p^{3}\leq p^{u+w}\leq|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\leq p^{p}
. Using Lemma
0.4.5
and
L\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
, gives
u
and
v
are odd and, as
u+w\leq p
,
H
centralizes

[\overline{W},S;(w-1)/2]/[\overline{W},S;(w+1)/2]\text{ and }[\overline{U},S;(u-1)/2]/[\overline{U},S;(u+1)/2].

Because
S
has maximal class, there exists
\ell\geq 1
such

[W,S;(w-1)/2]=\gamma_{\ell}(S)/U

and

[U,S;(u-1)/2]=\gamma_{\ell+(w+1)/2+(u-1)/2}(S)/T.

By Lemma
0.3.14
,

\ell-(\ell+(w+1)/2+(u-1)/2)\equiv 0\pmod{(p-1)/2}.

Hence

w+u=k(p-1)

for some integer
k
. Therefore
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\geq p^{p-1}
. Assume
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p^{p-1}
. Then
W=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, and

U=Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))=\Phi(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))=[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]=Z(\gamma_{1}(S))

and the statements in the lemma hold.
Assume that
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|>p^{p-1}
. Then Lemma
0.3.2
(iv) implies that
|S|=p^{p+1}
. This gives
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))=\gamma_{1}(S)
and there exists
\gamma_{1}(S)\geq W>U>T>A\geq 1
with
W/U
,
U/T
and
T/A
each
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-chief factors. We assume that they have order
p^{u}
,
p^{w}
and
p^{t}
respectively. Then
u+w=w+t=p-1
and
u+w+t\leq p
. This means that
w=t=1
and
u=p-2
. Notice that
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
is normalized by
S
and is
L
-invariant. So
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))\in\{W,U,T\}
. Since
\gamma_{1}(S)
is non-abelian by assumption, we must have
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))=T=Z(S)
. Hence
S
is exceptional, a contradiction.
∎

We remark that we have constructed a saturated fusion system which satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
0.8.5
using
Magma
(see Subsection
R.4
). The example is realized by a group
G
of shape
7^{3+3}{:}\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(7)
. Taking
S\in\mathrm{Syl}_{7}(G)
,
S
has exponent
7
,
\gamma_{1}(S)
is special with centre of order
7^{3}
. Setting
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
, we get

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong 7^{3}{:}\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(7)
.  In this case,
\gamma_{1}(S)
is the unique
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup   and
\mathcal{F}
cannot be further decorated with pearls to create a larger saturated fusion system
\mathcal{G}
with
\mathcal{G}
-pearls.

0.9.
The structure of
\gamma_{1}(S)
when
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
S
is exceptional

We now consider Hypothesis
0.7.1
once again, applying results from Section
0.8
to fusion systems on the non-exceptional group
S/Z(S)
.
The objective of this section is to prove

Proposition 0.9.1
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds with
\gamma_{1}(S)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial.

Proof.

Assume that
S
is exceptional and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential but is not extraspecial.

Set
Q=\gamma_{1}(S)
,
V=Z_{2}(Q)
,
Z=Z(Q)=Z(S)
and
\mathcal{K}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(Z)
. Then
Z
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized and so
\mathcal{K}
is saturated,
Q
is a
\mathcal{K}
-essential subgroup and
Q/Z
is a
\mathcal{K}/Z
-essential subgroup. By Lemma
0.3.3
(v) and (vi),
S/Z(S)
is not exceptional and
|S|\leq p^{p+1}
and
Lemma
0.3.2
(vi) yields
S/Z(S)
has exponent
p
.
Because
Q
is not extraspecial and
Q/Z=\Omega_{1}(Q/Z)
, we know
\Omega_{1}(Q/Z)
is not elementary abelian. Hence Lemma
0.8.5
yields
(Q/Z)^{\prime}=Z(Q/Z)=V/Z
and
Q
has order
p^{p}
. Furthermore,
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
.

Let
A
be the preimage of
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))
in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
and
a
be a natural number such that
|V/Z|=p^{a}
. Then, as
V/Z
is an irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)A
-module by Lemma
0.8.5
,
a
is odd and so
V
is not extraspecial and, as
A
acts irreducibly on
V/Z
, we deduce that
V
is elementary abelian. Because
A
acts irreducibly on
Q/V
and
V
is abelian, we also have
C_{Q}(V)=V
. Therefore, using
Z
has order
p
and
Q/Z(Q)
and
V/Z
are irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)A
-modules, we have
Q/V\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{GF}(p)}(V,Z)
(the dual of
V
) which also has order
p^{a}
. Since
|Q|=p^{p}
, we infer that
a=(p-1)/2
. As
a
is odd, we also have
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
. Since
Q/Z
is special, the commutator map
\kappa:Q/V\times Q/V\rightarrow V/Z
induces a non-trivial
\mathrm{GF}(p)A
-module homomorphism
\kappa^{*}:\Lambda^{2}(Q/V)\rightarrow V/Z
. In particular,
V/Z
is isomorphic to a quotient of
\Lambda^{2}(Q/V)
as
\mathrm{GF}(p)A
-modules. Writing
d=a-1=(p-1)/2-1
, Proposition
0.4.6
yields
d\equiv 2\pmod{4}
. Hence
p\equiv 7\pmod{8}
.
Finally, we note that by Lemma
0.3.2
(vi),
\gamma_{2}(S)
has exponent
p
and thus as
Q
is regular by Lemma
0.3.2
(ii), Lemma
0.2.1
(ii) and the irreducible action of
A
on
Q/V
implies
Q
has exponent
p
.
We summarise what has been established.

Claim 1
.

The following hold:

(i)

p\equiv 7\pmod{8}
and
d\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.

(ii)

Q
has exponent
p
and nilpotency class
3
,
V=Q^{\prime}=C_{Q}(V)
has order
p^{(p+1)/2}
and
Z=Z(Q)
has order
p
.

(iii)

A/\mathrm{Inn}(Q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
acts irreducibly on
Q/V\cong V/Z\cong\mathbf{V}_{\frac{p-3}{2}}
and
A
centralizes
Z
.

We intend to make an explicit calculation and so we begin by establishing some notation. Let
\epsilon
be a generator of
\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}=(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
. Thus
\epsilon
is an integer with
1\leq\epsilon\leq p-1
. We regard
Q/V
and
V/Z
as unfaithful representations of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and we identify
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(Q)
with
\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle
and let
\tau\in N_{A}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(Q))
have order
(p-1)/2
be
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\epsilon&0\\
0&\epsilon^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
The element
\iota
has order
2
and corresponds to
\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle
in
A
. Hence
\iota
inverts
\tau
and does not normalize
\mathrm{Out}_{S}(Q)
.

We have
Q/V
is semisimple as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)\langle\tau\rangle
-module and
\tau
normalizes
\gamma_{j}(S)
for all
j\geq 1
. The semisimple action of
\tau
implies that we may select
t_{i}\in\gamma_{i}(S)\setminus\gamma_{i+1}(S)
,
1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2
such that
t_{i}V
is an eigenvector for
\tau
on
Q/V
.

By
1
(iii) the
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
-modules
Q/V
and
V/Z
can identified with the
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module
\mathbf{V}_{\frac{p-3}{2}}=\mathbf{V}_{d}
. When we make this identification, we may suppose that
t_{1}
corresponds to
x^{d}
,
t_{(p-1)/2}
is
y^{d}
and generally
t_{j}
corresponds to
x^{d-j+1}y^{j-1}
for
1\leq j\leq(p-1)/2=d+1
. We calculate that

(t_{j}V)\tau=t_{j}^{\epsilon^{d-2j+2}}V.

Define
m=(d+2)/2
. Then, as
p\equiv 7\pmod{8}
by
1
(i),
m
is even and we also have
(t_{m}V)\tau=t_{m}V
.
Furthermore,
we can calculate

Claim 2
.

(t_{j}V)\iota=(t_{d+2-j}V)^{(-1)^{(j-1)}}.

For
1\leq\rho_{i}\in V
,
1\leq i\leq d+1
be such that
\rho_{i}Z
are eigenvectors for
\tau
with
\rho_{i}\in\gamma_{(p-1)/2+i}(S)\setminus\gamma_{(p-1)/2+i+1}(S)
. Lemma
0.3.11
implies that for each
j
we have

(\rho_{j}Z)\tau=\rho_{j}^{\epsilon^{d-2j+2}}Z

which is consistent with making the standard identification with
\mathbf{V}_{d}
.
Just as above we have

Claim 3
.

(\rho_{j}Z)\iota=(\rho_{d+2-j}Z)^{(-1)^{(j-1)}}.

Observe that, as
m
is even,
2
and
3
demonstrate that
\iota
inverts
t_{m}V
and
\rho_{m}Z
.

We use the commutator relations as follows

[\rho_{j},t_{k}]\tau=[\rho_{j}^{\epsilon^{d-2j+2}}z,t_{k}^{\epsilon^{d-2k+2}}v]=[\rho_{j}^{\epsilon^{d-2j+2}},t_{k}^{\epsilon^{d-2k+2}}]=[\rho_{j},t_{k}]^{\epsilon^{2d-2(j+k)+4}}

where
v\in V
and
z\in Z
. Since
\tau
centralizes
Z
by
1
(iii), using the above commutator calculation and exploiting
1
(ii) to obtain equality demonstrates that the following statement holds.

Claim 4
.

We have
C_{Q}(\rho_{j})=V\langle t_{k}\mid k\neq d+2-j\rangle
.

Set

r_{j}=[t_{j},t_{m}]
for
1\leq j\leq d+1
, then
r_{m}=1
and the action of
\tau
shows that
r_{j}\in\langle\rho_{j}\rangle Z
.

Since
Q
has nilpotency class
3
and
p\geq 5
, Lemma
0.2.3
and Theorem
0.2.4
imply that
V\leq E_{2}(Q)<Q
and the irreducible action of
A
on
Q/V
then yields
V=E_{2}(Q)
. We obtain a contradiction by demonstrating that
t_{m}\in E_{2}(Q)
. Thus we calculate
[t_{m},y,y]
where
y=\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}t_{i}^{a_{i}}
with
1\leq a_{i}\leq p
is a coset representative of
V
in
Q
. Notice that in this next calculation, we have
[t_{m},w_{1}w_{2}]\in[t_{m},w_{1}][t_{m},w_{2}]Z
and so
[t_{m},w_{1}w_{2},w]=[[t_{m},w_{1}][t_{m},w_{2}],w]
for
w,w_{1},w_{2}\in Q
.

\displaystyle[t_{m},y,y]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[t_{m},\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}t_{i}^{a_{i}},y]=[\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}[t_{m},t_{i}^{a_{i}}],y]=[\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}r_{i}^{a_{i}},y]=\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}[r_{i},y]^{a_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}[r_{i},\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}t_{k}^{a_{k}}]^{a_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}[r_{i},t_{d+2-i}]^{a_{i}+a_{d+2-i}}

where the last equality follows from
4
.
Notice that, as
m
is even by
1
(i), we apply
\iota
as follows

r_{i}\iota=[t_{i}\iota,t_{m}\iota]=[t_{d+2-i}^{(-1)^{(i-1)}},t_{m}^{-1}]=r_{d+2-i}^{(-1)^{i}}.

Hence, as
d
is even by
1
(i),
2
and
3
yield

[r_{i},t_{d+2-i}]\iota=[r_{d+2-i}^{(-1)^{(i)}},t_{i}^{(-1)^{(d+2-i-1)}}]=[r_{d+2-i},t_{i}]^{(-1)^{i+(d+2-i-1)}}=[r_{d+2-i},t_{i}]^{-1}

and so, as
r_{m}=1
, we can pair elements in the product
\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}[r_{i},t_{d+2-i}]^{a_{i}+a_{d+2-i}}
to obtain
[t_{m},y,y]=1
for all
y\in Q
.
Hence
t_{m}\in E_{2}(Q)
and this is our contradiction which establishes that if
Q
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then
Q
is extraspecial.

0.10.
Locating
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups in groups of maximal class I

This section and the following section are devoted to the proof of Theorem
D
. To set the scene we repeat its statement.

Theorem D
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime,
S
is a
p
-group of maximal class and order at least
p^{4}
and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
. If
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup, then either
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl, or
E=\gamma_{1}(S)
or
E=C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
. Furthermore, if
S
is exceptional, then
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
.

Lemmas
0.6.1
and
0.6.2
show that Theorem
D
holds if
p\leq 3
. Hence we may and do assume that
p\geq 5
and so Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds and we take our notation from there.

Lemma 0.10.1
.

Assume Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds. If
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian and
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then either
E
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl or
E=\gamma_{1}(S)
.

Proof 0.10.2
.

This is a consequence of Corollary
0.6.5
.

We now show that Theorem
D
holds when
|S|
is small.

Lemma 0.10.3
.

Assume Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds. Suppose that
p^{4}\leq|S|\leq p^{5}
and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
. If
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then
E
is either an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl or
E\in\{\gamma_{1}(S),C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\}
.

Proof 0.10.4
.

Suppose
E
is not an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl. If
|S|=p^{4}
, then
E
is normal in
S
and the result follows from Proposition
0.6.7
. Hence
|S|=p^{5}
and
|E|=p^{4}
by Lemma
0.6.8
. In particular,
E
is a normal subgroup of
S
. Again Proposition
0.6.7
yields the result.

Because of Corollary
0.3.12
, Proposition
0.7.2
and Lemmas
0.10.1
and
0.10.3
we work under the following hypothesis until the proof of Theorem
D
is complete.

Hypothesis 0.10.4
.

Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds with
|S|\geq p^{6}
and the saturated fusion system
\mathcal{F}
is a minimal counterexample to Theorem
D
, first with respect to
|S|
and second with respect to the number of morphisms in
\mathcal{F}
. Furthermore,

(i)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian or extraspecial.

(ii)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is cyclic of order dividing
p-1
and acts faithfully on
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
.

We now assume that Hypothesis
0.10.4
is satisfied and say   that an
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup which is not contained in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S),{C}_{S}({Z}_{2}(S))\}
is a
witness
(to the fact that Theorem
D
is false).

Lemma 0.10.5
.

If
E
is a witness, then
E
is not normal in
S
and
E<\gamma_{1}(S)
.

Proof 0.10.6
.

This follows from Lemma
0.6.4
and Propositions
0.6.7
and
0.7.2
.

Lemma 0.10.7
.

The saturated fusion system
\mathcal{F}
has no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls. Furthermore, every witness
E
is properly contained in
\gamma_{1}(S)
,
|E|\geq p^{4}
and
\Omega_{1}(E)=E\cap\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
.

Proof 0.10.8
.

The fact that
\mathcal{F}
has no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls follows from Lemma
0.5.23
and the minimality of
\mathcal{F}
.   The first part of the second statement is in Lemma
0.10.5
. Since
\mathcal{F}
has no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls, Lemma
0.6.8
implies
|E|\geq p^{4}
. Finally, we note that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is regular by Lemma
0.3.2
(ii) and so Lemma
0.2.1
(iii) gives the final statement.

Our first major consequence of Hypothesis
0.10.4
is as follows.

Proposition 5
.

We have
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
.

Proof 0.10.9
.

Let
Q=O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
and assume that
Q\neq 1
. Let
E
be a witness.
As
Q
is normal in
S
and contained in
E
by Lemma
0.5.10
, Lemma
0.10.5
implies
Q<E
with
|S:E|\geq p^{2}
and so there exists
j\geq 3
such that
Q=\gamma_{j}(S)
.

We first demonstrate that
Q
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric. Suppose this is false. Then
C_{S}(Q)\not\leq Q
and so
C_{S}(Q)=\gamma_{k}(S)
for some
k<j
where we assume that
S=\gamma_{0}(S)
. In particular,
Q
is abelian and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)\cong\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
.
If
C_{S}(Q)\leq E
, then
C_{S}(Q)=C_{E}(Q)
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant. Hence
N_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Q))\subset\mathcal{F}
as
C_{S}(Q)>Q
. Since
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\subset N_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Q))
, Lemma
0.5.14
implies
E
is
N_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Q))
-essential and this contradicts the minimality of
\mathcal{F}
.
Therefore
C_{S}(Q)=\gamma_{k}(S)\not\leq E
and we choose
k<\ell\leq j
so that
\gamma_{\ell}(S)\leq E
and
\gamma_{\ell-1}(S)\not\leq E
. Then
\gamma_{\ell-1}(S)\leq N_{S}(E)
and
\gamma_{\ell-1}(S)
centralizes
Q
.

Set
L=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{\gamma_{\ell-1}(S)}(E)^{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}\rangle
. Then
L
centralizes
Q
and
C_{L}(E/Q)
is a
p
-group by coprime action
[
24
, Theorem 5.3.6]
.
As
L
is normal in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
, we have
O_{p}(L)\leq O_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))=\mathrm{Inn}(E)
. Hence

\mathrm{Inn}(E)\geq O_{p}(L)\geq C_{L}(E/Q).

For
K\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
, set

\overline{K}=K\mathrm{Inn}(E)/\mathrm{Inn}(E)\leq\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)=\overline{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}.

By Lemma
0.5.7
,
\mathcal{F}/Q
is saturated on
S/Q
and we know
S/Q
has maximal class. Since
Q\neq 1
, Lemma
0.3.3
(vi) implies that, if
|S/Q|\geq p^{4}
,
S/Q
is not exceptional.
We claim
E/Q
is
\mathcal{F}/Q
-essential.
Certainly
E/Q
is fully
\mathcal{F}/Q
-normalized. Let
J=C_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(E/Q)
. We have
[J,L]\leq J\cap L\leq C_{L}(E/Q)\leq\mathrm{Inn}(E)
. Hence
\overline{J}
and
\overline{L}
commute. Therefore
\overline{J}
normalizes
\overline{\mathrm{Aut}_{\gamma_{\ell-1}(S)}(E)}
.

Let
T=J\cap\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(E)\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(J)
and assume that
\overline{T}\neq 1
. Then we obtain
\overline{J}=N_{\overline{J}}(\overline{\mathrm{Aut}_{\gamma_{\ell-1}(S)}(E)})
and, by the Frattini Argument,

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)=\overline{J}N_{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(\overline{T}).

Thus

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)=N_{\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)}(\overline{T})N_{\overline{J}}(\overline{\mathrm{Aut}_{\gamma_{\ell-1}(S)}(E)}),

which contradicts
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
having a strongly
p
-embedded subgroup. This proves that
T\leq\mathrm{Inn}(E)
and that
\overline{J}
is a
p^{\prime}
-group. Assume that
E/Q
is not
\mathcal{F}/Q
-centric. Then
C_{S/Q}(E/Q)\not\leq E/Q
. Hence there exists
x\in N_{S}(E)\setminus E
such that
c_{x}\in J
and
\overline{c_{x}}\neq 1
, contrary to
\overline{J}
having
p^{\prime}
-order. Hence
E/Q
is
\mathcal{F}/Q
-centric. Finally, we note that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}/Q}(E/Q)\cong\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)/J
and so
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}/Q}(E/Q)\cong\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)/\overline{J}
. As
J
has
p^{\prime}
-order and is centralized by
\overline{L}
, Lemma
0.2.11
implies
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}/Q}(E/Q)
has a strongly
p
-embedded subgroup. Thus
E/Q
is
\mathcal{F}/Q
-essential as claimed.
Therefore, if
Q
is not
\mathcal{F}
-centric, then
E/Q
is
\mathcal{F}/Q
-essential. In particular,
|E/Q|\geq p^{2}
and
|S:E|\geq p^{2}
as
E
is not normal in
S
. Hence
|S/Q|\geq p^{4}
and
E/Q<\gamma_{1}(S)/Q
. This contradicts the minimal choice of
\mathcal{F}
.
Hence
Q
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric.

By Theorem
0.5.4
, there exists a group
G
which is a model for
\mathcal{F}
. For this model, we have
Q=O_{p}(G)
and
C_{G}(Q)=Z(Q)
. Furthermore,
C_{G}(Q/\Phi(Q))=Q
and
E/\Phi(Q)
is
\mathcal{F}_{S/\Phi(Q)}(G/\Phi(Q))
-essential. If
\Phi(Q)\neq 1
, we apply induction to obtain a contradiction as
\mathcal{F}_{S/\Phi(Q)}(G/\Phi(Q))
has
essential subgroups which are properly contained
\gamma_{1}(S/\Phi(Q))
. Hence
Q
is elementary abelian. Since
Q
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric, it is the largest normal abelian subgroup of
S
, say
Q=\gamma_{w}(S)
. Then, as
|S:E|\geq p^{2}
and
E>Q
,
w\geq 3
and
|Q|\leq|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\leq p^{p}
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iv).

Since
Q=\gamma_{w}(S)
is elementary abelian, Theorem
0.3.15
implies that
\gamma_{w-1}(S)
has nilpotency class
2
. Hence
\gamma_{w-1}(S)
acts quadratically on
Q
. Consider
G/Q
. We have
O_{p}(G/Q)=1
. Since
\gamma_{w-1}(S)
acts quadratically on
Q
, and
p>3
,
\gamma_{w-1}(S)/Q
centralizes
O_{p,p^{\prime}}(G)/Q
by
[
13
, Lemma 1.2]
. In particular,
G/Q
has a component of order divisible by
p
. Suppose that
K
is the preimage of such a component. Then
S\cap K>Q
. If
K
is not normalised by
S
, then
|K^{S}|\geq p
and
\langle(K^{S}\rangle\cap S)/Q
contains an elementary abelian
p
-group of rank
p
. Since
|S|\geq|Q||\langle K^{S}\rangle\cap S||S:N_{S}(K)|\geq p^{2}.p^{p}.p=p^{p+3}
, this contradicts Lemma
0.3.2
(v). Hence
K
is normalized by
S
, it follows that
K\cap S
is normal in
S
. If
L/Q\neq K/Q
is a component of
G/Q
with
S\cap L>Q
, then
L\cap S
is normal in
S
and we see that
Z(S/Q)\geq Z((L\cap S)(K\cap S)/Q)
has order at least
p^{2}
. This is impossible as
S/Q
has maximal class and
|S/Q|\geq p^{3}
. Hence
K/Q
is the unique such component and we have
F^{*}(G/Q)\leq O_{p^{\prime}}(G/Q)K/Q
with
\gamma_{w-1}(S)
acting quadratically on
Q
. Since
\gamma_{w-1}(S)/Q
has order
p
and centralizes
O_{p^{\prime}}(G/Q)
,
\gamma_{w-1}(S)/Q
acts faithfully on
K/Q
. Because
S/Q
has maximal class and order at least
p^{3}
,
\gamma_{w-1}(S)/Q
is contained in every non-trivial normal subgroup of
S/Q
. Hence

S/Q
acts faithfully on
K/Q
. As
p\geq 5
,
[
13
, Theorem A]
implies that
K/Q
is a group of Lie type defined in characteristic
p
. We refer to
[
26
, Theorem 2.5.12]
for properties of automorphism groups of groups of Lie type. Since
p\geq 5
,
K/Q
has no graph automorphisms of order
p
and so, if
S\not\leq K
,
SK/K
is a cyclic group of field automorphisms of
K/Q
. If
SK/K>1
, it follows from
[
26
, Theorem 3.3.1]
that
|\Omega_{1}(Z((S\cap K)/Q))|\geq p^{p}
and again we contradict Lemma
0.3.2
(iv). Hence
S\leq K
.
Since
S/Q
is
2
-generated and non-abelian, we now see that
K
is a rank at most
2
Lie type group defined over
\mathrm{GF}(p)
. Hence
[
26
, Theorem 3.3.1(b)]
yields
K/Q
mod its centre is one of
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(p)
,
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(p)
,
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(p)
,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(p)
, and in each case
S/Q
has maximal class. However in these groups we have
|N_{K/Q}(S/Q)|\geq(p-1)^{2}/3>p-1
, contrary to Hypothesis
0.10.4
(ii). This contradiction illustrates that
Q=1
and completes the proof of the proposition.

Lemma 0.10.10
.

Assume that
E
is a witness and suppose there is
n>j\geq 1
such that
\gamma_{j}(S)\leq E
. Then
\gamma_{j}(S)
is not
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant.

Proof 0.10.11
.

Note that
\gamma_{j}(S)
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized since it is normal in
S
.
Consider the saturated fusion system
{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{j}(S))
on
S
. Aiming for a contradiction, suppose
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\subseteq{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{j}(S))
, Lemma
0.5.14
implies that
E
is
{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{j}(S))
-essential. Thus
{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{j}(S))
is a counterexample to Theorem
D
and by the minimality of
\mathcal{F}
we deduce that
\mathcal{F}={N}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{j}(S))
. But then
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})\neq 1
, contrary to Proposition
5
.

In the next three lemmas we exploit Proposition
5
to provide both lower and upper bounds for the order of
S
.

Proposition 6
.

If
S
is exceptional then
|S|\geq p^{8}
.
In particular, if
S
is exceptional, then
{C}_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential.

Proof 0.10.12
.

Because
S
is exceptional, Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds. Aiming for a contradiction, suppose that
S
has order
p^{6}
and let
E
be a witness. By Hypothesis
0.10.4
and Lemma
0.10.5
we know
E<\gamma_{1}(S)
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not extraspecial.
Note that
Z(S)
is not
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant by Lemma
0.10.10
. In particular,
Z(S)<Z(E)
and so
|Z(E)|\geq p^{2}
. By Lemmas
0.6.8
and
0.10.5
,
|E|=p^{4}
and
E\neq\gamma_{2}(S)
as
E
is not normal in
S
. So
\gamma_{1}(S)=EE^{s}
for some
s\in S
. Also, as
\gamma_{2}(S)={C}_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\cap\gamma_{1}(S)
,
E\neq\gamma_{2}(S)
implies
Z_{2}(S)\cap Z(E)=Z(S)
.
If
E
is abelian then
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))=E\cap E^{s}
has order
p^{3}
, contradicting the fact that
{Z}(\gamma_{1}(S))=Z(S)
because
S
is exceptional. Hence
E
is non-abelian and
|Z(E)|=p^{2}
. This implies

[E,E]\leq[\gamma_{1}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]\cap Z(E)\leq Z_{2}(S)\cap Z(E)=Z(S).

Since
E
is not abelian, we get that
[E,E]=Z(S)
and so
{Z}(S)
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant, a contradiction. This proves
|S|\geq p^{8}
and the last statement follows from Proposition
0.7.2
.

Lemma 0.10.13
.

We have
|S|\geq p^{7}
.

Proof 0.10.14
.

By Hypothesis
0.10.4
we have
|S|\geq p^{6}
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian.
Aiming for a contradiction, suppose
|S|=p^{6}
. By Lemma
6
the group
S
is not exceptional. Let
E<\gamma_{1}(S)
be a witness. By Lemmas
0.6.8
and
0.10.5
,
|E|=p^{4}
,
\gamma_{1}(S)=N_{S}(E)
and
Z_{2}(S)\leq Z(\gamma_{1}(S))<E
. In addition
E\neq\gamma_{2}(S)
as
E
is not normal in
S
. The group
Z_{2}(S)
is not
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant by Lemma
0.10.10
, hence
Z_{2}(S)<Z(E)
. So
|E\colon Z(E)|\leq p
and we conclude that
E
is abelian. Since
S^{p}\leq Z(S)
by Lemma
0.3.2
(vi) and
Z(S)
is not
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant by Lemma
0.10.10
, we also get that
E
has exponent
p
, that is,
E
is elementary abelian. Let
s\in S\setminus N_{S}(E)
. Then
\gamma_{1}(S)=EE^{s}
and so
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))=E\cap E^{s}
has order
p^{3}
and
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is non-abelian. Also,
\gamma_{3}(S)=Z(\gamma_{1}(S))={C}_{E}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and
[\gamma_{1}(S)\colon E]=p=[E\colon{C}_{E}(\gamma_{1}(S))]
. Proposition
0.5.17
applied with
V=E
implies
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and
E/{C}_{E}(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
is a natural module for
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E))
. In particular the group
K={C}_{E}(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
has order
p^{2}
and it is contained in
\gamma_{3}(S)={Z}(\gamma_{1}(S))
.

If
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then   Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds and Lemma
0.8.5
implies that
p^{5}=p^{p-1}
, a contradiction. Hence
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
E
is not properly contained in any
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup of
S
.
Let
\tau\in{Z}(O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)))
be an involution. Then there is
\hat{\tau}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
such that
\hat{\tau}|_{E}=\tau
and
\hat{\tau}
has
p^{\prime}
-order. So
{C}_{\gamma_{3}(S)}(\hat{\tau})=C_{E}(\hat{\tau})=K
has order
p^{2}
and
[\gamma_{1}(S),\hat{\tau}]\leq E
. Hence
|C_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(\hat{\tau})|=p^{3}
. The only way this is compatible with Lemma
0.3.10
is if
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
\gamma_{1}(S)/\gamma_{2}(S)
,
\gamma_{3}(S)/\gamma_{4}(S)
and
\gamma_{5}(S)=Z(S)
and inverts
\gamma_{2}(S)/\gamma_{3}(S)
and
\gamma_{4}(S)/Z(S)
. As
\gamma_{1}(S)/E\cong\gamma_{2}(S)/\gamma_{3}(S)
which is inverted by
\hat{\tau}
, we have a contradiction. Hence
|S|\geq p^{7}
.

We now turn to determining an upper bound for
|S|
.

Lemma 0.10.15
.

Suppose that
E
is a witness. Then
Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq E
and
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\not\leq E
. In particular,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not abelian,
|S|<p^{2p-4}
and
p\geq 7
.

Proof 0.10.16
.

Using Lemma
0.2.1
(iii), we have

[{N}_{Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))}(E),E]\leq\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cap E\leq\Omega_{1}(E)\leq\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))

and so the group
{N}_{Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))}(E)
stabilizes the characteristic series
1<\Omega_{1}(E)<E
. Since
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, Lemmas
0.2.6
and
0.5.15
imply that
Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq E
.

If
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq E
, then
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))=\Omega_{1}(E)
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant, contrary to Lemma
0.10.10
. Hence
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\not\leq E
. This proves the first two statements. In particular, as
Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq E
,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not abelian.

Assume that
|S|\geq p^{2p-4}
. Then
|S|>p^{p+1}
since
2p-4>p+1
unless
p=5
and in the latter case we know
|S|>5^{6}
by Lemma
0.10.13
. Thus
S
has positive degree of commutativity by Lemma
0.3.3
(iv) and (v), and
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))=\gamma_{n-p+1}(S)
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iii).
Since
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not abelian and
S
has positive degree of commutativity we get

\displaystyle Z(S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma_{n-1}(S)\leq[\gamma_{n-p+1}(S),\gamma_{n-p+1}(S)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\gamma_{n-p+1}(S),\gamma_{n-p+2}(S)]\leq\gamma_{2n-2p+3+1}(S).

This implies that
n\leq 2p-4-1
, contradicting our assumptions. Therefore
|S|<p^{2p-4}
. Now, if
p=5
we obtain
|S|<5^{6}
, contradicting Lemma
0.10.13
. Hence
p\geq 7
.

We conclude this section with a characterization of the proper subsystems of
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 0.10.17
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}
is a proper saturated fusion subsystem of
\mathcal{F}
on
S
. Then
\mathcal{K}\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
.

Proof 0.10.18
.

Suppose
\mathcal{K}\not\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Then there is a
\mathcal{K}
-essential subgroup
P
.
Since
\mathcal{K}
is properly contained in
\mathcal{F}
, it is not a counterexample to Theorem
D
. Hence either
P
is a
\mathcal{K}
-pearl or
P\in\{C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)\}
.
If
P
is a
\mathcal{K}
-pearl then
P
is contained in some
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup
E^{*}
. By Lemma
0.10.7
we have
P\leq E^{*}\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
or
P\leq E^{*}\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
both of which are impossible by Lemma
0.6.4
applied to
P
. Thus
P
is not a
\mathcal{K}
-pearl. If
P=C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\neq\gamma_{1}(S)
, then
S
is exceptional and
p
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential. This contradicts Lemma
6
. Hence the only option is
P=\gamma_{1}(S)
. This proves the statement.

0.11.
Locating
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups in groups of maximal class II

In this section, we continue to prepare for the proof of Theorem
D
. In particular, we continue to work under Hypothesis
0.10.4
.
We start by creating a compendium facts that we have established about saturated fusion systems which satisfy Hypothesis
0.10.4
.

Lemma 0.11.1
.

Suppose that
E\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
is a witness.

(i)

E<\gamma_{1}(S)
,
E
is not normal in
S
and
|E|\geq p^{4}
;

(ii)

if
S
is exceptional, then
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not extraspecial;

(iii)

p\geq 7
and
p^{7}\leq|S|<p^{2p-4}
;

(iv)

\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not abelian;

(v)

O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
;

(vi)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
acts faithfully on
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
and is cyclic of order dividing
p-1
;

(vii)

if
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then
S
is not exceptional and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
.

Proof 0.11.2
.

Part (i) follows from Lemmas
0.10.5
and
0.10.7
.   Part (ii) is Proposition
6
and, as in this case Hypothesis
0.7.1
holds, Lemma
0.7.4
.
Part (iii) is a combination of Lemmas
0.10.13
and
0.10.15
. Part (iv) follows from Lemma
0.10.15
.

Part (v) is precisely Proposition
5
. Using part (iv), (vi) follows from Corollary
0.3.12
. Finally, for part (vii), if
S
is exceptional, then Proposition
0.9.1
implies
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial, which is against (iv). Hence
S
is not exceptional and Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds. Thus
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
by Lemma
0.8.5
. Using (vi) gives
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
.

Suppose that
E
is a witness. Then
E<\gamma_{1}(S)
by Lemma
0.11.1
(i). If
N_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, then
E
is
N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-essential by Lemma
0.5.14
and we obtain the contradiction
E<\gamma_{1}(S)\leq O_{p}(N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq E
from Lemma
0.5.10
. Hence
N_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\not\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Hence the set of subgroups
T
of
\gamma_{1}(S)
with
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\not\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is non-empty. We now set up the notation which shall be used in the remainder of this section.

Notation 0.11.2
.

Set
\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. From among all non-trivial subgroups
T\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
, select one which satisfies the following conditions in the specified order.

(i)

N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
;

(ii)

|N_{S}(T)|
is maximal; and

(iii)

|T|
is maximal.

Observe that
N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
with equality if and only if
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential.

The discussion before Notation
0.11.2
shows that a witness satisfies the first condition and so we can conclude that a subgroup
T
as specified in Notation
0.11.2
exists.

Lemma 0.11.3
.

The subgroup

T
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized and
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
is saturated.

Proof 0.11.4
.

By
[
5
, Lemma I.2.6 (c)]
, there exists
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(T),S)
such that
T\alpha
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized. By the Alperin-Goldschmidt Theorem,
\alpha
is a product of maps from
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(A)
,
A\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
with
|N_{S}(T)|\leq|A|
and from
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Then, as
A\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
by Lemmas
0.10.5
and
0.11.1
(ii) , the selection method of
T
shows that each
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(A)
is contained in
\mathcal{G}
as is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Let
X,Y\leq N_{S}(T)
. Then
\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T\alpha)}(X\alpha,Y\alpha)\supseteq\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(X,Y)\alpha^{*}
. Since
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T\alpha)}(X\alpha,Y\alpha)\subset\mathcal{G}
if and only if
\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(X,Y)\alpha^{*}\subset\mathcal{G}
. We conclude that
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T\alpha)\not\subset\mathcal{G}
. Since
|N_{S}(T\alpha)|\geq|N_{S}(T)|
, the maximal choice of
N_{S}(T)
implies that
T
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized.

Lemma 0.11.5
.

Assume that
1\neq K\leq T
. Then following statements hold.

(i)

If
K
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-invariant, then
N_{S}(K)=N_{S}(T)
.

(ii)

If
T
is not normal in
S
and
K
is characteristic in
N_{S}(T)
, then
K
is not
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-invariant.

Proof 0.11.6
.

Suppose
that
K
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-invariant. Then
K
is invariant under
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(T)
and so is normal in
N_{S}(T)
. Lemma
0.5.6
states that
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(K)
. Therefore, if
N_{S}(K)>N_{S}(T)
, the maximal choice of
|N_{S}(T)|
implies
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(K)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
which contradicts the choice of
T
. This proves (i).

Part (ii) follows from (i).

Lemma 0.11.7
.

If
K
is a normal subgroup of
S
which is contained in
T
, then
K
is not
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-invariant. In particular,
T
is not normal in
S
.

Proof 0.11.8
.

Suppose that
K\leq T
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-invariant and normal in
S
. Then Lemma
0.11.5
(i) implies that
T
is normal in
S
. Hence
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
is a fusion system on
S
. Since
T\leq O_{p}(N_{\mathcal{F}}(T))
and
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
,
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\neq\mathcal{F}
. Application of Lemma
0.10.17
yields
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, a contradiction.

0.11.1.
The case
T
is
S
-centric

In this subsection we assume

Hypothesis 0.11.8
.

Hypothesis
0.10.4
holds and adopting Notation
0.11.2
we have
T
is
S
-centric; that is
C_{S}(T)\leq T
.

As
T
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-normalized by Lemma
0.11.3
, Hypothesis
0.11.8
implies
T
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric. Since
Z(S)\leq T
and
T
is not normal in
S
by Lemma
0.11.7
, Lemma
0.3.7
implies
N_{S}(T)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
.
By Theorem
0.5.4
there exists a model
G
for
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
. Choose
G_{1}
such that
G\geq G_{1}>N_{S}(T)
and
G_{1}
has minimal order such that
\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 0.11.9
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. We have
T=O_{p}(G_{1})=O_{p}(G)
and
C_{G_{1}}(T)\leq T
.

Proof 0.11.10
.

Notice that
N_{S}(O_{p}(G_{1}))\geq N_{S}(T)
and
O_{p}(G_{1})\geq T
. As
\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})=N_{\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})}(O_{p}(G_{1}))\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(O_{p}(G_{1}))
, we get
N_{\mathcal{F}}(O_{p}(G_{1}))\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
. The maximal choice of
T
now yields
T=O_{p}(G_{1})
. We now have
T\leq O_{p}(G)\leq O_{p}(G_{1})=T
and so
T=O_{p}(G)=O_{p}(G_{1})
. As
G
is a model for
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
,
C_{G_{1}}(T)\leq C_{G}(T)\leq T
.

Lemma 0.11.11
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. Then the following hold.

(i)

\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\not\leq T
; and

(ii)

Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq\Omega_{1}(T)
.

Proof 0.11.12
.

If
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq T
, then
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))=\Omega_{1}(T)
is normalized by
S
and is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-invariant contrary to Lemma
0.11.7
. Hence
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\not\leq T
. This proves (i).

Let
R=N_{Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))}(T)
. Then, as
\gamma_{1}(S)
is regular by Lemma
0.3.2
(ii), using Lemma
0.2.1
(iii) yields

[T,R]\leq T\cap\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq\Omega_{1}(T)

and
[\Omega_{1}(T),R]\leq[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),R]=1
. Hence
R
centralizes
T/\Omega_{1}(T)
and
\Omega_{1}(T)
. Now Lemmas
0.5.15
and
0.11.11
imply
\mathrm{Aut}_{R}(T)\leq O_{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(T))=\mathrm{Inn}(T)
. Hence
R\leq T
. Applying Lemma
0.2.6
yields
Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq T
, as claimed.

Before reading the next lemma the following example is worthy of some consideration. Let
X=2^{6}:3^{.}\mathrm{Sym}(6)
. Then
X
is isomorphic to a
2
-local subgroup in
\mathrm{M}_{24}
. Let
R\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(X)
. Then
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)
has three
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)
-essential subgroups
E_{0}=R\cap X^{\prime}
,
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
where
E_{1}/O_{2}(X)\cong E_{2}/O_{2}(X)
are elementary abelian of order
8
. We have
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)}(E_{i})\cong\mathrm{Sym}(3)
for
i=0,1,2
. Here is the point:
X=\langle N_{X}(E_{1}),N_{X}(E_{2})\rangle
, however
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)\neq\langle\mathcal{F}_{R}(N_{X}(E_{1})),\mathcal{F}_{R}(N_{X}(E_{2}))\rangle
by
[
50
, Lemma 3.13]
.

Lemma 0.11.13
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. Then
N_{S}(T)
is contained in a unique maximal subgroup of
G_{1}
.

Proof 0.11.14
.

Suppose that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are maximal subgroups of
G_{1}
with
N_{S}(T)\leq M_{1}\cap M_{2}
and
M_{1}\neq M_{2}
.
By the minimal choice of
G_{1}
,

\langle\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(M_{1}),\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(M_{2})\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{G}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(M_{1})}(T),\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(M_{2})}(T)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{M_{1}}(T),\mathrm{Aut}_{M_{2}}(T)\rangle=\mathrm{Aut}_{G_{1}}(T)

as
G_{1}=\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle.
Since
T
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric,
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq T
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)
leaves
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
invariant. Since
N_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(\gamma_{1}(S)))
is a fusion system on
S
and
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
,
N_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(\gamma_{1}(S)))\subseteq\mathcal{G}
by Lemma
0.10.17
.
Therefore

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})}(T)=\mathrm{Aut}_{G_{1}}(T)\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(\gamma_{1}(S))).

Since
\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})=N_{\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})}(T)
, application of Lemma
0.5.6
gives
\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, a contradiction. Hence
N_{S}(T)
is contained in a unique maximal subgroup of
G_{1}
.

Let
U
be the unique maximal subgroup of
G_{1}
which contains
N_{S}(T)
.

Lemma 0.11.15
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. If
K
is a non-trivial characteristic subgroup of
N_{S}(T)
, then
N_{G_{1}}(K)\leq U
.

Proof 0.11.16
.

We have
N_{G_{1}}(K)\geq N_{S}(T)
and so, as
U
is the unique maximal subgroup of
G_{1}
which contains
N_{S}(T)
, if
U
does not contain
N_{G_{1}}(K)
, then
K
is normalized by
G_{1}
. Suppose that
K
is normal in
G_{1}
. Then, by Lemma
0.11.9
,
K\leq O_{p}(G_{1})=T\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
and
\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(K)
which means that
N_{\mathcal{F}}(K)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
. It follows from the maximal choice of
|N_{S}(T)|
that
|N_{S}(N_{S}(T))|\leq|N_{S}(K)|\leq|N_{S}(T)|
, and so we must have that
T
is normal in
S
. This contradicts Lemma
0.11.7
and demonstrates
N_{G_{1}}(K)\leq U
.

Recall from
[
11
]
that for a finite group
X
and
R\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(X)
,

C(X,R)=\langle N_{X}(K)\mid 1\neq K\text{ a characteristic subgroup of }R\rangle.

In
[
11
]
, they also define

C^{*}(X,R)=\langle N_{X}(K)\mid 1\neq K\text{ a characteristic subgroup of }B(R)\text{ or }\Omega_{1}(Z(R))\rangle,

where
B(R)
is the Baumann subgroup (see
[
11
, Definition 1.1 and just before]
). Since
B(R)
and
\Omega_{1}(Z(R))
are characteristic in
R
, we have
C^{*}(X,R)\leq C(X,R)
.

Lemma 0.11.17
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. Then there is a natural number
b\geq 1
such that
O^{p}(G_{1})/O_{p}(O^{p}(G_{1}))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{b})
and
O_{p}(O^{p}(G_{1}))
is a natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)O^{p}(G_{1})/O_{p}(O^{p}(G_{1}))
-module. Furthermore,
G_{1}/T\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{b})
.

Proof 0.11.18
.

By Lemma
0.11.15
, we have
C^{*}(G_{1},N_{S}(T))\leq C(G_{1},N_{S}(T))\leq U
. Lemma
0.11.9
states
C_{G_{1}}(T)\leq T
and
T=O_{p}(G_{1})
. Since
U
is the unique maximal subgroup of
G_{1}
containing
N_{S}(T)
, we may apply
[
11
, Corollary 1.9]
to obtain
B(N_{S}(T))
-blocks
B_{1},\dots,B_{r}
such that the product
B_{1}\dots B_{r}
is normal in
G_{1}
,
[B_{i},B_{j}]=1
for
1\leq i<j\leq r
and
G_{1}=(B_{1}\dots B_{r})C(G_{1},N_{S}(T))
. Furthermore,   as
U
is the unique maximal subgroup of
G_{1}
containing
N_{S}(T)
, we have
G_{1}=(B_{1}\dots B_{r})N_{S}(T)
and
N_{S}(T)
permutes
\{B_{1},\dots,B_{r}\}
transitively by conjugation. In particular,
r
is a power of
p
.
The definition of a
B(N_{S}(T))
-block and the fact that
p\geq 5
yields
B_{1}=O^{p}(B_{1})
,
B_{1}/O_{p}(B_{1})\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{b})
for some
b\geq 1
,
O_{p}(B_{1})=\Omega_{1}(Z(O_{p}(B_{1})))
and
O_{p}(B_{1})
is a natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)B_{1}/O_{p}(B_{1})
-module. Assume
r\geq p
. Then
|S|\geq|N_{S}(T)|\geq p^{3p}
which contradicts Lemma
0.11.1
(iii). Hence
r<p
, and, as
r
is a power of
p
, we must have
r=1
. It follows that
B_{1}
is normal in
G_{1}
and thus
B_{1}=O^{p}(G_{1})
. Finally, we note that if
G_{1}/T=G_{1}/O_{p}(G_{1})\not\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{b})
, then some
p
element of
G_{1}
must induce a non-trivial field automorphism on
B_{1}/O_{p}(B_{1})
. But then
p\geq b
and
|N_{S}(T)\cap B_{1}|=p^{3p}
, which once again contradicts Lemma
0.11.1
(iii). Hence
G_{1}/O_{p}(G_{1})\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{b})
and this proves the lemma.

We define

B_{1}=O^{p}(G_{1})\text{ and }W=O_{p}(B_{1}).

Lemma 0.11.19
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. Then
G_{1}/T\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and
W=[T,B_{1}]
has order
p^{2}
.

Proof 0.11.20
.

By Lemma
0.11.17
, there is a natural number
b
such that
B_{1}/O_{p}(B_{1})\cong G_{1}/T\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p^{b})
and
G_{1}=B_{1}T
. Select
g
of order
p^{b}-1
in
N_{G_{1}}(N_{S}(T))
and set
\theta=c_{g}
. Then, by saturation,
\theta
is the restriction of a morphism
\theta^{*}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(T))
. The maximal choice of
T
implies that
\theta^{*}
is the restriction of a morphism
\hat{\theta}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
.
In particular,
\hat{\theta}
has order a multiple of
p^{b}-1
. We shall show that
b=1
.
Since
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
has order dividing
p-1
by Lemma
0.11.1
(vi), we may suppose that
\mathcal{G}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Hence
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. In this case
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
by Lemma
0.11.1
(vii), and, as the maximal order of a
p^{\prime}
-element of
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
is
p+1
, we conclude that
b=1
. Therefore
G_{1}/T\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and
W=[T,B_{1}]
has order
p^{2}
.

Lemma 0.11.21
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. Suppose that
\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
and let
t\in G_{1}
be an involution. Then
c_{t}
is the restriction of
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
,
W=[N_{S}(T),\tau]
,
Z(S)\leq W
and
Z(S)
is inverted by
\tau
.

Proof 0.11.22
.

Since
t
is an involution,
tT\in Z(G_{1}/T)
and so
t\in N_{G_{1}}(N_{S}(T))
. Let
\tilde{\tau}
be the element of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(T))
which restricts to
c_{t}
. The maximality of
T
implies
N_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(T))\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and so
\tilde{\tau}
is the restriction of a morphism
\tau
in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Since
t
centralizes
N_{S}(T)/T
,
[T,t]\leq[T,O^{p^{\prime}}(G_{1})]=W
and
t
inverts
W
, we have
W=[N_{S}(T),t]=[N_{S}(T),\tau]
. If
Z(S)\not\leq W
then, as
Z(S)\leq T
,
V=Z(S)W
is normalized by
B_{1}N_{S}(T)=G_{1}
and is contained in
Z(T)
.
As
W
is the unique non-central
G_{1}
-chief factor in
V
, we get
V=C_{V}(\tau)\times[V,\tau]
and
C_{V}(\tau)=C_{V}(G_{1})
. Hence, as
Z(S)
is
\langle\tau\rangle
-invariant
Z(S)\leq C_{V}(\tau)=C_{V}(G_{1})
. But then
\mathcal{F}_{N_{S}(T)}(G_{1})\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(S))\subseteq\mathcal{G}
by Lemma
0.10.17
. Since this is not the case, we conclude that
Z(S)\leq W
and
Z(S)
is inverted by
\tau
.

Lemma 0.11.23
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. Then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and
S
is not exceptional. In particular, Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds.

Proof 0.11.24
.

Assume that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not
\mathcal{F}
-essential. Then
\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Let
t\in G_{1}
and
\tau
be as in Lemma
0.11.21
. Then

Z(S)\leq[N_{S}(T),\tau]=W.

By Lemma
0.3.3
(iii) and (vi) the group
\gamma_{1}(S)/Z(S)
is the unique
2
-step centralizer in
S/Z(S)
. Hence

[T,Z_{3}(S)]\leq[\gamma_{1}(S),Z_{3}(S)]\leq Z(S).

Since
Z(S)\leq T
, we deduce that
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
and consequently we also have
[Z_{3}(S),\tau]\leq Z_{3}(S)\cap W
.

By Lemma
0.3.10
,
\tau
does not centralize
Z_{3}(S)/Z(S)
. Hence, as
\tau
inverts
Z(S)
, we have
[Z_{3}(S),\tau]\leq[T,\tau]=W
. As
|S|\geq p^{7}
by Lemma
0.11.1
(iii),
Z_{3}(S)
is abelian and so
Z_{3}(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(W)=T
. Furthermore,
Z_{5}(S)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
and it follows that
Z_{5}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
. Therefore

[Z_{5}(S),\tau]\leq[N_{S}(T),\tau]\leq W\leq Z_{3}(S)

and this contradicts Lemma
0.3.10
. Hence
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential. Lemma
0.11.1
(vii) yields
S
is not exceptional.

Lemma 0.11.25
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds. Then the following hold:

(i)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
;

(ii)

\gamma_{1}(S)=N_{S}(T)
; and

(iii)

\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has class
2
and order
p^{p-1}
.

Proof 0.11.26
.

By Lemma
0.11.23
, we have Hypothesis
0.8.1
is satisfied. Part (i) is just Lemma
0.11.1
(vii). The group
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not abelian by Lemma
0.11.1
(iv).
From Lemma
0.8.5
we obtain
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has order
p^{p-1}
and nilpotency class
2
and

[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))).

Hence, using Lemma
0.11.11
,

[\Omega_{1}(T),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq\Omega_{1}(T).

In particular,
\gamma_{1}(S)
normalizes
\Omega_{1}(T)
. Therefore, as
N_{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega_{1}(T))\supseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, the maximal choice of
N_{S}(T)
implies that
N_{S}(T)\geq\gamma_{1}(S)
. As
T
is not normal in
S
by Lemma
0.11.7
, we now have
N_{S}(T)=\gamma_{1}(S)
which is part (ii).

By Lemma
0.11.19
,
G_{1}/T\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and so
T
is a maximal subgroup of
N_{S}(T)
. As
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\not\leq T
by Lemma
0.11.11
, we have
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))T
. Hence Lemmas
0.2.2
and
0.3.2
(ii) imply
\mho^{1}(T)=\mho^{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. If
\mho^{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\neq 1
, then
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\leq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\mho^{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and this contradicts the choice of
T
. Therefore
\mho^{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))=1
and we conclude
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Thus part (iii) holds.

Proposition 7
.

If Hypothesis
0.10.4
holds, then
T
is not
S
-centric.

Proof 0.11.27
.

Suppose that
T
is
S
-centric. Then Hypothesis
0.11.8
holds.
From Lemma
0.11.23
we know Hypothesis
0.8.1
holds.  Lemma
0.11.25
implies that
N_{S}(T)=\gamma_{1}(S)
has exponent
p
, order
p^{p-1}
and nilpotency class
2
. We also know that
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
. Let
L=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))^{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))}\rangle
be the preimage of
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
.

As in Lemma
0.11.23
, take
\theta\in N_{G_{1}}(N_{S}(T))
of order
p-1
and let
\rho
the element of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(T))=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
which restricts to
\theta
.
Set
H=\langle\rho\rangle
. Then, as
H
centralizes
T/W
, we have
|[N_{S}(T),H]|=|[\gamma_{1}(S),H]|=p^{3}
.
Since
H\cap L
has index at most
2
in
H
, we have
|(H\cap L)\mathrm{Inn}(\gamma_{1}(S))/\mathrm{Inn}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=(p-1)/2
and subgroups of this order normalize a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
L
. We calculate, using
|\gamma_{1}(S)|=p^{p-1}
, that

|C_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(\rho^{2})|=|\gamma_{1}(S)/[\gamma_{1}(S),\rho^{2}]|=p^{p-4}.

However, Lemma
0.8.5
states that
|C_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(\rho^{2})|=p^{2}
and so we conclude that
p<7
. This contradicts Lemma
0.11.1
(iii) and proves that
T
is not
\mathcal{F}
-centric.

0.11.2.
The case
T
is not
S
-centric

In this subsection, we continue the notation so far established and, in addition, assume that

Hypothesis 0.11.27
.

Hypothesis
0.10.4
holds and adopting Notation
0.11.2
we have
C_{S}(T)\not\leq T
.

Lemma 0.11.28
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
\mathcal{E}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof 0.11.29
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}=\emptyset
. Then, because
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
is saturated by Lemma
0.11.3
,
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(T))
. Hence
N_{\mathcal{F}}(N_{S}(T))\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and, as
T\neq S
, this contradicts the maximal choice of
T
. We conclude
\mathcal{E}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}\neq\emptyset
.

Since
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
is saturated and
\mathcal{E}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}\neq\emptyset
, the Alperin-Goldschmidt fusion theorem implies there is an
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-essential subgroup
P
such that
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
.

Notation 0.11.29
.

The subgroup
P\leq N_{S}(T)
is an
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-essential subgroup of maximal order such that
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 0.11.30
.

If Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds, then

(i)

T<P
;

(ii)

N_{S}(P)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
;

(iii)

|N_{S}(P)|<|N_{S}(T)|
; and

(iv)

P<N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)<N_{S}(T)
.

Proof 0.11.31
.

By Lemma
0.5.10
,
T\leq O_{p}({N}_{\mathcal{F}}(T))\leq P
. Since
T
is not
S
-centric,
T
is not
N_{S}(T)
-centric and so
T<P
. This is (i).

Suppose that
P\not\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
. Then Lemma
0.11.1
(i) implies that
P
is not contained in any
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups. Therefore the elements of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
are all restrictions of elements in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
and this means that
O_{p}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P))=\mathrm{Out}_{S}(P)
. In particular,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
has a unique Sylow
p
-subgroup. As
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
, we have
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
has a unique Sylow
p
-subgroup and this contradicts
P
being
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-essential. Hence
P\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
. Since
Z(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
, we have
Z(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(P)\leq P
and so, either,
P
is normal in
S
or
N_{S}(P)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
by Lemma
0.3.7
. As
T
is not normal in
S
by Lemma
0.11.7
, the maximal choice of
T
implies
N_{S}(P)\neq S
. Hence
N_{S}(P)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
. This proves (ii). If
|N_{S}(P)|\geq|N_{S}(T)|
then as
P>T
, we have a contradiction to the maximal choice of
T
. Thus
|N_{S}(P)|<|N_{S}(T)|
so (iii) holds. Part (iii) yields
P<N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)<N_{S}(T)
which is (iv).

Recall from Section
0.5
, that the subgroup
H_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
of
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
is generated by those
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-automorphisms of
P
which extend to
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-isomorphisms between strictly larger subgroups of
N_{S}(T)
.

Lemma 0.11.32
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds.
Let
A,B\leq N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
with
P\leq A\cap B
. Then

\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(A,B)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and

H_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)=N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P))\subseteq\mathcal{G}.

Proof 0.11.33
.

From the maximal choice of
P
, we know that any
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-essential subgroup
P_{1}
containing
A
has
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P_{1})\subset\mathcal{G}
. It follows that
\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(A,B)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
. In particular, the elements of
\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(A,B)
are restrictions of morphisms in
N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. If
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(A,B)
and
P=P\alpha
, then taking any
\hat{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
which restricts to
\alpha
, we have
P\hat{\alpha}=P
,
T=T\hat{\alpha}
and, because of Lemma
0.11.30
,

N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)\hat{\alpha}=N_{N_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(T)}(P)\hat{\alpha}=N_{N_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(T)}(P)=N_{N_{S}(T)}(P).

Hence
H_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)=N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P))
, as stated.

Lemma 0.11.34
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
C_{S}(T)\not\leq P
. In particular,
\mathrm{Out}_{C_{S}(T)}(P)\neq 1
.

Proof 0.11.35
.

If
C_{S}(T)\leq P
, then
C_{S}(T)T=C_{P}(T)T
is normalized by
N_{S}(T)
and is invariant under the
action of
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
. Thus the maximal choice of
T
yields
C_{S}(T)T=T
. Hence
C_{S}(T)\leq T
, a contradiction. Therefore
C_{S}(T)\not\leq P
. Since
PC_{S}(T)
is a subgroup of
S
, we have
N_{C_{S}(T)}(P)\not\leq P
by Lemma
0.2.6
and so
\mathrm{Out}_{C_{S}(T)}(P)\neq 1
.

Lemma 0.11.36
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(S))\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and
Z(S)\not\leq T
. In particular,
\Omega_{1}(P)\not\leq T
.

Proof 0.11.37
.

By Lemma
0.10.17
,
{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(S))\subseteq\mathcal{G}
.

As
T
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
-invariant, the group
K=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{S}(T)}(P)^{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}\rangle
centralizes
T
.
Aiming for a contradiction, suppose
Z(S)\leq T
. Then
K
centralizes
Z(S)
and so

K\mathrm{Inn}(P)\subseteq{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(S))\subseteq\mathcal{G}.

Since
C_{S}(T)\not\leq P
by Lemma
0.11.34
,
K\not\leq\mathrm{Inn}(P)
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)=KH_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
by the Frattini Argument. Hence we get
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
from Lemma
0.11.32
, a contradiction. Thus
Z(S)\not\leq T
. Since
Z(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(P)\leq P
, we have
Z(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
and so
\Omega_{1}(P)\not\leq T
.

Lemma 0.11.38
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
.

Proof 0.11.39
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{G}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and Lemma
0.11.1
(vii) says that
S
is not exceptional. Also Lemmas
0.3.7
and
0.11.5
imply
N_{S}(T)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
. As
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is not abelian by Lemma
0.10.15
, Lemma
0.8.5
implies that
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has nilpotency class
2
and order
p^{p-1}
with
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))=Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))=[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]
. In particular,
Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq N_{S}(T)
and
Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(P)\leq P
. Thus

[\Omega_{1}(P),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq[\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)]=Z(\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\leq\Omega_{1}(P)

and so
\Omega_{1}(P)
is normal in
\gamma_{1}(S)
. In particular, as
N_{S}(T)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
,
N_{S}(T)\leq N_{S}(T\Omega_{1}(P))
. By Lemma
0.5.6
we have
N_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\subseteq N_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(T\Omega_{1}(P))
and so, as
N_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
,
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T\Omega_{1}(P))\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
. The maximal choice of
T
now implies that
T=T\Omega_{1}(P)
. Therefore
\Omega_{1}(P)\leq T
, and this contradicts Lemma
0.11.36
.

Notation 0.11.39
.

Define

L=O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P))=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)^{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}\rangle.

Then the Frattini Argument yields

\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)=LN_{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)).

Lemma 0.11.40
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
Z(S)
is not
L
-invariant.

Proof 0.11.41
.

Suppose that
Z(S)
is normalized by
L
. Then, as
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)=LN_{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P))
and
N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P))\subseteq\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
by Lemmas
0.11.32
and
0.11.38
, we deduce from Lemma
0.11.36
that
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\subseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(Z(S))\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, which is a contradiction.

The next three lemmas limit the structure of
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
.

Lemma 0.11.42
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P))
is centralized by
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
. In particular,
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
centralizes
O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P))
.

Proof 0.11.43
.

Suppose false. Then there exists an
x\in\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
such that
x\mathrm{Inn}(P)
does not centralize
O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P))
. Let
K\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
be the preimage of
[O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)),x\mathrm{Inn}(P)]
. Then
K\langle x\rangle
is
p
-soluble and, since
p
is odd, there exists a non-central
K\langle x\rangle
-chief factor
V
in
\Omega_{1}(P)
by
[
24
, Theorem 5.3.10]
. By Proposition
0.2.5
,
|V|\geq p^{p-1}
. Since
\Omega_{1}(P)<\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and
1<\Omega_{1}(T)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
is
L
-invariant, Lemma
0.3.2
(iv) implies
\Omega_{1}(P)=\Omega_{1}(T)\leq T
and this contradicts Lemma
0.11.36
.

Lemma 0.11.44
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
is cyclic.

Proof 0.11.45
.

If
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
is not cyclic, then, as
p
is odd,
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
has an elementary abelian subgroup of order
p^{2}
. Using Lemma
0.2.10
, we obtain
K=\mathrm{Out}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)/O_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P))
is an almost simple group. Because
p\geq 7
by Lemma
0.11.1
(iii), using Proposition
0.2.12
we obtain that
K\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{a})
with
a\geq 2
,
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(p^{a})
with
a\geq 1
,
\mathrm{Alt}(2p)
or
p=11
and
L\cong\mathrm{J}_{4}
. Since
H_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\subset\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
by Lemmas
0.11.32
and
0.11.38
, we get
N_{L}(\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P))
is cyclic of order dividing
p-1
from Lemma
0.11.1
(vi).
Using
[
26
, Theorem 7.6.2]
, we find this is not compatible with any of the candidates for
O^{p}(L/\mathrm{Inn}(P))/Z(L/\mathrm{Inn}(P))
. Thus
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
is cyclic.

Lemma 0.11.46
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
.

Proof 0.11.47
.

Since
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
centralizes
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P))
by Lemma
0.11.42
and
O_{p}(L)=\mathrm{Inn}(P)
,
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
centralizes the Fitting subgroup of
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
and so
E(\mathrm{Out}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P))\neq 1
and, by Lemma
0.11.44
, there is a unique component
K/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
of
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)
which has order divisible by
p
. Since
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)\leq L
,
K
is a normal subgroup of
L
and
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)=H_{\mathcal{F}}(P)K
. Since
H_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
leaves
Z(S)
invariant, and
L\leq H_{\mathcal{F}}(P)K
,
K
does not leave
Z(S)
invariant by Lemma
0.11.40
.

Assume that
K/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
does not have a quotient isomorphic to
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
. Then
K
is not of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type and
K/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
is quasisimple. Hence, as
Z(S)
is not
K
-invariant, setting
U=\langle Z(S)^{K}\rangle\leq\Omega_{1}(Z(P))
,
U
has a non-central
K
-chief factor. Since
U
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)K
-invariant, Theorem
0.4.2
yields
|U|\geq p^{2(p-1)/3}
,
|\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)|=p
and
U
is indecomposable as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
-module. In particular,
[U,\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P);\lceil 2(p-1)/3\rceil-1]\neq 1
. This with Lemma
0.3.5
gives
\lceil 2(p-1)/3\rceil-1<(p+1)/2
and yields
p=7
. But then, as
p=7
, Lemma
0.3.5
additionally
tells us that
S
is exceptional. As
|S|\geq 7^{7}
by Lemma
0.11.1
(iii), Lemma
0.3.3
(v) implies
|S|=7^{8}
. Since
T\leq P
and
U\not\leq T
, we also have
|P|\geq 7^{5}
.
By Lemma
0.11.30
,
P<N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)<N_{S}(T)
. Hence
N_{S}(T)
is a maximal subgroup of
S
. Since
S
is exceptional, either
Z(N_{S}(T))=Z(S)
or
N_{S}(T)=C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
. In the former case, as
T
is normal in
N_{S}(T)
, we have
Z(S)\leq T
which contradicts Lemma
0.11.36
. Hence
N_{S}(T)=C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
and consequently
Z_{2}(S)\leq P
. Since
P\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
by Lemma
0.11.30
,
P\leq\gamma_{1}(S)\cap C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))=\gamma_{2}(S)
. Now
[\gamma_{2}(S),\gamma_{2}(S)]\leq\gamma_{5}(S)=Z_{2}(S)\leq P
and so
\gamma_{2}(S)=N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
and
\gamma_{2}(S)
acts quadratically on
P
contrary to
[U,\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P);\lceil 2(p-1)/3\rceil-1]\neq 1
. We conclude that
K/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
is of
\mathrm{L}_{2}(p)
-type and it follows that
K/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. Since
O_{p}(L)=\mathrm{Inn}(P)
and
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
is cyclic, we further deduce that
L=K
.

By Lemma
0.11.46
,
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
has order
p
. Hence Lemma
0.11.34
implies

\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{S}(T)}(P)\mathrm{Inn}(P)=\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P).

We now establish some notation which will play an important role in the remaining lemmas of this section. Let
\langle\theta\rangle
be a complement in
L
to
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)=\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{S}(T)}(P)
chosen so that
\langle\theta\rangle\leq\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{S}(T)}(P)^{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}\rangle\leq C_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
. We know
\theta
has order
(p-1)/2
when
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
and order
p-1
when
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. Since
\theta\in\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{S}(T)}(P)^{\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)}\rangle
, we also know
\theta
centralizes
T
.

Because
\theta
normalizes
\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
,
\theta
is the restriction of a morphism

\tilde{\theta}\in\mathrm{Aut}_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(N_{N_{S}(T)}(P))\subseteq H_{N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{G}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)

by Lemmas
0.11.32
and
0.11.38
. Hence
\theta
is in fact the restriction of an element
\hat{\theta}
of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
and we may assume that
\hat{\theta}
has
p^{\prime}
-order.

If
\theta
has order
p-1
, then Lemma
0.11.1
(vi) shows that
\hat{\theta}
has order
p-1
and so
\hat{\tau}=\hat{\theta}^{(p-1)/2}
is an involution which restricts to
\tau=\theta^{(p-1)/2}
. Recall that the action for
\hat{\theta}
on
\gamma_{1}(S)
is the subject of Lemmas
0.3.10
and
0.3.14
.

Lemma 0.11.48
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
Z(S)\not\leq C_{\Omega_{1}(P)}(\theta)
.

Proof 0.11.49
.

Suppose that
Z(S)
is centralized by
\theta
and define
V=\langle Z(S)^{L}\rangle\Omega_{1}(T)
. Since
Z(S)\not\leq C_{V}(O^{p}(L))
by Lemma
0.11.40
, we can select
U
maximal such that

1\neq\Omega_{1}(T)\leq C_{V}(O^{p}(L))\leq U<V

is
L
-invariant. Then
V/U
is irreducible as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)L
-module and, by the definition of
V
,
Z(S)\not\leq U
. Set
\overline{V}=V/C_{V}(O^{p}(L))
. Then
|\overline{V}|<p^{p}
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iv). Combining Lemma
0.11.46
and Corollary
0.4.5
yields
V=Z(S)U
and
\overline{V}=\overline{Z(S)}\overline{U}>\overline{U}
. Lemma
0.2.7
implies
\overline{V}=C_{\overline{V}}(L)\overline{U}
. By coprime action,
C_{\overline{V}}(L)\leq C_{\overline{V}}(O^{p}(L))=1
. Hence
\overline{V}=\overline{U}
, a contradiction. Therefore
Z(S)
is not centralized by
\theta
.

Lemma 0.11.50
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. If
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
is abelian, then
Z_{2}(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T)))
. In particular,
Z_{2}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
.

Proof 0.11.51
.

If
S
is not exceptional, then, as
T
is not normal in
S
by Lemma
0.11.7
, Lemma
0.3.7
implies
N_{S}(T)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
. The claim then follows as

Z_{2}(S)\leq Z(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq Z(N_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(T))=Z(N_{S}(T)).

Suppose that
S
is exceptional. Then
Z(\gamma_{1}(S))=Z(S)
. If
\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
, then

Z_{2}(S)\leq C_{S}(\Omega_{1}(T))\leq N_{S}(\Omega_{1}(T))=N_{S}(T)

by Lemma
0.11.5
. Thus
Z_{2}(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T)))
in this case.

So assume that
\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))\not\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
. Then
N_{S}(T)\not\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
and, as
T
is not normal in
S
by Lemma
0.11.7
, Lemma
0.3.7
implies
N_{S}(T)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
. Since
\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))\not\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\not\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
and, as
S
has maximal class, we deduce
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
which has exponent
p
by Lemmas
0.3.2
(ii) and
0.2.1
(ii). In particular,
P=\Omega_{1}(P)
and
T=\Omega_{1}(T)
.
By Lemmas
0.11.30
and
0.11.46
,
N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)<N_{S}(T)
and
N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)/P
has order
p
. Therefore we can pick
x\in N_{N_{S}(T)}(N_{N_{S}(T)}(P))\setminus N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
such that
N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)=PP^{x}
and
P\cap P^{x}
has index
p
in
P
. Using Lemma
0.11.46
we can find
\ell\in L
such that
L=\langle\mathrm{Inn}(P),\mathrm{Aut}_{P^{x}}(P),\mathrm{Aut}_{P^{x}}(P)^{\ell}\rangle
. Then, as
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)=P/T
is abelian,
O^{p}(L)
centralizes
(P\cap P^{x}\cap P^{x\ell})/T
and centralizes
T
and so coprime action implies
|P/C_{P}(O^{p}(L))|\leq p^{2}
. It follows that
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
, and
\theta
has order
p-1
. By Lemmas
0.3.3
(vi) and
0.3.14
,
|C_{\gamma_{1}(S)/Z(S)}(\hat{\theta})|\leq p
. As
Z(S)
is not
L
-invariant by Lemma
0.11.40
, it follows that
|C_{P}(O^{p}(L))|\leq p
and so
T=C_{P}(O^{p}(L))
has order
p
and
|P|=p^{3}
. In particular, we have
|N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)|=p^{4}
and
P/T
is an
(N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)/T)
-pearl.

Since
p^{8}\leq|S|\leq p^{p+1}
by Lemmas
0.3.3
(v) and
0.11.1
(iii) and
\gamma_{1}(S)
has exponent
p
, we have
Z_{4}(S)
is elementary abelian. We establish some notation for the action of
\hat{\theta}
which we know has order
p-1
. So let
x\in C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\setminus\gamma_{1}(S)
and
s_{1}\in\gamma_{1}(S)\setminus\gamma_{2}(S)
. Then we may suppose that

\displaystyle x\hat{\theta}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle x^{a}\mod\gamma_{2}(S)

\displaystyle s_{1}\hat{\theta}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle s_{1}^{b}\mod\gamma_{2}(S)

where
a,b\in\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}
. We also know that
\hat{\theta}
centralizes
T
. By Lemma
0.3.10
,

s_{n-1}\hat{\theta}=s_{n-1}^{c}

where
c={a^{n-3}b^{2}}
and, as
P/T
is a natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module for
y\in P/Z(S)T
we have

y\hat{\theta}\equiv y^{c^{-1}}\mod Z(S)T.

Set
V=Z_{4}(S)/Z(S)
. Then, by Lemma
0.3.10
, we may select elements
v_{n-4},v_{n-3}
and
v_{n-2}
of
V
such that
v_{j}\hat{\theta}=v_{j}^{a^{j-1}b}
. It particular, the action of
\hat{\theta}
on each of these elements is different and they correspond to eigenvectors of
\hat{\theta}|_{V}
on
V
with different eigenvalues. Pick
t\in T^{\#}
, then

\hat{\theta}|_{V}
and
c_{t}|_{V}
commute, and so
T
normalizes the eigenspaces of
\theta
on
V
. Since the
\theta
-eigenspaces on
V
have order
p
, we conclude that
V=C_{V}(T)
. This shows that
[Z_{4}(S),T]\leq Z(S)
and so
|C_{Z_{4}(S)}(T)|\geq p^{3}
.

Suppose that
[P,Z_{4}(S)]\leq Z(S)
. Then
C_{Z_{4}(S)}(T)\leq N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
as
Z(S)\leq P
. Since
|N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)|=p^{4}
and
N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
is non-abelian,
TC_{Z_{4}(S)}(T)
has order
p^{3}
. Hence
T\leq Z_{4}(S)
. But then
Z_{4}(S)=N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
, a contradiction as
Z_{4}(S)
is abelian. We have proved that

[P,Z_{4}(S)]\not\leq Z(S).

In particular, as
S/Z(S)
has positive degree of commutativity,
P\not\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
.

Suppose that
T\not\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
. Then
\langle T^{S}\rangle=\gamma_{1}(S)
and so, as
[Z_{4}(S),T]\leq Z(S)
, we have

\displaystyle[Z_{4}(S),P]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle[Z_{4}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]=[Z_{4}(S),\langle T^{S}\rangle]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle[Z_{4}(S),T]^{S}\rangle\leq Z(S),

which is a contradiction. Hence
T\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
. Since
Z(S)\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
and
P\not\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
, for
y\in P\setminus Z(S)T
, we have
y\not\in\gamma_{2}(S)
. Hence

y\hat{\theta}\equiv y^{b}\mod\gamma_{2}(S)

and

y\hat{\theta}\equiv y^{c^{-1}}\mod\gamma_{2}(S).

Therefore
c=b^{-1}
.
Suppose that
T\not\leq\gamma_{3}(S)
, then as
\hat{\theta}
centralizes
T
, we have
ab=1
by Lemma
0.3.10
. Now applying Lemma
0.3.10
again yields

s_{3}\hat{\theta}\equiv s_{3}^{b^{-1}}\mod\gamma_{3}(S)=s_{3}^{c}\mod\gamma_{3}(S)

and also

s_{n-1}\hat{\theta}=s_{n-1}^{b^{-1}}=s_{n-1}^{c}.

Lemma
0.3.14
yields
p-1
divides
n-1-3
. Hence
n\geq p+3
and this contradicts
n\leq p+1
.
Therefore
T\leq\gamma_{3}(S)
.

Since
[Z_{3}(S),\gamma_{3}(S)]=1
, we now have
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
.
As
P\not\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
,
P
does not centralize
Z_{3}(S)
. Hence
PZ_{3}(S)=N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
and
Z_{3}(S)\cap P=Z(N_{N_{S}(T)}(P))=Z(S)T.
In particular,
T\leq Z_{3}(S)
and
Z_{5}(S)\leq\gamma_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
. Now
|Z_{5}(S)/Z_{3}(S)|=p^{2}
and is centralized by
N_{S}(T)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
. Therefore
N_{S}(T)/T
does not have maximal class. However,
P/T
is an
(N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)/T)
-pearl and consequently Lemma
0.5.18
(i) implies that
N_{S}(T)/T
does have maximal class. We have derived a contradiction and this proves the lemma.

Lemma 0.11.52
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then we have
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\not\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
.

Proof 0.11.53
.

Suppose that
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
.
We start by considering the case
|C_{\Omega_{1}(P)}(\theta)|\leq p^{2}
. In this case, as
\theta
centralizes
\Omega_{1}(T)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
,
|C_{\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)}(\theta)|\leq p
. Since
Z(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
and
O^{p}(L)
does not centralize
Z(S)
by Lemma
0.11.40
,
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
contains at least one non-central
L
-chief factor contained in
\langle Z(S)^{L}\rangle
.   As
|\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)|<p^{p}
, Lemma
0.4.5
implies  each
L
-chief factor in
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
contributes
p
to
|C_{\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)}(\theta)|
and therefore
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
is a non-central
L
-chief factor. In particular, Lemma
0.4.5
implies
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
has order
p^{a}
with
a
odd in the range
3\leq a\leq p-2
,
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)}(P)
acts indecomposably on
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
and, as
|C_{\Omega_{1}(P)}(\theta)|\leq p^{2}
,
|\Omega_{1}(T)|=p
.

We will frequently use

[\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T),N_{N_{S}(T)}(P),N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)]\neq 1

which is a consequence of
a\geq 3
and
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)}(P)
acts indecomposably on
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
.
We also remark that

\Omega_{1}(T)\langle Z(S)^{L}\rangle\leq Z(\Omega_{1}(P))\leq\Omega_{1}(P)

and, as
\langle Z(S)^{L}\rangle
has a non-central
L
-chief factor, we obtain
\Omega_{1}(P)=Z(\Omega_{1}(P))
is abelian.

By Lemma
0.11.50
,
Z_{2}(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T)))\leq N_{S}(T)
.
Since
[P,Z_{2}(S)]\leq Z(S)\leq P
,
Z_{2}(S)\leq N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
. If
Z_{2}(S)\not\leq P
, then, as
|N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)/P|=p
,
N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)=PZ_{2}(S)
and we obtain

[P,N_{N_{S}(T)}(P),N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)]=[P,Z_{2}(S),Z_{2}(S)]\leq[Z(S),Z_{2}(S)]=1,

which is a contradiction. Thus
Z_{2}(S)\leq P
and so
Z_{2}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
.

Since
Z_{2}(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T)))
,
Z_{2}(S)
is centralized by
N_{\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))}(P)
. Assume that
N_{\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))}(P)\leq P
. Then, by Lemma
0.2.6
,
\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))\leq P
and so
\Omega_{1}(P)=\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))
. But then
\Omega_{1}(P)T=\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))T
,
N_{\mathcal{F}}(\Omega_{1}(P)T)\supseteq N_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\not\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and
N_{S}(\Omega_{1}(P)T)\geq N_{S}(T)
. The maximal choice of
T
implies
T\geq\Omega_{1}(P)\geq Z(S)
, and this contradicts Lemma
0.11.36
.
Hence
N_{\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))}(P)\not\leq P
and
N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)=PN_{\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))}(P)
. As
N_{\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T))}(P)
centralizes
Z_{2}(S)
and
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
acts indecomposably on
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
, we deduce that

Z_{2}(S)\Omega_{1}(T)/\Omega_{1}(T)\leq C_{\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)}(N_{N_{S}(T)}(P))=Z(S)\Omega_{1}(T)/\Omega_{1}(T).

Hence, as
\Omega_{1}(T)
has order
p
, we have
\Omega_{1}(T)\leq Z_{2}(S)
. In particular,
\gamma_{2}(S)
normalizes
T
and
Z_{2}(S)=Z(S)\Omega_{1}(T)\leq P
.

Since
|S|\geq p^{7}
by Lemma
0.11.1
(iii),
Z_{4}(S)\leq\gamma_{2}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
. Hence
[P,Z_{4}(S)]\leq Z_{2}(S)\leq P
implies
Z_{4}(S)\leq N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
. In addition

[P,Z_{4}(S),Z_{4}(S)]\leq[\gamma_{1}(S),Z_{4}(S),Z_{4}(S)]\leq[Z_{2}(S),Z_{4}(S)]=1.

Since
N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
does not act quadratically on
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
, we have
Z_{4}(S)\leq P
. Since
Z_{4}(S)
has exponent
p
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iii) and (vi),
Z_{4}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
. Now

\displaystyle[Z_{4}(S)/\Omega_{1}(T),N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle[Z_{4}(S)/\Omega_{1}(T),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq Z_{2}(S)/\Omega_{1}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z(S)\Omega_{1}(T)/\Omega_{1}(T)

and, as
|Z_{4}(S)/Z(S)\Omega_{1}(T)|=p^{2}
, we have a contradiction to the indecomposable action of
\mathrm{Out}_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
on
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
. This contradiction shows that
|C_{\Omega_{1}(P)}(\theta)|>p^{2}
.

By Lemma
0.11.48
the morphism
\theta
does not centralize
{Z}(S)
. Hence

|C_{\Omega_{1}(P/Z(S))}(\theta)|>p^{2}.

Consider the group
S/Z(S)
and note that
S
is not exceptional. Since
\hat{\theta}
has order divisible by
(p-1)/2
, and
|C_{\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S/Z(S)))}(\hat{\theta})|\geq|C_{\Omega_{1}(P/Z(S))}(\theta)|>p^{2}
, Lemma
0.3.14
implies
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S/Z(S)))|=p^{p}
. By Lemma
0.3.2
(iv) we get
|S/Z(S)|=p^{p+1}
, and so
|S|=p^{p+2}
and
\mho^{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))\leq{Z}(S)=\gamma_{p+1}(S)
. However this contradicts Lemma
0.3.2
(iii) which states that
\mho^{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))=\gamma_{p}(S)>Z(S)
.

Lemma 0.11.54
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Then
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\not\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
.

Proof 0.11.55
.

Suppose that
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. Then,
\hat{\theta}
has order
p-1
and acts faithfully on
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
.
If
|C_{\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))}(\theta)|\geq p^{2}
, then Lemma
0.3.14
applied to
S/Z(S)
implies that
Z(S)
is centralized by
\theta
. This is impossible by Lemma
0.11.48
. Hence
|C_{\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))}(\theta)|\leq p
.
Applying Lemma
0.4.5
(i) and (ii), delivers all the non-central
L
-chief factors in
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
are faithful
\mathrm{GF}(p)L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)
-modules. In particular,
\tau
inverts
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
and so
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
is abelian and
\Omega_{1}(T)
has order
p
.

Suppose that
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
. Then, as
[Z_{3}(S),P]\leq Z(S)
, we obtain
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
.

Assume that
Z_{3}(S)\not\leq P
. Then
[P,Z_{3}(S)]\leq[Z_{3}(S),\gamma_{1}(S)]\leq Z(S)
. Hence
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
has exactly one non-central
L
-chief factor and
|P/\Omega_{1}(T)|=p^{2}
by Lemma
0.4.4
. Hence
|Z_{3}(S)\cap P|=p^{2}
. If
\Omega_{1}(T)\not\leq Z_{3}(S)
, then
P=\Omega_{1}(T)(P\cap Z_{3}(S))
and this means that
Z_{3}(S)
centralizes
P
. Since
P
is
N_{\mathcal{F}}(T)
-centric, this is impossible. Thus
Z_{3}(S)P/P\cong Z_{3}(S)/(Z_{3}(S)\cap P)
and
\Omega_{1}(T)
are both centralized by
\tau
. As
Z(S)\leq P
and
Z(S)\cap\Omega_{1}(T)=1
, we have
Z_{3}(S)/Z(S)
is centralized by
\tau
. This is impossible by Lemma
0.3.14
.
Hence
Z_{3}(S)\leq P
and so
Z_{3}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iii) and (vi). Now Lemma
0.3.14
implies
1\neq C_{Z_{3}(S)}(\tau)\leq C_{\Omega_{1}(P)}(\tau)=\Omega_{1}(T)
. As
\Omega_{1}(T)
has order
p
, this means that
\Omega_{1}(T)\leq Z_{3}(S)
.

We conclude that, if
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
, then
\Omega_{1}(T)\leq Z_{3}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
and, in particular,
N_{S}(T)\geq\gamma_{3}(S)
.

Continue to assume that
Z_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
. Then
Z_{4}(S)\leq\gamma_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
by Lemma
0.11.1
(iii). Since
Z_{3}(S)\leq P
,
Z_{4}(S)\leq N_{N_{S}(T)}(P)
.
Suppose that
Z_{4}(S)\not\leq P
. Then
\tau
centralizes
Z_{4}(S)P/P
and so
\tau
centralizes
Z_{4}(S)/Z_{3}(S)
. Lemma
0.3.10
implies
\tau
inverts
Z_{3}(S)/Z_{2}(S)
and so
\Omega_{1}(T)\leq Z_{2}(S)
. Since
[P,Z_{4}(S)]\leq Z_{2}(S)
,
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
has order
p^{2}
by Lemma
0.4.4
. Hence
\Omega_{1}(P)=Z_{3}(S)
and we then have
[\Omega_{1}(P),Z_{4}(S)]\leq[Z_{3}(S),\gamma_{3}(S)]=1
, a contradiction. Therefore
Z_{4}(S)\leq P
. Since
Z_{4}(S)
has exponent
p
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iii) and (vi),
Z_{4}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
and
Z_{4}(S)/\Omega_{1}(T)
is inverted by
\tau
. Lemma
0.3.10
yields
\Omega_{1}(T)^{\#}\subseteq Z_{3}(S)\setminus Z_{2}(S)
and
Z_{2}(S)
is inverted by
\tau
. In particular,
S
is exceptional and so
|S|\geq p^{8}
,
\gamma_{2}(S)
has exponent
p
by Lemma
0.3.2
(vi) and
0.3.3
(v).
Therefore
Z_{5}(S)\leq\gamma_{3}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
and
[P,Z_{5}(S)]\leq Z_{3}(S)\leq P
.
If
Z_{5}(S)\leq P
, then
Z_{5}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
and
C_{Z_{5}(S)}(\tau)=C_{\Omega_{1}(P)}(\tau)=\Omega_{1}(T)
has order
p
. This contradicts Lemma
0.3.10
. Hence
Z_{5}(S)\not\leq P
,
Z_{4}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
and, in particular,
|\Omega_{1}(P)|\geq p^{4}
. As

[P,Z_{5}(S),Z_{5}(S)]\leq[Z_{3}(S),Z_{5}(S)]=1

and
Z_{3}(S)/Z(S)
has order
p^{2}
, we have that
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
has two
L
-chief factors and they are both natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)L/P
-modules (of order
p^{2}
) by Lemma
0.4.4
. Hence
|P|=p^{5}
and
|P:C_{P}(Z_{5}(S))|\geq p^{2}
. If
Z_{5}(S)
is abelian, then
Z_{4}(S)\leq C_{P}(Z_{5}(S))
and
|P:C_{P}(Z_{5}(S))|\leq p
, a contradiction. Hence
Z_{5}(S)
is non-abelian and consequently
|S|=p^{8}
. Since
[Z_{4}(S),\Omega_{1}(P)]\leq\Omega_{1}(P)^{\prime}\cap Z_{2}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(T)\cap Z_{2}(S)=1
,
Z_{4}(S)\leq Z(\Omega_{1}(P))
and we deduce that
\Omega_{1}(P)
is abelian. On the other hand,
C_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(Z_{4}(S))=Z_{k}(S)
for some
k\geq 4
. As
Z_{5}(S)
is non-abelian, we have that
C_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(Z_{4}(S))=Z_{4}(S)
which means that
Z_{4}(S)<P\leq Z_{4}(S)
, a contradiction. We have demonstrated that
Z_{3}(S)
does not normalize
T
.

Assume that
S
is not exceptional. Then, as
Z_{2}(S)\leq Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
,
Z_{2}(S)\leq N_{S}(T)
and so
Z_{2}(S)\leq P
. Since
\tau
inverts
Z_{2}(S)\Omega_{1}(T)/\Omega_{1}(T)
, Lemma
0.3.14
implies that
Z_{2}(S)\geq\Omega_{1}(T)
. Hence
\Omega_{1}(T)
is centralized by
\gamma_{1}(S)
and Lemma
0.11.5
(i) implies
\gamma_{1}(S)
normalizes
T
. Since
Z_{3}(S)\leq\gamma_{1}(S)
, this is impossible. Thus
S
is exceptional.

By Lemma
0.11.50
,
Z_{2}(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(\Omega_{1}(N_{S}(T)))
and so, in particular,
Z_{2}(S)\leq C_{N_{S}(T)}(\Omega_{1}(P))
. Because
L/P
acts faithfully on
\Omega_{1}(P)/\Omega_{1}(T)
, we deduce that
Z_{2}(S)\leq\Omega_{1}(P)
. If
\Omega_{1}(T)\leq Z_{2}(S)
, then
N_{S}(T)\geq\gamma_{2}(S)
and then
Z_{3}(S)
normalizes
T
, a contradiction. Hence
Z_{2}(S)\cong Z_{2}(S)\Omega_{1}(T)/\Omega_{1}(T)
is inverted by
\tau
.

It follows that
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
S/C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
and so

[\gamma_{1}(S),\hat{\tau}]\leq\gamma_{1}(S)\cap C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))=\gamma_{2}(S).

As
T
is not normalized by
Z_{3}(S)
,
\Omega_{1}(T)
is not centralized by
Z_{3}(S)
by Lemma
0.11.5
(i). Hence
\Omega_{1}(T)\not\leq\gamma_{3}(S)
, but
\Omega_{1}(T)
does centralize
Z_{2}(S)
and so
\Omega_{1}(T)\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
. Thus
\gamma_{2}(S)/\gamma_{3}(S)=\Omega_{1}(T)\gamma_{3}(S)/\gamma_{3}(S)
is centralized by
