\hat{\tau}
. Therefore
\hat{\tau}
centralizes
\gamma_{1}(S)/\gamma_{3}(S)
and this finally contradicts Lemma
0.3.10
. We have shown that
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\not\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
.

Proposition 8
.

If Hypothesis
0.10.4
holds. Then
T
is
S
-centric.

Proof 0.11.56
.

If
T
is not
S
-centric, then Hypothesis
0.11.27
holds. Recall the definitions of
P
and
L
from Notation
0.11.29
and Notation
0.11.39
. Then Lemma
0.11.46
yields
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
or
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
, whereas Lemmas
0.11.52
asserts that
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\not\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
and Lemma
0.11.54
states that
L/\mathrm{Inn}(P)\not\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. This is impossible. Thus
T
is
S
-centric.

Proof 0.11.57

(Proof of Theorem
D
)
.

As we remarked at the beginning of Section
0.10
,
Lemmas
0.6.1
and
0.6.2
show that Theorem
D
holds if
p\leq 3
as in this case
S
is not exceptional by Lemma
0.3.3
(v). Hence we may assume that Hypothesis
0.6.3
holds.  Assume Theorem
D
is false. Then Hypothesis
0.10.4
and Notation
0.11.2
hold. Now we obtain a contradiction as Proposition
7
yields
T
is not
S
-centric while Proposition
8
asserts that
T
is
S
-centric. We conclude that the main statement in Theorem
D
is true.
If
S
is exceptional, then
we obtain
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
from Proposition
0.7.2
. This concludes the proof.

0.12.
The saturated fusion systems on exceptional maximal class groups: the proof of Theorem B

The objective of this section is to prove Theorem
B
, which for convenience we repeat below.
Recall that in the introduction we defined
S(p)
as the unique split extension of an extraspecial group of exponent
p
and order
p^{p-2}
by a cyclic group of order
p
which has maximal class
[
41
, Proposition 8.1]
.

Theorem B
.

Suppose that
p\geq 5
,
S
is an exceptional maximal class
p
-group of order at least
p^{4}
and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
. Assume that
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Then one of the following holds.

(i)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial, and, if
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, then one of the following holds:

(a)

S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(p)
and either

(
\alpha
)

\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))
,
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
;

(
\beta
)

p=5
,
1\neq O_{p}(\mathcal{F})\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
,
\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{F}_{S}(5^{3}{}^{.}\mathrm{SL}_{3}(5))
;

(
\gamma
)

p\geq 5
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(p))
;

(
\delta
)

p=5
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
where
G=\operatorname{Ly},\operatorname{HN},\mathrm{Aut}(\operatorname{HN})
or
\mathrm{B}
; or

(
\varepsilon
)

p=7
and either
\mathcal{F}
is exotic (27 examples) or
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\operatorname{M})
.

(b)

p\geq 11
,
S\cong S(p)
,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\not=\emptyset
and, if
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\cong\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}\times\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}
,
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{2}(S)))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and
\gamma_{1}(S)/Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
is the unique
(p-3)
-dimensional irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module.

(ii)

p=5
,
S=\mathrm{SmallGroup(5^{6},661)}
,
O_{5}(\mathcal{F})=C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
is the unique
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroup,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is cyclic of order
4
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)
and
\mathcal{F}
is unique.

In particular, if
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, then
\mathcal{F}=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
and, in addition,
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
in all cases other than parts (i)(a)(
\alpha
), (i)(a)(
\beta
) and part (ii).

Proof 0.12.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a saturated fusion system on
S
and assume that
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
is non-empty and

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)),\gamma_{1}(S)\}

by Theorem
D
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
. Then
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial by Proposition
0.9.1
. From now on, suppose
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\neq\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
. Then by Proposition
0.7.2
either
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial or conclusion (ii) holds. So suppose
\gamma_{1}(S)
is extraspecial and let
R=O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
.

Assume
R\neq 1
. Then
R
is normal in
S
and, for
E\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
,
R\leq E
and
R
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
-invariant by Lemma
0.5.10
. In particular,
\mathcal{F}
has no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls as all the
\mathcal{F}
-pearls are abelian. Since
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\neq\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
by assumption, we deduce that
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Thus Proposition
0.7.2
implies that
|S|=p^{6}
and
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
acts transitively on the maximal subgroups of
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
. As
\gamma_{2}(S)
has exponent
p
by Lemma
0.3.2
(vi) and
\Phi(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))=\gamma_{4}(S)
,
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
has exponent
p
. In particular, there exists an element
x
of order
p
in
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
such that
S=\gamma_{1}(S)\langle x\rangle
. Hence
[
41
, Proposition 8.1]
implies that
S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(p)
. If
|\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}|=1
then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}={C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))}
and we are in case (i)(a)(
\alpha
). If
|\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}|=2
, then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{\gamma_{1}(S),C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\}
. We have
R\leq C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))\cap\gamma_{1}(S)=\gamma_{2}(S)
and, as
R
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))
-invariant and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))
acts irreducibly on
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))/\Phi(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))
, we obtain
R\leq\Phi(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))=\gamma_{3}(S)
.
Since
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))
does not normalize
Z(S)
, we have
R=Z_{2}(S)
or
R=Z_{3}(S)=\gamma_{3}(S)
.
Assume that
R=Z_{2}(S)
. Then
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
normalizes
C_{\gamma_{1}(S)}(Z_{2}(S))=\gamma_{2}(S)
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
acts on
\gamma_{2}(S)/Z_{2}(S)
which has order
p^{2}
. Since
\gamma_{2}(S)/\gamma_{3}(S)
,
Z_{2}(S)/Z(S)
and
Z(S)
are centralized by
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S))
, we have
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
. Now using an element
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
which maps to the involution in
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))
we have
[\gamma_{1}(S),\tau]
is extraspecial of order
p^{3}
and is normalized by
S
. Since
[\gamma_{1}(S),\tau]\neq\gamma_{3}(S)
, this is impossible. Hence
R=\gamma_{3}(S)
and
R
is
\mathcal{F}
-centric. It follows that there is a model
H
for
N_{\mathcal{F}}(R)
. From the structure of
\mathrm{Aut}(R)\cong\mathrm{GL}_{3}(p)
, we deduce that
O^{p^{\prime}}(H)/R\cong\mathrm{SL}_{3}(p)
as
|\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}|\geq 2
. Let
P=C_{O^{p^{\prime}}(H)}(Z(S))
, then
P/\gamma_{1}(S)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and
O^{p^{\prime}}(H)
acts on the natural
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-modules
\gamma_{1}(S)/R
and
R/Z(S)
. Since
S
has maximal class,
\gamma_{1}(S)/Z(S)
is a non-split extension for
P/\gamma_{1}(S)
. Now we may apply
[
19
, Proposition 4.2]
and obtain
p=5
. This, together with the model theorem gives (i)(a)(
\beta
).

Hence we may now suppose that
R=1
.
[
41
, Main Theorem]
implies that either
S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(p)
or
S\cong S(p)
. In the first case,
[
47
, Theorem 1.1]
shows that
\mathcal{F}
is known and it is one of the fusion systems described in (i)(a)(
\gamma
), (
\delta
) and (
\epsilon
). If
S\cong S(p)
, then
\mathcal{F}
is known by
[
41
, Theorem 2]
and corresponds to case (i)(b).

0.13.
The saturated fusion systems on non-exceptional maximal class groups

In this section we will lay the ground work for the proof of Theorem
C
which will be presented in the next section.  So suppose that
p
is an odd prime and
S
is a maximal class
p
-group of order at least
p^{4}
.
Following
[
19
]
, we define

\Delta=\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}\times\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}

which has order
(p-1)^{2}
and, for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the diagonal subgroups

\Delta_{i}=\{(r,r^{i})\mid r\in\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}\}.

Most important here are
\Delta_{0}
and
\Delta_{-1}
. Indeed, these diagonals originate from the existence of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls, as we shall see in Lemma
0.13.4
.
Suppose that
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}(S)
,
x\in S\setminus\gamma_{1}(S)
and
z\in Z(S)^{\#}
. Then there exist
r,s\in\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}
which are independent of
x
and
z
such that

x\alpha\gamma_{1}(S)=x^{r}\gamma_{1}(S)

and

z\alpha=z^{s}.

With this notation established, define the homomorphism

\displaystyle\mu:\mathrm{Aut}(S)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(r,s).

We also use the following definitions from
[
19
]
.

Definition 0.13.1
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime,
G
is a group, and
U\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(G)
. Then

G
is in class
\mathcal{G}_{p}^{\wedge}
if and only if
O_{p}(G)=1
,
|U|=p
, and
|\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(U)|=p-1
.

Definition 0.13.2
.

Suppose that
G\in\mathcal{G}_{p}^{\wedge}
and let
V
be a faithful
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module. Then
G
is
minimally active
on
V
if and only if the matrix representing
u\in U^{\#}
in its action on
V
has one non-trivial Jordan block.

We will need the following consequence of the results of Craven, Oliver and Semeraro
[
19
]
as listed in Appendix
P
.

Proposition 9
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime,
\mathcal{G}
is a reduced fusion system on a
p
-group
S
of maximal class of order at least
p^{4}
with
\gamma_{1}(S)
elementary abelian and
\mathcal{G}
-essential. Assume that
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\cong\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{0}
or
\Delta_{-1}
and
|\gamma_{1}(S)|\leq p^{p-1}
. Then
p\geq 5
,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
,
\gamma_{1}(S)
is irreducible as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-module   and
|\gamma_{1}(S)|=p^{p-2}
.  Furthermore,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{-1}
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{G})
is non-empty.

Proof 0.13.3
.

Set
V=\gamma_{1}(S)
,
G=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(V)
and
G_{0}=F^{*}(G)
. By Theorem
D
we have

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})\cup\{V\}

and since
\mathcal{G}
is reduced,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})\neq\emptyset
. Because
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\cong\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{0}
or
\Delta_{-1}
, we know
Z(G)=1
from the definition of
\Delta_{0}
and
\Delta_{-1}
. Now, as
Z(G)=1
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{0}
or
\Delta_{-1}
, applying Theorem
P.0
we have that
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
and that
\mathcal{G}
is described in lines 3, 4, 29, 30, 33 or 34 of Table
2
. If
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{0}
, then inspection of the consequence of column six of Table
2
as read from Table
1
yields a contradiction to the structure of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\mu
.  If
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{-1}
,   then, again using Tables
1
and
2
,  we obtain cases 3 and 4 and that III from Table
1
holds. Thus
|V|=p^{p-2}
and, additionally,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{G})
.

From here on we assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
and that
S
is not exceptional. By Theorem
D
, the
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups are contained in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
. If
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is empty, then
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and, if
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is non-empty, then
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})\leq Z(S)
. Hence
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
if and only if
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is non-empty.

Lemma 0.13.4
.

Assume that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian and
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
.

(i)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is a Hall
p^{\prime}
-subgroup of
\mathrm{Out}(S)
, is cyclic of order
p-1
and acts faithfully on
S/\gamma_{1}(S)
.

(ii)

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)=N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(P)\mathrm{Inn}(S)
.

(iii)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
.

(iv)

If
P
is abelian, then
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{-1}
.

(v)

If
P
is extraspecial, then
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{0}
.

(vi)

Either
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
or
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
.

In particular, if
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is a member of
\mathcal{G}^{\wedge}_{p}
and
Z(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S)))=1
.

Proof 0.13.5
.

Parts (i), (ii) and (iii) are Lemma
0.5.24
.

Suppose that
P
is abelian. Then Lemma
0.5.19
(iii) implies that
P\cap\gamma_{1}(S)=Z(S)
and
S=P\gamma_{1}(S)
. Let
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
be such that
\tau
restricts to a generator of
N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(P))
. As
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
by (iii), the element
\tau
raises elements of
S/\gamma_{1}(S)\cong P/Z(S)
to the power
r
and elements of
Z(S)
to the power
r^{-1}
for some
r\in\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}
. Hence
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{-1}
in this case. If
P
is extraspecial, then, as
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
,
Z(S)
is centralized by
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
. Thus
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{0}
. As
|\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)|=p-1
by (i), we have shown parts (iv) and (v) hold. Finally (vi) follows from (iv) and (v).

For the rest of this section, we assume that

Hypothesis 0.13.5
.

(i)

p\geq 5
,
S
has maximal class, is not exceptional and has order at least
p^{4}
;

(ii)

\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
;

(iii)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential and is not abelian; and

(iv)

P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
.

To ease notation we set

Q=\gamma_{1}(S),\;G=\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)\text{ and }G_{0}=F^{*}(G).

We also put

V=\Omega_{1}(Z(Q))\text{ and }S_{1}=VP.

As
S
is not exceptional,
V\geq Z_{2}(S)
and so
|V|\geq p^{2}
.
Set

H=\mathrm{Inn}(S_{1})\langle\phi|_{S_{1}}\mid\phi\in N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)}(P)\rangle\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S_{1})

and

B=\{\phi|_{V}\mid\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)\}\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(V).

Proposition
0.5.27
implies that

\mathcal{F}_{0}=\langle\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P),B,H\rangle

is a saturated fusion system on
S_{1}
and, by construction,
V
has index
p
in
S_{1}
which has maximal class. Furthermore
P
is an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-pearl. Set

V_{0}=V\cap\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}).

Lemma 0.13.6
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
(i),(ii) and (iv) hold with
Q
non-abelian but not necessarily
\mathcal{F}
-essential.
Suppose that
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
has order
p-1
and let
s_{i}\in\gamma_{i}(S)\setminus\gamma_{i+1}(S)
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
.
Then either

(i)

P
is abelian,
\tau
acts fixed-point-freely on
V_{0}
and centralizes
V/V_{0}
and for all
j
,

s_{n-j}\gamma_{n-j+1}(S)\tau=s_{n-j}^{r^{-j}}\gamma_{n-j+1}(S)

where
\tau\mu=(r,r^{-1})\in\Delta_{-1}
; or

(ii)

P
is extraspecial,
\tau
acts fixed-point-freely on
V/Z(S)
and centralizes
Z(S)
and, for all
j
,

s_{n-(j+1)}\gamma_{n-j}(S)\tau=s_{n-(j+1)}^{r^{-j}}\gamma_{n-j}(S)

where
\tau\mu=(r,1)\in\Delta_{0}
.

Proof 0.13.7
.

Recall that
|S|=p^{n}
. Assume that
P
is abelian. Then Lemma
0.13.4
(iv) gives
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{-1}
. Hence we take
\tau=(r,r^{-1})
. Then, for
x\in P\setminus Q
we have
xQ\tau=x^{r}Q
, and
s_{n-1}\tau=s_{n-1}^{r^{-1}}
. We may write
s_{1}\tau\gamma_{2}(S)=s_{1}^{b}\gamma_{2}(S)
. Then, Lemma
0.3.10
shows that
r^{-1}=r^{n-2}b
,

s_{n-j}\gamma_{n-j+1}(S)\tau=s_{n-j}^{r^{n-j-1}b}\gamma_{n-j+1}(S)\tau=s_{n-j}^{r^{-j}}\gamma_{n-j+1}(S).

Since
r^{-1}
has order
p-1
, we now have
\tau
acts fixed-point-freely on
V_{0}
which has order
p^{p-2}
and
\tau
centralizes
V/V_{0}=\gamma_{n-(p-1)}(S)/\gamma_{n-(p-2)}(S)
. Since
O^{p^{\prime}}(G)
centralizes
V/V_{0}
,
G
centralizes
V/V_{0}
. Thus (i) holds.

The proof of (ii) follows similarly.

Lemma 0.13.8
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.  Then

(i)

G
is faithful and minimally active on
V
.

(ii)

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(V)\cong G
.

(iii)

Either
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=1
or
P
is extraspecial and
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=Z(S)
.

(iv)

If
P
is abelian, then
\mathrm{Out}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))\cong\mathrm{Aut}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))\mu=\Delta_{-1}
and, if
P
is extraspecial
\mathrm{Out}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))\cong\mathrm{Aut}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))\mu=\Delta_{0}
.

(v)

If
P
is extraspecial and
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=Z(S)
, then

\mathrm{Aut}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}/Z(S))}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0})/Z(S))\mu=\Delta_{-1}.

(vi)

If
P
is abelian, then
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=1
and
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
is reduced.

(vii)

If
P
is extraspecial, then either
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=1
and
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
is reduced or
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=Z(S)
and
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}/Z(S))
is reduced.

Proof 0.13.9
.

Since
V
is centralized by
Q
,
V
is a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module. As
C_{V}(S)=Z(S)
has order
p
,
S/Q
acts with a unique Jordan block on
V
and as
|V|\geq p^{2}
this block is not trivial. Therefore
G/C_{G}(V)
is minimally active on
V
and
C_{G}(V)
is a
p^{\prime}
-group. Assume that
C_{G}(V)\neq 1
. Let
K\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
be of
p^{\prime}
-order satisfy
K\mathrm{Inn}(Q)/\mathrm{Inn}(Q)=C_{G}(V)
. By Lemma
0.13.4
(i),
C_{K\mathrm{Inn}(Q)/\mathrm{Inn}(Q)}(S/Q)=1
. In particular,
[K,N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(Q)}(K)]=K
by coprime action. Using
[
24
, Theorem 5.3.10]
, we obtain that
K
acts faithfully on
\Omega_{1}(Q)
. Since
K
centralizes
V
,
KN_{\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(Q)}(K)
acts faithfully on
\Omega_{1}(Q)/V
which has order at most
p^{p-2}
by Lemma
0.3.2
(vi). However, Proposition
0.2.5
implies that
|\Omega_{1}(Q)/V|\geq p^{p-1}
, which is a contradiction. Hence
C_{G}(V)=1
and
G
acts faithfully on
V
. This proves (i).

To see (ii), just note that the restriction map
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(V)
has kernel
Q
as
G
acts faithfully on
V
. Thus (ii) follows immediately from (i).

Since
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\leq P
, is normal in
S
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P)
-invariant by Lemma
0.5.10
, if
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\neq 1
, then
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=Z(P)=Z(S)
and
P
is extraspecial. This proves (iii).

Because
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(PV)=H
, and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P)=O^{p}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P))\leq O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
by Lemma
0.5.11
, we have
\mathrm{Aut}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})}(PV)\mu=N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))}(P)\mu
and so (iv) follows from Lemma
0.13.4
(iv) and (v).

Assume that
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=Z(S)
. Then
P/Z(S)
is an abelian
\mathcal{F}_{0}/Z(S)
-pearl. The argument which proves Lemma
0.13.4
(iv), also establishes part (v).

As
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{(P)}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))
, Lemma
0.5.20
and the fact that
V
is abelian provide us the hypothesis of Lemma
0.5.13
and this yields
O^{p^{\prime}}(O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))=O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
. Hence
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
is reduced whenever
O_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=1
and otherwise we have
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})/Z(S)
is reduced.

Lemma 0.13.10
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.  If
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
, then
P
is abelian,
V_{0}
is irreducible as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module,
|V_{0}|=p^{p-2}
and
G\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
.

Proof 0.13.11
.

Because of Lemma
0.13.8
(iv), (vi) and (vii), we have
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
is reduced and, as
\gamma_{1}(S_{1})=V
, we may apply Proposition
9
to
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
to obtain the result.

Lemma 0.13.12
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.  If
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})\neq 1
, then
P
is extraspecial,
G\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
,
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=Z(S)=Z(P)
and
V=V_{0}
is abelian with
G
-composition factors
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=Z(S)
which is  centralized by
G
and
V/Z(S)
which is an irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module of order
p^{p-2}
.

Proof 0.13.13
.

By Lemma
0.13.8
(iii) and (vii),
P
is extraspecial and
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}/Z(S))
is reduced. In addition, Lemma
0.13.8
(v) gives
\mathrm{Aut}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}/Z(S))}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}_{0})/Z(S))\mu=\Delta_{-1}
. Now Proposition
9
yields
G\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
, or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
and
V_{0}/Z(S)
has order
p^{p-2}
is irreducible as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module. Thus
|V_{{0}}|=p^{p-1}
and
O^{p^{\prime}}(G)
centralizes
Z(S)
. In particular,
V=V_{0}
. Since
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
also centralizes
Z(S)
, we have
G
centralizes
Z(S)=O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
and this completes the proof of the lemma.

Lemma 0.13.14
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.  Then
V=\Omega_{1}(Q)
has order
p^{p-1}
and
|S|>{p^{p+1}}
. In particular,
\Omega_{1}(Q)<Q
.

Proof 0.13.15
.

We know
|V_{0}|\geq p^{p-2}
by Lemmas
0.13.10
and
0.13.12
. Suppose that
|S|\leq p^{p+1}
. If
|S|=p^{p}
, then
V_{0}
has index
p
in
Q
and as
V_{0}\leq Z(Q)
, we deduce that
Q
is abelian, a contradiction. Suppose that
|S|=p^{p+1}
. If
P
is extraspecial, then Lemma
0.13.12
implies that
V_{0}=V
has order
p^{p-1}
. Thus, in this case,
V
has index
p
in
Q
. Since
V\leq Z(Q)
, we have
Q
is abelian, a contradiction. So suppose that
P
is abelian.
Then
Q/V_{0}
has order
p^{2}
and
Q/V_{0}
is abelian. Since
G
does not embed in
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(p)
, we must have
[Q/V_{0},O^{p^{\prime}}(G)]=1
. But then
S/V_{0}
is abelian, a contradiction. Thus
|S|>p^{p+1}
and so
\Omega_{1}(Q)
has order
p^{p-1}
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iv).
In particular,
V_{0}
has index at most
p
in
\Omega_{1}(Q)
with equality if
P
is extraspecial. So assume
P
is abelian. Then, as
Q
is the
2
-step centralizer in
S
and
V_{0}
is an irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module, we deduce that
V_{0}>[\Omega_{1}(Q),Q]=1
and so
\Omega_{1}(Q)\leq Z(Q)
. Hence
V=\Omega_{1}(Q)
in both cases. This proves the lemma.

There are two possibilities for the structure of
V
as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module dependent upon the type of
P
. If
P
is abelian, then
V
is a non-split extension of a submodule
V_{0}
of order
p^{p-2}
(that is
Z_{p-2}(S)
) by a quotient of order
p
, whereas, if
P
is extraspecial, then there is a submodule of order
p
(that is
Z(S)
) and a quotient of order
p^{p-2}
which is isomorphic to
V_{0}
.
In each case there is a unique proper submodule and a unique non-trivial quotient.

In the next lemma, we set
\Omega_{0}(Q)=1
.
Also, its useful to remember that if
|Q/\Omega_{j-1}(Q)|>p^{p}
for some
j\geq 0
, then
|\Omega_{j}(Q)/\Omega_{j-1}(Q)|=p^{p-1}
by Lemma
0.3.2
(iii) applied to
S/\Omega_{j-1}(S)
.

Lemma 0.13.16
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.  Suppose that
j\geq 1
is maximal such that
\Omega_{j}(Q)\leq Q
and
|\Omega_{j}(Q)/\Omega_{j-1}(Q)|=p^{p-1}
. Then, for
1\leq k\leq j
, as
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-modules,
\Omega_{k}(Q)/\Omega_{k-1}(Q)\cong V
and, either
Q=\Omega_{j}(Q)
or, as
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-modules

Q/\Omega_{j}(Q)\cong\begin{cases}Z(S)&P\text{ extraspecial }\\
V_{0}&P\text{ abelian.}\end{cases}

In particular,
\Omega_{m}(Q)/\Omega_{m-1}(Q)
is centralized by
Q
for all
m
and the (ascending)
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-chief factors in the unique
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-chief series in
Q
alternate between having order
p
and order
p^{p-2}
until it reaches
Q
.

Proof 0.13.17
.

From Lemma
0.13.14
we know that
Q>\Omega_{1}(Q)=V
and
V\leq Z(Q)
. In particular,
\Omega_{2}(Q)>V
. Suppose that
\ell\geq 0
is minimal such that
\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)
is not isomorphic to
V
. By Lemma
0.13.14
,
\ell\geq 1
.

Let
\Omega_{\ell}(Q)<W\leq\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q)
be defined as

W/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)=C_{\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)}(Q).

Notice that
W^{\prime}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)\leq\Omega_{\ell}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
and so
W^{\prime}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
has exponent
p
. Also, as
\Omega_{\ell}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)\cong V
as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-module by the definition of
\ell
,
\Omega_{\ell}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
is centralized by
Q
and so is
W^{\prime}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
.

Consider the map
\theta:W\rightarrow\Omega_{\ell}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
defined by
w\mapsto w^{p}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
. Then, for
x,y\in W
, using
[
24
, Lemma 2.2 (ii)]
together with
[x,y]^{p}\in\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
, we have

(xy)\theta=(xy)^{p}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)=x^{p}y^{p}[x,y]^{\frac{1}{2}p(p-1)}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)=x^{p}y^{p}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)=x\theta y\theta

as
p
is odd.
In addition, for
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
,

(xy)\alpha\theta=(x\alpha)^{p}(y\alpha)^{p}\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)=x^{p}\alpha y^{p}\alpha\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)=((x^{p}y^{p})\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q))\alpha=(xy)\theta\alpha.

Hence
\theta
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-invariant. As
Q
centralizes
\Omega_{\ell}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell-1}(Q)
and
W/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)
, the map
\theta
is a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module homomorphism and
\ker\theta=\Omega_{\ell}(Q)
as
Q
is regular.

Suppose that
\ell+1\leq j
. Then
\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)
has order
p^{p-1}
and, as
Q
is the
2
-step centralizer,
|[\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q),Q]\Omega_{\ell}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)|\leq p^{p-3}
and
|W/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)|\geq p^{2}
. Since
\ker\theta=\Omega_{\ell}(Q)
,
W\theta
has order at least
p^{2}
. If
P
is extraspecial, we know that the unique proper submodule of
V
has order
p
and so, in this case,
\theta
is onto and
W=\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q)
, which is a contradiction. If
P
is abelian, then
W\theta
has order at least
p^{p-2}
and so
|W/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)|\geq p^{p-2}
. Since
W\neq\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q)
, we know that
W/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)
is isomorphic to the unique proper submodule
V_{0}
of
V
. In particular,
W/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)
is an irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module. As
|[\Omega_{\ell+1}(Q),Q]\Omega_{\ell}(Q)/\Omega_{\ell}(Q)|\leq p^{p-3}
, this is a contradiction. We have proved that, for
1\leq k\leq j
, as
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-modules,
\Omega_{k}(Q)/\Omega_{k-1}(Q)\cong V
. In particular, the result is proved if
Q=\Omega_{j}(Q)
.
Hence we assume that
Q>\Omega_{j}(Q)
which means
\ell+1>j
.

As
\ell+1>j
and, as
j\geq\ell
by the maximal choice of
j
, we get
\ell=j
. By the maximal choice of
j
,
\Omega_{j+1}=Q
and
|Q/\Omega_{j}(Q)|<p^{p-1}
. If
P
is abelian, then
W/\Omega_{j}(Q)\cong W\theta\cong V_{0}
which has order
p^{p-2}
and so
W=Q
. If
P
is extraspecial, then, as
|W\theta|<p^{p-1}
, we conclude
W\theta
has order
p
and
W=Q
as
Q
is a
2
-step centralizer. This concludes the proof.

Lemma 0.13.18
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.
Suppose that
R_{2}<R_{1}\leq Q
with
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
both
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-invariant. If
|R_{1}/R_{2}|\leq p^{p-1}
, then
R_{1}/R_{2}
is elementary abelian and centralized by
Q
.

Proof 0.13.19
.

We may assume that
R_{1}/R_{2}
is not a
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-chief factor as this case is clear. Hence Lemma
0.13.16
implies that
|R_{1}/R_{2}|=p^{p-1}
and that there are exactly two
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-chief factors in
R_{1}/R_{2}
one of order
p
and one of order
p^{p-2}
. Since
Q
is a
2
-step centralizer and
S
has maximal class we know that
|R_{1}:[R_{1},Q]|\geq p^{2}
and
|C_{R_{1}/R_{2}}(Q)|\geq p^{2}
. If follows that
Q
centralizes
R_{1}/R_{2}
and, in particular,
R_{1}/R_{2}
is abelian. Since
\mho^{1}(R_{1}/R_{2})
and
\Omega_{1}(R_{1}/R_{2})
are
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-invariant and
\mho^{1}(R_{1}/R_{2})\leq\Omega_{1}(R_{1}/R_{2})
, it follows that
R_{1}/R_{2}
is elementary abelian.

For
\mathrm{Alt}(p)
there is a unique irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)
-module of dimension
p-2
and this is the irreducible heart of the degree
p
permutation module. For
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
, again there is a unique irreducible module of dimension
p-2
and this time it is the module that we denoted by
\mathbf{V}_{p-3}
in Section
0.4
which consists of homogeneous polynomials in
\mathrm{GF}(p)[x,y]
of degree
p-3
. Lemma
0.13.6
now gives us a unique action of
G=G_{0}\langle\tau\mathrm{Inn}(Q)\rangle
on
V
. In the case that
G\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
, this module is in fact the quotient of the
p
-dimensional permutation module by the sum of all vectors in the natural basis. In the case of
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
, it is less natural to define.

Lemma 0.13.20
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.  Suppose that
Q=Q_{1}>Q_{2}>\dots>Q_{\ell}=1
is an
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-chief series in
Q
. Then
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})\neq\mathcal{F}
if and only if
|Q_{1}/Q_{2}|=p
.
Furthermore, if
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})\neq\mathcal{F}
, then
\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})=P\gamma_{2}(S)
and
\gamma_{1}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}))=\gamma_{2}(S)
.

Proof 0.13.21
.

By Lemma
0.5.11
(i)

\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=\langle[g,\alpha]\mid g\in R\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\{S\}\text{ and }\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(R)\rangle.

Lemma
0.13.4
implies that
P\leq\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})
and so
\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=P[Q,\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)]
.

Lemma
0.13.16
says that, as
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-modules, either
Q/Q_{2}\cong V_{0}
or
Q/Q_{2}\cong Z(S)
is centralized by
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
.
If
Q/Q_{2}\cong V_{0}
, then
Q=[Q,\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q))]Q_{2}
and so, as
S
has maximal class,
Q=[Q,\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)]
and
S=\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})
. Hence, using Lemma
0.5.11
(ii) yields
\mathcal{F}=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
. Thus, if
|Q_{1}/Q_{2}|>p
, then
\mathcal{F}=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
.

Conversely, if
|Q/Q_{2}|
has order
p
, then
Q_{2}=\gamma_{2}(S)
and
Q_{2}/Q_{3}\cong V_{0}
by Lemma
0.13.16
. Hence
[Q,\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)]=\gamma_{2}(S)
in this case. This means that
\mathrm{foc}(\mathcal{F})=P\gamma_{2}(S)=\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})
and
\gamma_{1}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}))=\gamma_{2}(S)
. In particular, Lemma
0.5.11
(ii) implies
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
.

Lemma 0.13.22
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.
Then
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof 0.13.23
.

Assume that
\mathcal{F}=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
and let
Q=Q_{1}>Q_{2}>\dots>Q_{\ell}=1
be an
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-chief series in
Q
. Then Lemmas
0.13.16
and
0.13.20
imply that
Q/Q_{2}\cong V_{0}
as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module.

By hypothesis
Q
is non-abelian. We intend to show that this cannot be the case. Choose
N\leq Q
maximal in
Q
such that
N
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-invariant and
Q/N
is non-abelian. Since
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
acts on
Q/N
, it is sufficient to show that
Q/N
cannot admit
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
. Thus we work with the quotient
Q/N
and to make the notation lighter we assume that
N=1
. Set
M=Q^{\prime}
. Then, the maximal choice of
N
implies that
M
is the smallest non-trivial
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-invariant subgroup of
Q
. In particular
M
is elementary abelian,
M\leq Z(Q)
and by Lemma
0.13.16
either
M
has order
p
and is centralized by
G
or
M\cong V_{0}
as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module.
Since
Q
is now a quotient of
\gamma_{1}(S)
, we need to argue that
|Q|\geq p^{p+1}
.
Note that
[Q\colon Z(Q)]\geq[Q\colon Q_{2}]=p^{p-2}
since
Q
is non-abelian. Also,
|Z(Q)|>p
because
Q
is the
2
-step centralizer and so Lemma
0.13.16
implies
|Z(Q)|\geq p^{p-2}
. Hence, as
p\geq 5
,
|Q|\geq p^{2(p-2)}\geq p^{p+1}
.

Set
\overline{Q}=Q/\mho^{1}(Q)
. As
|Q|\geq p^{p+1}
, Lemmas
0.2.1
(iv) and
0.3.2
(iii) yield
|\overline{Q}|=|Q/\mho^{1}(Q)|=|\Omega_{1}(Q)|=p^{p-1}
. Lemma
0.13.18
implies that
\overline{Q}
and
\Omega_{1}(Q)
are elementary abelian.

Define

\kappa:\overline{Q}\times\overline{Q}\rightarrow M

by
(x\mho^{1}(Q),y\mho^{1}(Q))\kappa=[x,y].

Since
M
is elementary abelian and central in
Q
, we have
[x^{p},y]=[x,y]^{p}=1
for all
x,y\in Q
. In particular,
\mho^{1}(Q)\leq Z(Q)
and
\kappa
is a well-defined, surjective,
G
-invariant, alternating, bilinear map. Therefore there is a unique
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module homomorphism from
\Lambda^{2}(\overline{Q})
onto
M
.
Notice that
M
either has order
p
or
p^{p-2}
.
Suppose that
G\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
. In this case,
\overline{Q}/C_{\overline{Q}}(G^{\prime})
is the unique
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Alt}(p)
-module of dimension
p-2
and
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathrm{Alt}(p),\overline{Q}/C_{\overline{Q}}(G^{\prime}))
has dimension
1
by Lemma
0.4.11
. Hence
\overline{Q}/C_{\overline{Q}}(G^{\prime})
is uniquely determined as the submodule of dimension
p-1
of the natural permutation module for
\mathrm{Alt}(p)
on
p
points. However, this means that
\Lambda^{2}(\overline{Q})
is the module described in Lemma
0.4.10
and this has no quotients isomorphic to
M
.

Suppose that
G=\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
. Then
\overline{Q}
is an indecomposable
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p)
-module with socle the trivial
1
-dimensional module and quotient of dimension
p-2
. By Lemma
0.4.8
, there are unique irreducible quotients of
\Lambda^{2}(\overline{Q})
of dimensions
1
and of dimension
p-2
only if the same is true for
\Lambda^{2}(\overline{Q}/C_{\overline{Q}}(G))
. Since
|\overline{Q}/C_{\overline{Q}}(G)|=p^{p-2}
and
p-2
is odd,
\overline{Q}/C_{\overline{Q}}(G)
cannot support an alternating bilinear form and thus
|M|=p^{p-2}
.

By Lemma
0.13.6
,
\tau
acts fixed-point-freely on
M
and on
\overline{Q}/C_{\overline{Q}}(G)
. Since
\mho^{1}(Q)\leq Z(Q)
and
\mho^{1}(Q)
has index
p
in the preimage of
C_{\overline{Q}}(G)
, we get that the preimage of
C_{\overline{Q}}(G)
is abelian. Let
Q^{*}
represent the quotient of
\overline{Q}
by
C_{\overline{Q}}(G)
and we consider the map from
\kappa^{*}:\Lambda^{2}(Q^{*})\rightarrow M
. Let
t_{1},\dots,t_{p-2}
be eigenvectors for the action of
\tau
on
Q^{*}
where
t_{j}\in\gamma_{j}(S)\setminus\gamma_{j+1}(S)
. Since the action of
G
on
M
and on
Q^{*}
is isomorphic to the action of
G
on
V_{0}
, Lemma
0.13.6
implies
t_{i}\tau=t_{i}^{r^{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq p-2
. It follows that, as
r^{p-1}=1
,

[t_{1},t_{p-2}]\tau=[t_{1}^{r},t_{p-2}^{r^{(p-2)}}]=[t_{1},t_{p-2}]^{(rr^{p-2})}=[t_{1},t_{p-2}].

Since
C_{M}(\tau)=1
, we deduce that
[t_{1},t_{p-2}]=1
.
Thus
\kappa^{*}(t_{1}\wedge t_{p-2})=[t_{1},t_{p-2}]=1
and this contradicts Lemma
0.4.9
. This contradiction proves that
Q
is abelian. However,
Q
is not abelian by hypothesis and so we deduce that
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
as claimed.

Lemma 0.13.24
.

Assume that Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds.  Then

(i)

\gamma_{2}(S)
is abelian and is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-invariant;

(ii)

Z(Q)=\mho^{1}(Q)
,
|Q:Z(Q)|=p^{p-1}
and
Q
has nilpotency class
2
;

(iii)

P
is abelian and
[Q,Q]=V_{0}
;

(iv)

|S|=p^{j(p-1)+1}
for some
j\geq 2
and
S
has sectional rank
p-1
; and

(v)

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=P^{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof 0.13.25
.

By Lemmas
0.13.20
and
0.13.22
we have
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
,
\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})=P\gamma_{2}(S)
and
\gamma_{1}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}))=\gamma_{2}(S)
. As
O^{p}(O^{p}(\mathcal{F}))=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
and
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
satisfies Hypothesis
0.13.5
except
\gamma_{1}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}))
being abelian, Lemma
0.13.22
implies that
\gamma_{2}(S)
is abelian. This is (i).

Set
X=\langle r^{p}\mid r\in Q\setminus\gamma_{2}(S)\rangle
. Let
t\in Q\setminus\gamma_{2}(S)
. Then
t^{p}
is centralized by
t
and
\gamma_{2}(S)
. Hence
t^{p}\in Z(Q)
and so
X\leq Z(Q)
is a normal subgroup of
S
and
t\in\Omega_{1}(Q/X)
. Since the preimage of
\Omega_{1}(Q/X)
is normal in
S
and
S
has maximal class we deduce that
Q/X
has exponent
p
. Hence
\mho^{1}(Q)\leq X\leq\mho^{1}(Q)
which means that
X=\mho^{1}(Q)
. It follows that
Z(Q)\geq\mho^{1}(Q)
. Since
|Q/\mho^{1}(Q)|=|\Omega_{1}(Q)|=p^{p-1}
by Lemmas
0.2.1
(iii) and
0.13.14
we can use Lemma
0.13.16
to conclude
Z(Q)=\mho^{1}(Q)
as surely
Z(Q)\neq\gamma_{2}(S)
.
Also,
Q/\mho^{1}(Q)
is elementary abelian by Lemma
0.13.18
. Hence
Q^{\prime}\leq Z(Q)
and
Q
has nilpotency class
2
. This proves (ii).

Let
x,y\in Q
, then, by (ii),
x^{p}\in Z(Q)
and so
1=[x^{p},y]=[x,y]^{p}
. Hence
Q^{\prime}\leq\Omega_{1}(Q)=V
.
Now the commutator map
\kappa:Q/X\times Q/X\rightarrow V
again determines a
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-module homomorphisms
\kappa^{*}:\Lambda^{2}(Q/X)\rightarrow V
. Now
Q/X
has
\gamma_{2}(S)/X\cong V_{0}
as its unique submodule and we know that
\Lambda^{2}(\gamma_{2}(S)/X)
is in the kernel of
\kappa^{*}
. We have
\Lambda^{2}(Q/X)/\Lambda^{2}(\gamma_{2}(S)/X)\cong\gamma_{2}(X)\cong V_{0}
as
\mathrm{GF}(p)G
-modules. Hence the image of
\kappa^{*}
in
V
is isomorphic to
V_{0}
. If
P
is extraspecial, then
Z(S)
is the unique proper subgroup of
V
which is
G
-invariant. Hence
P
is abelian and
Q^{\prime}=V_{0}
. Thus (iii) holds.

As for part (iv), if
Q=Q_{1}>Q_{2}>\dots>Q_{\ell-1}>Q_{\ell}=1
is an
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
-chief series in
Q
, then, by part (i),
Q_{2}=\gamma_{2}(S)
and, by part (iii),
Q_{\ell-1}=Q^{\prime}
has order
p^{p-2}
. Hence Lemma
0.13.16
implies that
|Q|=p^{j(p-1)}
for some
j\geq 1
. Thus Lemma
0.13.14
yields
|S|=p^{j(p-1)+1}
for some
j\geq 2
.   In particular
|S|\geq p^{2p}
and
[
28
, Theorem A]
implies that
S
has sectional rank
p-1
.

Note that
P\gamma_{2}(S)
is
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
-invariant and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
centralizes
\gamma_{1}(S)/\gamma_{2}(S)
. Hence
P\gamma_{2}(S)
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
are the only
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
-invariant maximal subgroups of
S
. Therefore every
\mathcal{F}
-pearl is contained in
P\gamma_{2}(S)
and from this it follows that
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\{P^{x}\mid x\in S\}=P^{\mathcal{F}}
. Hence (v) holds.

0.14.
The proofs of Theorems A and C

In this short section we prove Theorems
A
and
C
as well as Corollary
0.1.3
. We begin with Theorem
C
.

Theorem C
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime,
S
is a maximal class
p
-group of order at least
p^{4}
and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
. Assume that
S
is not exceptional,
\gamma_{1}(S)
is not abelian and
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Then one of the following holds:

(i)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
,
|S:\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|\leq p
with
|S:\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|=p
if and only if
|S|=p^{j(p-1)+1}
for some
j\geq 2
. Furthermore,   either
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
is simple and exotic or
p=3
and
O^{3}(\mathcal{F})
is realized by
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(q)
for suitable prime powers
q
.

(ii)

p\geq 5
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
,
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=Z(S)
,
|S:\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|\leq p
with
|S:\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|=p
if and only if
|S|=p^{j(p-1)+2}
for some
j\geq 2
. Furthermore,
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}/Z(S))
is simple and exotic.

(iii)

p\geq 5
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
,
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
,
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
and

(a)

\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
is a single
\mathcal{F}
-class,
|S|=p^{j(p-1)+1}
for some
j\geq 2
and
S
has sectional rank
p-1
;

(b)

\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
;

(c)

Z(\gamma_{1}(S))=\mho^{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has index
p^{p-1}
in
\gamma_{1}(S)
,
\gamma_{1}(S)^{\prime}<\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has order
p^{p-2}
and
\gamma_{2}(S)
is abelian but not elementary abelian;

(d)

every composition factor of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
on
\gamma_{1}(S)
has order
p
or
p^{p-2}
and the composition factors of order
p
are centralized by the automorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
;

(e)

for
P\in\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
,
\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})=P\gamma_{2}(S)
,
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
is a saturated fusion system on
P\gamma_{2}(S)
, and
\mathrm{Aut}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F})}(\gamma_{2}(S))\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
.

Furthermore, in all cases
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is a Hall
p^{\prime}
-subgroup of
\mathrm{Out}(S)
and is cyclic of order
p-1
and, if
|S|=p^{n}
, and
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
, then either
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=P^{S}
or
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
and
n\equiv\epsilon\pmod{p-1}

where
\epsilon=0
if
P\in\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
and
\epsilon=1
if
P\in\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof 0.14.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
with
|S|\geq p^{4}
. In addition, suppose
S
has maximal nilpotency class, is not exceptional and has
Q=\gamma_{1}(S)
non-abelian. Assume that
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(Q)
. By Theorem
D
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{Q\}
and so
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is non-empty.

Assume that
p=3
. Then the saturated fusion systems are presented in Appendix
Q
. Since we require
\gamma_{1}(S)
to be non-abelian, the discussion after Theorem
Q.1
says we only need to inspect Table
4
for
S=\mathrm{B}(2\ell;1,0,2),\mathrm{B}(2\ell,1,0,0)
,
\ell\geq 3
and
\mathrm{B}(2k+1,1,0,0)
with
k\geq 2
. This shows that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
,
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
, and
|S:\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|=3
if and only if
|S|=3^{2k+1}
. Furthermore, either
S
is one of
\mathrm{B}(2\ell;1,0,2)
or
\mathrm{B}(2\ell,1,0,0)
with
\ell\geq 3
and
\mathcal{F}
is simple and exotic or
S=\mathrm{B}(2k+1,1,0,0)
and
O^{3}(\mathcal{F})
is realised by
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(q)
for suitable
q
. Hence (i) holds when there are no saturated fusion systems
\mathcal{F}
with extraspecial
\mathcal{F}
-pearls or with
\gamma_{1}(S)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Assume from now on that
p\geq 5
.  By Lemma
0.13.4
(vi), either
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
or
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
. Furthermore,   if
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{Q\}
, then Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds and this contradicts Lemma
0.13.24
(iii).  Thus
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\neq\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{Q\}
. By Lemma
0.13.4
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
is a Hall
p^{\prime}
-subgroup of
\mathrm{Out}(S)
and is cyclic of order
p-1
. This, together with
[
28
, Theorem 3.15]
establishes the chaser to the theorem.

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
. Then Lemma
0.5.25
gives (i).

If
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
, then
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=Z(S)
. Since
P\leq\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})
is normal in
S
,
|S:\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|\leq p
. Furthermore,
|S:\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|=p
if and only if
s_{1}\gamma_{2}(S)
is centralized by
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
which is if and only if
n-2\equiv 0\pmod{p-1}
by Lemma
0.13.6
(ii). Hence
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{F}
if and only if
|S|=p^{j(p-1)+2}
with
j\geq 1
.
In addition,
P/Z(S)
is an abelian
\mathcal{F}/Z(S)
-pearl. In the case that
Q/Z(S)
is non-abelian,
\mathcal{F}/Z(S)
satisfies (i) with
|S/Z(S)|=p^{n-1}\geq p^{4}
. So, in particular,
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}/Z(S))
is simple and exotic and
j\geq 2
when
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})\neq\mathcal{F}
.
On the other hand, if
Q/Z(S)
is abelian, then applying
[
42
, Theorem 2.8 (a)(i) and a(iv)]
delivers
O^{p}(\mathcal{F}/Z(S))
is simple and exotic. Suppose that
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{F}
and
j=1
. Then
|S|=p^{p+1}
. We have
Q^{\prime}=Z(S)
. In addition,
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
has extraspecial pearls and
|\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F})|=p^{p}
and so
[
28
, Theorem A]
applied
O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
yields
\gamma_{1}(\mathrm{hyp}(\mathcal{F}))=\gamma_{2}(S)
is elementary abelian.
Let
\tau\in\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
have order
p-1
. Then
\tau
centralizes
s_{1}\gamma_{2}(S)
and
Z(S)
.
Choose
k
maximal so that
[s_{1},s_{k}]\neq 1
. Then
[s_{1},s_{k}]\in Z(S)
and

[s_{1},s_{k}]=[s_{1},s_{k}]\tau=[s_{1}\tau,s_{k}\tau].

Since
1<k<n-1=p
and
\tau
has order
p-1
,
s_{k}\tau=s_{k}^{b}g_{k+1}
for some
b\in\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}\setminus\{1\}
and
g_{k+1}\in\gamma_{k+1}(S)
by Lemma
0.3.14
. We also have
s_{1}\tau=s_{1}g_{2}
for some
g_{2}\in\gamma_{2}(S)
.
Since
\gamma_{2}(S)
is abelian, the maximal choice of
k
gives

[s_{1},s_{k}]=[s_{1}\tau,s_{k}\tau]=[s_{1}g_{2},s_{k}^{b}g_{k+1}]=[s_{1},s_{k}]^{b},

a contradiction.

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{Q\}
. Then Hypothesis
0.13.5
holds. That
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
is just Lemma
0.13.22
. Now part (iii)(a) is Lemma
0.13.24
(iv) and (v), part (iii)(b) is Lemma
0.13.10
, part (iii)(c) is Lemma
0.13.24
(i), (ii), (iii) and (iv), part (iii)(d) is Lemma
0.13.16
, finally part (iii)(e) follows from Lemmas
0.13.20
and
0.13.24
(i) as clearly
\mathrm{Aut}_{O^{p}(\mathcal{F})}(\gamma_{2}(S))\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(p)
by (iii)(b).

Theorem A
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a reduced saturated fusion system on a
p
-group
S
of maximal class   of order at least
p^{4}
.  Then one of the following statements holds.

(i)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is non-abelian,
S
is not exceptional,
\mathcal{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
, and
\mathcal{F}
is simple and exotic.

(ii)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is non-abelian,
S
is exceptional and either

(a)

p\geq 5
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(p))
;

(b)

p=5
,
S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
5
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(5)
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
where
G
is one of the sporadic simple groups
\operatorname{Ly},\operatorname{HN}
or
\mathrm{B}
;

(c)

p=7
,
S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
7
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(7)
and either
\mathcal{F}
is exotic (20 examples) or
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\operatorname{M})
where
\operatorname{M}
denotes the monster; or

(d)

p\geq 11
,
S
is uniquely determined of order
p^{p-1}
,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\not=\emptyset
and, if
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, then
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\cong\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}\times\mathrm{GF}(p)^{\times}
,
O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{2}(S)))\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
and
\gamma_{1}(S)/Z(\gamma_{1}(S))
is the
(p-3)
-dimensional irreducible
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
-module.

(iii)

\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian and
\mathcal{F}
is described by Theorem
P.0
.

Proof 0.14.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is reduced (
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
and
\mathcal{F}=O^{p}(\mathcal{F})=O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})
). In addition we may assume that
\gamma_{1}(S)
is non-abelian. If
S
is not exceptional, then, as
\mathcal{F}
is reduced, Theorem
C
(ii) and (iii) cannot hold as in the first case
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})\neq 1
and in the second case
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
. Hence Theorem
C
(ii) holds and in particular
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
consists of abelian
\mathcal{F}
-pearls. This is point (i) of the theorem.

Suppose that
S
is exceptional. Then Theorem
B
applies. In particular, this immediately gives parts (ii)(a) and (ii)(d) of the theorem, completing the proof for
p\geq 11
. For
p=5
and
\mathcal{F}\neq\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(5))
, Theorem
B
(i)(a)(
\alpha
), (i)(a)(
\beta
) and (ii) cannot hold, as
O_{5}(\mathcal{F})\neq 1
in these cases. Hence Theorem
B
(i)(a)(
\delta
) holds. We deduce that
\mathcal{F}
is not realized by
\mathrm{Aut}(\operatorname{HN})
and this gives point (ii)(b) of the theorem.

Finally, when
p=7
and
S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
7
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(7)
, then we use
[
47
, Table 5.1]
to determine how many of the examples are reduced. There are 20 exotic examples all appearing as subsystems of
\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{M})
. This is point (ii)(c) of the theorem and completes the proof.

Corollary
0.1.3
.

Let
p
be a prime,
S
be a
p
-group of maximal class and let
\mathcal{F}
be a saturated fusion system on
S
with
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
. If
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is empty, then
S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(p)
and either

(i)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(p))
;

(ii)

p=5
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
where
G=\operatorname{Ly},\operatorname{HN},\mathrm{Aut}(\operatorname{HN})
or
\mathrm{B}
;

(iii)

p=7
,
\mathcal{F}
is exotic and the
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups are
C_{S}(Z_{2}(S))
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
, with
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(C_{S}(Z_{2}(S)))\cong\mathrm{GL}_{2}(7)
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong 3\times 2^{.}\mathrm{Sym}(7)
, and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\cong\mathrm{GF}(7)^{\times}\times\mathrm{GF}(7)^{\times}
.

Proof 0.14.3

(Proof of Corollary
0.1.3
.)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
has no
\mathcal{F}
-pearls and
O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
. Theorem
C
implies that   either
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian  or
S
is exceptional.   If
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian, then
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is non-empty by
[
42
, Lemma 2.3]
. So assume that
S
is exceptional.
Then examining Theorem
B
we see that part (i)(a) must hold. In particular,
S
is isomorphic to a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{G}_{2}(p)
. Using
[
47
, Theorem 1.1 and Table 5.1]
yields the result.

0.15.
A series of examples with non-abelian
2
-step centralizer

Let
p
be an odd prime, then by Dirichlet’s Theorem
[
22
]
there exists a prime
r
such that
r\equiv 1\pmod{p^{k}}
. Let
T
be a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{GF}(r^{p})^{\times}
and
M
be the monomial subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{p}(r^{p})
which has all matrix entries in
T
. Then
T
is a cyclic group and
|M|=|T|^{p}p!
. Notice that
Z(M)\cong T
and consists of scalar matrices. We denote by
D
the subgroup of diagonal matrices of
M
. Let
R\in\mathrm{Syl}_{p}(M)
. We claim
R/Z(M)
has maximal class. Let
\pi
be the permutation matrix corresponding to the permutation
(1,2,\dots,p)
:

\pi=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0&\dots&0\\
0&0&1&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\dots&1\\
1&0&0&\dots&0\end{smallmatrix}\right).

We may assume that
\pi\in R
. Then a typical element of
R
has the form

\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{p})\pi^{j}\in D\langle\pi\rangle

where
d_{i}\in T
and
1\leq j\leq p
. Now a calculation shows that the set of matrices in
R
which centralize
\pi
mod
Z(M)
is

C=\{\mathrm{diag}(d,de,de^{2},\dots,de^{p-1})\pi^{j}\mid d,e\in T,e^{p}=1,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}\leq\mathrm{GL}_{p}(r).

Because
(C\cap D)/Z(M)=\{\mathrm{diag}(1,e,\dots,e^{p-1})Z(M)\mid e\in T,e^{p}=1\}
has order
p
, Lemma
0.3.4
yields
R/Z(M)
has maximal class.   Since
\pi\in\mathrm{SL}_{p}(r)
, and

\det(\mathrm{diag}(1,e,\dots,e^{p-1}))=e^{p(p-1)/2}=1

we see the image of
C
in
\mathrm{PGL}_{p}(r)
is contained in
\mathrm{PSL}_{p}(r)
.  Using
[
2
, Section 4]
we record the well-known fact:

Lemma 0.15.1
.

If
p
divides
r-1
, then the Sylow
p
-subgroups of
G=\mathrm{PGL}_{p}(r)
and
G=\mathrm{PSL}_{p}(r)
have maximal class. Furthermore, in both cases,  we have
\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\widetilde{C})\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
where
\widetilde{C}
is the image of
C
in
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(r)
. ∎

Since
p^{k}
is the highest power of
p
which divides
r-1
,
p^{k+1}
exactly divides
r^{p}-1
(see
[
39
, Lemmas 4.1(iv) and 4.2(ii)]
). Let
\sigma:\mathrm{GF}(r^{p})\rightarrow\mathrm{GF}(r^{p})
be the field automorphism given by
x\mapsto x^{r}
and extend
\sigma
to the standard Frobenius automorphism of
\mathrm{GL}_{p}(r^{p})
which acts as
\sigma
on each matrix entry. We denote this automorphism by
\sigma
as well. Define
M^{*}
to be the semidirect product of
M
and
\langle\sigma\rangle
, identify
M
and
\langle\sigma\rangle
with their images in
M^{*}
and let
R^{*}=R\langle\sigma\rangle
. Consider the subgroup
C^{*}
of
R^{*}
which centralizes
\pi
mod
Z(M)
. Since
\pi\in\mathrm{GL}_{p}(r)
, we see

C^{*}=C\langle\sigma\rangle=\{\mathrm{diag}(d,de,de^{2},\dots,de^{p-1})\pi^{j}\sigma^{k}\mid d,e\in T,e^{p}=1,j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}

and so
|C^{*}/Z(M)|=p^{3}
.
Let
R>R_{0}\geq Z(M)
be such that
R_{0}/Z(M)
is a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}_{p}(r^{p})
and put
D_{0}=D\cap R_{0}
. Then
R^{*}>R>R_{0}
and
R^{*}/R_{0}=\langle R_{0}\sigma,R_{0}\mathrm{diag}(d,1,\dots,1)\rangle
which has order
p|T|
. Now let
R_{1}=R_{0}\langle\mathrm{diag}(d,1,\dots,1)\sigma\rangle
. Then
\pi\in R_{1}
,
C^{*}\cap R_{1}=C
and so
R_{1}/Z(M)
has maximal class by Lemma
0.3.4
and

\gamma_{1}(R_{1}/Z(M))=\langle D_{0}/Z(M),Z(M)\mathrm{diag}(d,1,\dots,1)\sigma\rangle

is not abelian as
\sigma
does not centralize
D_{0}/Z(M)
.

Example 0.15.2
.

Assume that
p>3
is a prime.  Put

X_{1}=\mathrm{SL}_{p}(r^{p})
,
X=X_{1}R_{1}
and
\overline{X}=X/Z(X)
. Set
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\overline{R}_{1}}(\overline{X})
. Then

(i)

\overline{R_{1}}
has maximal class and
\gamma_{1}(\overline{R_{1}})
is non-abelian;

(ii)

\overline{C}
and
\gamma_{1}(\overline{R_{1}})
are
\mathcal{F}
-essential with
\overline{C}
an abelian
\mathcal{F}
-pearl;

(iii)

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{C})\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(p)
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(\overline{R_{1}}))\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
; and

(iv)

O_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
.

In particular, there exist realizable saturated fusion systems which satisfy Theorem
C
(iv) with
\gamma_{1}(S)
non-abelian and
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{Sym}(p)
.
By
[
50
, Theorem 6.2]
, the subfusion systems generated by
N_{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{R_{1}})}(\overline{C})
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{C})
is also saturated and this gives an example of Theorem
C
(i).

P.
Saturated fusion systems on maximal class
p
-groups with
\gamma_{1}(S)
abelian

In this appendix we curate a list of the reduced fusion systems on maximal class
p
-groups which have
\gamma_{1}(S)
abelian. Thus we present a synopsis of the main results from
[
42
,
19
,
45
]
. For this, we first establish some further notation from
[
42
,
19
,
45
]
which is honed to our special situation. First there are the sets of
\mathcal{F}
-essential subgroups in the sets
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{B}
which are introduced in
[
42
]
.
We continue to use the notation introduced in Subsection
0.3.1
so that

S/\gamma_{2}(S)=\langle x\gamma_{2}(S),s_{1}\gamma_{2}(S)\rangle.
We also recall the definition of the set
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls from Definition
0.1.1
.
If
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is non-empty, then we may additionally assume that
x
has order
p
. For
0\leq i\leq p-1
, define

H_{i}=\langle xs_{1}^{i}\rangle Z(S)\text{ and }B_{i}=\langle xs_{1}^{i}\rangle Z_{2}(S).

Then
\mathcal{H}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{p-1}H_{i}^{S}
and
\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{p-1}B_{i}^{S}.

In our notation we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{H\in\mathcal{H}\mid H\text{ is }\mathcal{F}\text{-essential}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{B\in\mathcal{B}\mid B\text{ is }\mathcal{F}\text{-essential}\}.

We also let
\mathcal{P}^{i}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
consist of those
\mathcal{F}
-pearls which are
S
-conjugate to
H_{i}
and
\mathcal{P}^{i}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
contain the
\mathcal{F}
-pearls
S
-conjugate to
B_{i}
. Finally, for
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\{a,e\}
define

\mathcal{P}^{I}_{b}(\mathcal{F})=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{P}_{b}^{i}(\mathcal{F})\text{ and }\mathcal{P}_{b}^{*}(\mathcal{F})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{p-1}\mathcal{P}_{b}^{i}(\mathcal{F}).

Set
G=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
. Then, we define

\mu_{1}:N_{G}(\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S)))\rightarrow\Delta

by

\alpha\mu_{1}=\beta\mu

where
\beta
in
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
,
\beta|_{\gamma_{1}(S)}=\alpha
and
\mu
is as defined at the beginning of Section
0.13
. In
[
19
, page 218]
it is explained why
\mu_{1}
(denoted
\mu_{A}
) is well-defined. If
X
is a finite cyclic group and
n
divides
|X|
, then
\frac{1}{n}X
denotes the unique subgroup of
X
which has index
n
. Almost all the other notation that we require can be found in the introduction to Section
0.13
one exception being the cyclic subgroups of
\Delta
defined as

\Delta_{k/\ell}=\{(u^{\ell},u^{k})\mid u\in\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}^{\times}\}

whenever
k
and
\ell
are coprime.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a reduced saturated fusion system on
S
where
S
has maximal class,
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian and
|S|=p^{n}>p^{3}
. Since we are primarily focussed on the cases when
\gamma_{1}(S)
is
\mathcal{F}
-essential, we can sift through the results in
[
19
,
45
]
.

By Lemma
0.3.2
(iv) we know that either
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\leq p^{p-1}
or
|S|=p^{p+1}
and
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p^{p}
. Furthermore, Lemma
0.3.4
implies that
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
acts on
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
with a single Jordan block.
By

[
19
, Proposition 3.7]
, if
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
has order at most
p^{p}
, then
\mathrm{Aut}_{S}(\gamma_{1}(S))
operates on
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
with a single Jordan block and so from the results in
[
19
,
45
]
we just need to collate the ones with
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\leq p^{p}
. If
\gamma_{1}(S)>\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, then
|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|\neq p^{p}
and so
[
45
, Theorem A]
implies

|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p^{p-1}
. In particular, if
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is irreducible, then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is homocyclic. In Table
1
we present conditions on the structure of
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
which determine the various possibilities for constellations of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls in a reduced fusion system. This table is transcribed from
[
19
, Theorem 2.8, Table 2.1]
.

Table 1.
Configurations of pearls determined by

where

and

.

Theorem P.0
.

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime,
S
has maximal class of order at least
p^{4}
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian. If
\mathcal{F}
is a reduced saturated fusion system on
S
, then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}\cup\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
is non-empty. Furthermore, one of the following holds:

(i)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})
.

(ii)

|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|<p^{p-1}
,
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
, the candidates for
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and the configurations of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls are listed in the first section of Table
2
.

(iii)

|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p^{p}
,
\gamma_{1}(S)=\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
,
|S|=p^{p+1}
and the possibilities for
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and the configurations of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls are listed in the second section of Table
2
.

(iv)

|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p^{p-1}
,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is irreducible as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-module,
\gamma_{1}(S)
is homocyclic of order
p^{a(p-1)}
for some
a\geq 1
and the possibilities for
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and the configurations of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls are listed in the third section of Table
2
.

(v)

|\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))|=p^{p-1}
,
\Omega_{1}(\gamma_{1}(S))
is indecomposable but not irreducible as a
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
-module,
\gamma_{1}(S)
not necessarily homocyclic and the possibilities for
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and the configurations of
\mathcal{F}
-pearls are listed in the fourth section of Table
2
.

Proof P.1
.

This mostly follows from our previous discussion and by combining the results of
[
19
, Corollary 2.10 and Theorem 4.1]
with
[
45
, Theorem A]
. However, lines 29, 30 and 33 and 34 in Table
2
need further explanation to confirm column 6, where the possibilities for
\mathcal{F}
-pearls are described. So suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is reduced. For lines 29 and 33, we note that if IV from Table
1
holds, then
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})
and
Z(S)=O_{p}(\mathcal{F})
which is impossible. The possibility that III holds when considering lines 30 and 34 leads to
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)\mu=\Delta_{-1}
. Using Lemma
0.3.10
we obtain
[\gamma_{1}(S),\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)]\leq\gamma_{2}(S)
and so, as
[\gamma_{1}(S),O^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))]=\gamma_{2}(S)
, we have
[\gamma_{1}(S),\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))]=\gamma_{2}(S)
. Now applying
[
19
, Lemma 2.7 (b)]
shows that
\mathcal{F}\neq O^{p}(\mathcal{F})
, which contradicts
\mathcal{F}
being reduced.

Q.
The saturated fusion systems on maximal class
3
-groups

In this appendix, we bring together the outcome of various investigations into saturated fusion systems on maximal class
3
-groups. These results are mainly extracted from
[
20
,
48
,
54
]
.

There are two maximal class
3
-groups of order
3^{3}
, they are both extraspecial one of exponent
3
and one of exponent
9
.

Theorem Q.0
.

Suppose that
S
is extraspecial of order
3^{3}
and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
. Then either
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
or
S
has exponent
3
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})
and one of the following holds.

(i)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(3^{2}{:}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(3))
or
\mathcal{F}_{S}(3^{2}{:}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(3))
;

(ii)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(3))
or
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(3){:}2)
;

(iii)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}({}^{2}\mathrm{F}_{4}(2)^{\prime})
; or

(iv)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(\mathrm{J}_{4})
.

Proof Q.1
.

The extraspecial group of order
3^{3}
and exponent
9
is meatacyclic. Hence, in this case,
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
by
[
56
, Proposition 5.4]

(see also
[
16
, Theorem 7.5]
). If
S
is extraspecial of exponent 3, we read the result from
[
54
, Theorem 1.1]
.

The classification of maximal class
3
-groups is due to Blackburn
[
8
]
. We take their presentations from
[
20
, Theorem A.2]
. For
n\geq 4
, and
\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\{0,1,2\}
, define

\mathrm{B}(n;\beta,\gamma,\delta)=\langle x,s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1}\mid\textbf{R1},\textbf{R2},\textbf{R3},\textbf{R4},\textbf{R5},\textbf{R6}\rangle

where, understanding that
s_{n}=s_{n+1}=1
, the relations are as follows:

R1
:

s_{i}=[s_{i-1},x]
for
i\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
;

R2
:

[s_{1},s_{i}]=1
for
i\in\{3,\dots,n-1\}
;

R3
:

s_{i}^{3}s_{i+1}^{3}s_{i+2}=1
for
i\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
;

R4
:

[s_{1},s_{2}]=s_{n-1}^{\beta}
;

R5
:

s_{1}^{3}s_{2}^{3}s_{3}=s_{n-1}^{\gamma}
; and

R6
:

x^{3}=s_{n-1}^{\delta}
.

Theorem Q.1

(Blackburn)
.

Suppose that
S
is a maximal class
3
-group of order
3^{n}
with
n\geq 4
. Then
S\cong\mathrm{B}(n;\beta,\gamma,\delta)
for some
\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\{0,1,2\}
. Furthermore,
S
is metabelian and, unless
S\cong\mathrm{B}(4;0,1,0)
,
S
has rank
2
.

Proof Q.2
.

This comes from the text before and after
[
8
, Theorems 4.2 and 4.3]
.

There are isomorphisms between some of the Blackburn group. Using the discussion after
[
8
, Theorem 4.3]
,
the full list of groups of order
3^{4}
is given as
\mathrm{B}(4;0,\gamma,\delta)
where

(0,\gamma,\delta)\in\Sigma_{4}=\{(0,1,0),(0,2,0),(0,0,0),(0,0,1)\}.

Since these groups have order
3^{4}
, they each have an abelian subgroup of index
3
. The group
\mathrm{B}(4;0,1,0)
is the unique maximal class
3
-group of rank
3
. Thus
\mathrm{B}(4;0,1,0)
is isomorphic to a Sylow
3
-subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(9)
. For
n\geq 5
, the groups with no abelian maximal subgroups are given by

(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\in\Theta=\{(1,0,0),(1,0,1),(1,0,2)\}.

With this we can write down the a full irredundant list of maximal class
3
-groups of order at least
3^{5}
:

(i)

for
n
odd,

(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\in\Theta\cup\{(0,1,0),(0,0,1),(0,0,0)\}.

(ii)

for
n
even,

(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\in\Theta\cup\Sigma_{4}.

So, for
n\geq 5
, there are six maximal class
3
-groups when
n
is odd, and seven when
n
is even.

Lemma Q.3
.

Assume that
S=\mathrm{B}(4;0,1,0)
is a Sylow
3
-subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(9)
and
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
with
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Set
A=\langle x,s_{3}\rangle
and
E=\langle s_{2},A\rangle
. Then
\gamma_{1}(S)=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle
and
\mathcal{F}
is described as follows:

(i)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
,
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))\cong\mathrm{Frob}(39)
,
\mathrm{Frob}(39)\times 2
,
\mathrm{Alt}(4)
,
2\times\mathrm{Alt}(4)
,
\mathrm{Sym}(4)
two different actions, or
2\times\mathrm{Sym}(4)
;

(ii)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})=\{E\}
,
\mathcal{F}=N_{\mathcal{F}}(E)
and either
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(E)\cong{3^{2}}{:}\mathrm{SL}_{3}(3)
or
3^{2}{:}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(3)
;

(iii)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{E,\gamma_{1}(S)\}=\mathcal{P}_{e}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
with
G\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(3)
or
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(3){:}2
;

(iv)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=A^{\mathcal{F}}\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
with
G\cong\mathrm{Alt}(9)
or
\mathrm{Sym}(9)
;
or

(v)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{F})=A^{\mathcal{F}}
,
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{F}}(A)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{3}(3)
or
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(3)
, and
O^{3^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})
is simple and exotic.

In particular,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
.

Proof Q.4
.

The examples have been enumerated by computer using the procedures from
[
50
]
. The code is in Subsection
R.5
. This confirms that there are 15 examples.

Theorem Q.4
.

Suppose that
S=\mathrm{B}(n;\beta,\gamma,\delta)
is a maximal class
3
-group of rank
2
and order
3^{n}
with
n\geq 4
. Assume
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on
S
with
\mathcal{F}\neq N_{\mathcal{F}}(S)
. Then

S\cong\begin{cases}\mathrm{B}(2k;0,0,0),\mathrm{B}(2k;0,2,0)&k\geq 2\cr\mathrm{B}(2\ell;0,1,0),\mathrm{B}(2\ell;1,0,0),\mathrm{B}(2\ell;1,0,2)&\ell\geq 3\cr\mathrm{B}(2k+1;0,0,0),\mathrm{B}(2k+1;1,0,0)&k\geq 2\end{cases}

and
\mathcal{F}
is as described in Table
4
.
Furthermore, for each of the fusion systems tabulated,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
and, if
\gamma_{1}(S)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
, then
S\cong\mathrm{B}(2k+1;0,0,0)
with
k\geq 2
and
\gamma_{1}(S)
is homocyclic of rank
2
.

*

*

*

*

\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(q^{3}_{1}).3

Table 4.
The saturated fusion systems on maximal class
3
-groups of rank
2
and order at least
3^{4}
.

In Table
4
,
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
and
q_{4}
are prime powers with
\nu_{3}(q_{1}-1)=\nu_{3}(q_{4}^{2}-1)=k
and
\nu_{3}(q_{2}-1)=\nu_{3}(q_{3}^{2}-1)=k-1
where
\nu_{3}(m)
denotes that exponent of the highest power of
3
which divides
m
. Of course,
q_{4}
is an odd power of
2
.

To understand the data presented in Table
4
, let
S=\mathrm{B}(n;\beta,\gamma,\delta)
with
n\geq 4
and assume that
S
has rank
2
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a saturated fusion system on
S
.
It is easy to see that
Z(S)=\langle s_{n-1}\rangle
,
Z_{2}(S)=\langle s_{n-2},s_{n-1}\rangle
and
\gamma_{1}(S)=\langle s_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n-1\rangle
. For
i\in\{0,1,-1\}
define

A_{i}=\langle xs_{1}^{i},s_{n-1}\rangle,

and

E_{i}=\langle A_{i},s_{n-2}\rangle.

When the subgroups
A_{i}
,
E_{i}
have exponent
3
, then up to
S
-conjugacy they are the candidates to be
\mathcal{F}
-pearls. If there is a
*
in Table
4
, this indicates that for the given
S
, the corresponding subgroups
A_{i}
and
E_{i}
do not have exponent
3
(see
[
48
, Table 1]
). We do not make this indication for
\gamma_{1}(S)
as it does not have exponent
3
. The notation
A_{1}\sim_{\mathcal{F}}A_{-1}
,
E_{1}\sim_{\mathcal{F}}E_{-1}
means that these subgroups are
\mathcal{F}
-conjugate. We only show this in the case when
A_{1}
or
E_{1}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-pearl. Reading across a row of Table
4
, first comes the Blackburn name of
S
, then
|\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(S)|
. As we move a long the row, an entry displays the structure of
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(A_{i})
,
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(E_{i})
or
\mathrm{Out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{1}(S))
in the case that the corresponding subgroup is
\mathcal{F}
-essential. A group or
N_{\mathcal{F}}(E_{0})
in the final column indicates that the fusion system is realizable, all the other tabulated fusion systems are exotic. Where the exotic fusion system is in
[
20
, Theorem 5.10]
, this is indicated by a subscript DRV and we have adhered to their names.

The fusion systems
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DRV}}(3^{2k+1},5)
are exotic and incorrectly labeled in
[
20
, Table 6]
(hence the strange numbering of the system). The fusion systems on
S=\mathrm{B}(2k+1;1,0,0)
included as the final row of Table
4
are claimed to be exotic in
[
48
]
. In fact they can be constructed by the methods in Example
0.15.2
.

Theorem Q.5
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a saturated fusion system on a non-abelian maximal class
3
-group. Then
\mathcal{F}
is known,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})\cup\{\gamma_{1}(S)\}
and, if
\gamma_{1}(S)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}}
, then
\gamma_{1}(S)
is abelian.

Proof Q.6
.

Combine Theorems
Q.0
and
Q.4
and Lemma
Q.3
.

R.
Computer code

In this appendix we provide the
Magma
code that we have used for the work. We have used the functionality developed in
[
50
]
. To use these subroutines, the intrinsics provide in
[
49
]
must be available. For this you will be required to attach the package file following the instructions provided here
https://magma.maths.usyd.edu.au/magma/handbook/text/24#168.

R.1.
The
Magma
code for Example
0.1.2

This code requires the fusion system package written by Parker and Semeraro
[
50
,
49
]
.

X:= ASL(2,5); S5:= Sylow(X,5);
BA:= Normalizer(X,S5);

Y:= PSp(4,5); S5:= Sylow(Y,5);
B:= Normalizer(Y,Centre(S5));
B1:= DerivedSubgroup(B);
BE:=Normalizer(B1,S5);

// BA is isomorphic to the normalizer of
//a Sylow 5-subgroup of 5^2:\SL_2(5) and BE
//is isomorphic to the normalizer of a
//Sylow 5-subgroup of 5^{1+2}:\SL_2(5)

AP:=[];
EP:=[];

SS:= SmallGroups(5^7,IsMaximalClass);
#SS;

SSA:=[];
for x in SS do
ΨL:= LowerCentralSeries(x);
ΨG1:=Centralizer(x,L[2],L[4]);
Ψif not IsAbelian(G1) then Append(~SSA,x);
Ψend if;
end for;
#SSA;

for x in SSA do MakeAutos(x); end for;
SSAA:= [x : x in SSA |#Sylow(x‘autoperm,2) eq 4];

#SSAA;

for X in SSAA do
ΨL:= LowerCentralSeries(X);
ΨG1:=Centralizer(X,L[2],L[4]);
ΨAP[Index(SSAA,X)] :=[];
ΨEP[Index(SSAA,X)] :=[];
ΨSAP:= Subgroups(X:OrderEqual:= 25);
ΨSAP:= {x‘subgroup: x in SAP| not x‘subgroup subset G1};
ΨSEP:= Subgroups(X:OrderEqual:= 125);
ΨSEP:= {x‘subgroup: x in SEP|not IsAbelian(x‘subgroup)
ΨΨand not x‘subgroup subset G1 and Exponent(x‘subgroup) eq 5};
ΨH:= Sylow(X‘autoperm,2);
ΨH:= SubInvMap(X‘autopermmap,X‘autogrp,H);
ΨAFS:= sub<X‘autogrp|Inn(X),H>;
ΨSAP:={x: x in SAP|IsOdd(#AutOrbit(X,x,AFS))};
ΨSEP:={x: x in SEP|IsOdd(#AutOrbit(X,x,AFS))};
Ψ
Ψfor x in SAP do
ΨΨNSx:= Normalizer(X,x);
ΨΨA, B, C:= AutOrbit(X,x,AFS);
ΨΨMakeAutos(NSx);Ψ
ΨΨW:= Sylow(SubMap(X‘autopermmap,X‘autoperm,B),2);
ΨΨK:= SubInvMap(X‘autopermmap,X‘autogrp,W);
ΨΨK:= sub<NSx‘autogrp|K>;
ΨΨB := Holomorph(NSx,K);
ΨΨa:= IsIsomorphic(B,BA);
ΨΨif a then Append(~AP[Index(SSAA,X)] ,x); end if;
Ψend for;

Ψfor x in SEP do
ΨΨNSx:= Normalizer(X,x);
ΨΨA, B, C:= AutOrbit(X,x,AFS);
ΨΨMakeAutos(NSx);Ψ
ΨΨW:= Sylow(SubMap(X‘autopermmap,X‘autoperm,B),2);
ΨΨK:= SubInvMap(X‘autopermmap,X‘autogrp,W);
ΨΨK:= sub<NSx‘autogrp|K>;
ΨΨB := Holomorph(NSx,K);
ΨΨa:= IsIsomorphic(B,BE);
ΨΨif a then Append(~EP[Index(SSAA,X)] ,x); end if;
Ψend for;
end for;

IA:={i:i in [1..#SSAA]|#AP[i] ne 0};
IE:={i:i in [1..#SSAA]|#EP[i] ne 0};
print "there are", #{x:x in AP|#x ne 0},
"maximal class groups of order 5^7
with abelian pearls.";

print "there are", #{x:x in EP|#x ne 0},
"maximal class groups of order 5^7
with extraspecial pearls.";

JJ:={1297,1308,1321,1360,1363,1374,1384};
for i in IA do
Ψfor j in JJ do
ΨΨif IsIsomorphic(SmallGroup(5^7,j),SSAA[i]) then
ΨΨΨi,j;end if;
Ψend for;
end for;

for i in IE do
Ψfor j in JJ do
ΨΨif IsIsomorphic(SmallGroup(5^7,j),SSAA[i]) then
ΨΨΨi,j; end if;
Ψend for;
end for;
JJ:={1297,1308,1321,1360,1363,1374,1384};
A:=[];
for j in JJ do
A[j]:= AllFusionSystems(SmallGroup(5^7,j):
OpTriv:=false, pPerfect:=false);
end for;
for j in JJ do
Ψif IsDefined(A,j) then A[j]; end if;
end for;

for j in JJ do
ΨS:= SmallGroup(5^7,j);
ΨL:= LowerCentralSeries(S);
ΨG1:=Centralizer(S,L[2],L[4]);
Ψj, NilpotencyClass(G1);
end for;

R.2.
The
Magma
code required to verify Lemma
0.4.10

This code verifies that when
p\in\{5,7\}
, the exterior square of the module
S^{p-1,1}
for
\mathrm{GF}(p)\mathrm{Sym}(p)
has two composition factors and no quotient factor which is
1
-dimensional or isomorphic to
D^{p-1,1}
.

for p in {5,7} do
G:= Sym(p);
V:= PermutationModule(G,GF(p));
W:= sub<V|V.1-V.1*G.1>;
DPminus11:= W/Socle(W);
U:= ExteriorSquare(W);
C:=CompositionFactors(U);
C;
IsIrreducible(U/Socle(U));
IsIsomorphic(U/Socle(U),DPminus11);
IsIsomorphic(Socle(U),DPminus11);
end for;

R.3.
The
Magma
code for Lemma
0.7.5

This code requires the fusion system package written by Parker and Semeraro
[
50
,
49
]
.

SS:= SmallGroups(5^6,IsMaximalClass);
#SS;
TT:=[];
for S in SS do
ΨL:= LowerCentralSeries(S);
Ψgamma1:= Centralizer(S,L[2],L[4]);
ΨCSZ2:= Centralizer(S,L[4]);
Ψif gamma1 ne CSZ2 then Append(~TT,S);
Ψend if;
end for;
#TT;

TT2:=[];
for S in TT do
ΨL:= LowerCentralSeries(S);
Ψgamma1:= Centralizer(S,L[2],L[4]);
ΨCSZ2:= Centralizer(S,L[4]);
ΨMakeAutos(CSZ2);
Ψif not IsExtraSpecial(gamma1) and
not IsSoluble(CSZ2‘autoperm) then
ΨΨAppend(~TT2,S);
Ψend if;
end for;
#TT2;

TT3:=[];
for S in TT2 do MakeAutos(S);
Ψif not IsNilpotent(S‘autoperm) then Append(~TT3,S);
Ψend if;
end for;

#TT3;
S:= TT3[1];
A:=AllFusionSystems(S:OpTriv:= false,pPerfect:= false);
A;

R.4.
The group providing an example for Lemma
0.8.5

This code constructs a group
G
of shape
7^{3+3}{:}\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(7)
which has Sylow
7
-subgroup
S
of maximal class and
\gamma_{1}(S)
special with centre of order
7^{3}
. It further shows that
S
cannot support a saturated fusion system
\mathcal{G}
containing
\mathcal{G}
-pearls.

G:= FreeGroup(5);
R<r1,r2,r3,s,t>:= FreeGroup(5);
R:= quo<R|r1^7,r2^7,r3^7,((r1,r2),r1),((r1,r2),r2),
((r1,r2),r3),((r1,r3),r1),((r1,r3),r2),((r1,r3),r3),
((r2,r3),r1),((r2,r3),r2),((r2,r3),r3),
s^3, t^2, (s,t)^4,(t*s)^8,
r1^s = r1^5*r2^2*(r1,r2)^2*(r1,r3)*(r2,r3)^3,
r2^s =r1*r2*r3*(r1,r2)^5*(r1,r3)^4*(r2,r3)^2,
r3^s = r1^4*r2^2*r3*(r1,r3)^3*(r2,r3)^6,
r1^t = r2*r3*(r1,r2)^3*(r1,r3)^2*(r2,r3),
r2^t =r1^5*r2^3*r3^2*(r1,r2)^4*(r1,r3)^3*(r2,r3)^5,
r3^t =r1^3*r2^4*r3^5*(r1,r2)*(r1,r3)^5*(r2,r3)^4>;
G:= CosetImage(R,sub<R|s,t>);
ChiefFactors(G);
S:=Sylow(G,7);
IsMaximalClass(S);

//We now check if G can be decorated with pearls

p:= FactoredOrder(S)[1][1];
L:= LowerCentralSeries(S);
gamma1:= Centralizer(S,L[2],L[4]);
Z2:= L[#L-2];
R:= Centralizer(S,Z2);

M:= MaximalSubgroups(S);
PotAbelianPearls:=[];
PotExtraspecialPearls:=[];
for x in M do y:= x‘subgroup;
Ψif y ne gamma1 and y ne R then
ΨΨif exists(xx){z:z in y|
not z in gamma1 and Order(z) eq p and not z in R}
ΨΨthen
Append(~PotExtraspecialPearls,sub<S|Z2,xx>);
Append(~PotAbelianPearls,sub<S|L[#L-1],xx>);
ΨΨend if;
Ψend if;
end for;

J1:= Centralizer(PSp(4,p),Centre(Sylow(PSp(4,p),7)));
BE:= Normalizer(J1,Sylow(J1,p));
BA:= Normalizer(ASL(2,p),Sylow(ASL(2,p),p));

for x in PotExtraspecialPearls do
Ψ if IsIsomorphic(Normalizer(G,x),BE) then
Ψ Ψ"S can be decorated with extraspecial pearls";
Ψ Ψelse "this potential extraspecial pearl,
is not a pearl."; end if;
end for;

for x in PotAbelianPearls do
Ψ if IsIsomorphic(Normalizer(G,x),BA) then
Ψ Ψ"S can be decorated with abelian pearls";
Ψ Ψelse "this potential abelian pearl,
is not a pearl."; end if;
end for;

R.5.
The
Magma
code for Lemma
Q.3

This code requires the fusion system package written by Parker and Semeraro
[
50
,
49
]
.

S:= Sylow(Sym(9),3);
A:= AllFusionSystems(S:OpTriv:= false,pPerfect:= false);
#A;
